Decay estimates for beam equations with potentials in dimension two
Shuangshuang Chen, Han Cheng, Zijun Wan and Xiaohua Yao †

Abstract
. This paper establishes time decay estimates for the following two-dimensional beam (plate) equation with a decaying real-valued potential V V : ∂ t 2 u + ( Δ 2 + V ) ​ u = 0 , u ⁡ ( 0 , x ) = f ⁡ ( x ) , ∂ t u ⁡ ( 0 , x ) = g ⁡ ( x ) . \partial_{t}^{2}u+(\Delta^{2}+V)u=0,\qquad u(0,x)=f(x),\quad\partial_{t}u(0,x)=g(x). When zero is a regular point or a first-kind resonance of H = Δ 2 + V H=\Delta^{2}+V , we first prove sharp L 1 → L ∞ L^{1}\to L^{\infty} estimates for the solution operators: ‖ cos ⁡ ( t ​ H ) ​ P ac ​ ( H ) ‖ L 1 → L ∞ + ‖ sin ⁡ ( t ​ H ) t ​ H ​ P ac ​ ( H ) ‖ L 1 → L ∞ ≲ 1 | t | , \displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|}, and obtain an enhanced decay ( | t | ​ log ⁡ | t | ) − 1 (|t|\log|t|)^{-1} in logarithmically weighted spaces L ω 1 → L − ω ∞ L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega} with ω ⁡ ( x ) = log ⁡ ( 2 + | x | ) \omega(x)=\log(2+|x|) . This reveals that the potential V V effectively shifts the intrinsic zero resonance of the bi-Laplacian Δ 2 \Delta^{2} , endowing the two perturbed cosine and sine evolutions with a logarithmic gain ( log ⁡ | t | ) − 1 (\log|t|)^{-1} unattainable in the free case. For second-kind resonances of H H (the bi-Laplacian Δ 2 \Delta^{2} belongs to this class), a non-zero trace moment ⟨ | x | 2 ​ V , ϕ ⟩ ≠ 0 \langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle\neq 0 for some second-kind resonance function ϕ \phi induces severe threshold singularities, worsening the L 1 → L ∞ L^{1}\to L^{\infty} estimate to | t | − 1 ​ ( log ⁡ | t | ) 2 |t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{2} . When all the second-order moment tensor G i ​ j = ⟨ x i ​ x j ​ V , ϕ ⟩ G_{ij}=\langle x_{i}x_{j}V,\phi\rangle vanish for every second-kind resonance function ϕ \phi , we not only recover the unweighted | t | − 1 |t|^{-1} bound but also achieve the weighted bound ( | t | ​ log ⁡ | t | ) − 1 (|t|\log|t|)^{-1} for the cosine, perfectly matching the free dynamics of Δ 2 \Delta^{2} . Finally, for third-kind resonances or a zero eigenvalue , we prove that the presence of d d -wave resonance leads to the worst L 1 → L ∞ L^{1}\to L^{\infty} decay rate ∼ ( log ⁡ | t | ) − 1 \sim(\log|t|)^{-1} . Several improved estimates are also obtained without a d d -wave resonance. In particular, in the pure eigenvalue case (i.e., neither d d -wave nor p p -wave resonance), both propagators recover the optimal unweighted L 1 → L ∞ L^{1}\to L^{\infty} estimate | t | − 1 . |t|^{-1}. Moreover, they achieve the best weighted bound ( | t | ​ log ⁡ | t | ) − 1 (|t|\log|t|)^{-1} if and only if the fourth-order moment tensor T i ​ j ​ k ​ l = ⟨ x i ​ x j ​ x k ​ x l ​ V , ϕ ⟩ T_{ijkl}=\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}V,\phi\rangle is completely isotropic for every zero eigenfunction ϕ . \phi. These results provide a complete picture of the time-decay behavior for the two-dimensional plate equation with a potential, covering all possible threshold singularities at zero energy.



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Introduction

In this paper, we investigate the time decay estimates of the following two-dimensional beam (or plate) equation with a real-valued potential
V(x)
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\Delta^{2}u+V(x)u=0,\ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
u(0,x)=f(x),\quad\partial_{t}u(0,x)=g(x).&\end{cases}

(1.1)

The beam (or plate) equation is a fundamental model originating from classical elasticity theory, dating back to Euler and Bernoulli, and describes the transverse vibrations of thin elastic structures. In unperturbed scenarios, the dynamics are governed by the biharmonic operator
\Delta^{2}
, which reflects the intrinsic bending stiffness of the material. In realistic contexts, spatially decaying potentials
V(x)
are typically introduced to model structural heterogeneities, localized defects or material impurities.

Let
V(x)
satisfy
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
. By the Kato-Rellich theorem,
H=\Delta^{2}+V
is a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with domain
H^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(Sobolev space). The solution to the beam equation (
1.1
) can be explicitly expressed as

u(t,x)=\cos(t\sqrt{H})f(x)+\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}g(x).

Since the negative eigenvalues of
H
can lead to exponentially growing modes, we will restrict our attention to the evolution projected onto the absolutely continuous spectrum. That is, we focus on the continuous part of the solution:

u_{c}(t,x)=\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)f(x)+\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)g(x),

where
P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum space of
H
. Under the assumption that
H
has no embedded positive eigenvalues (see Subsection
1.4.1
below for a sufficient condition), there exist several interesting works about the decay estimates of the continuous part
u_{c}(t,x)
.
In dimension
n=3
, Chen et al.
[
9
]
proved that the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
decay rates for the solution operators are
{O}(|t|^{-3/2})
when zero is a regular point or a first-kind resonance, while the decay rate changes when other types of zero-energy resonances occur. In dimension
n=1
, Chen-Wan-Yao
[
10
]
established an
O(|t|^{-1/2})
decay rate, which remains unchanged in the presence of resonances. For higher dimensions (
n\geq 5
), the decay rate
O(|t|^{-n/2})
in the regular case can be deduced using wave operator methods developed by Erdoğan and Green
[
18
,
19
]
.

It is worth noting that decay estimates in even dimensions, particularly
n=2
, are more challenging. This difficulty arises from logarithmic singularities and delicate threshold obstructions at zero energy. For the Schrödinger operator
-\Delta+V
in two dimensions, Schlag
[
44
]
first established
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimates when zero is regular. Later, Erdoğan and Green
[
17
]
systematically treated zero-energy resonances and obtained improved decay estimates in log-weighted spaces for the regular case
[
16
]
.
For the beam equation with potentials, we need to consider the fourth-order Schrödinger operator
H=\Delta^{2}+V
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. However, the bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
exhibits high-order degeneracy at zero energy. This degeneracy, combined with the inherent logarithmic singularities in two dimensions, makes the dispersive analysis of
\Delta^{2}+V
a challenging task (see Theorem
2.7
).

In the free case of
V=0
, this simplifies to
\sqrt{H}=-\Delta
, and
it is known that the following optimal decay estimates in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
hold (see Proposition
3.1
):

\displaystyle\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim{|t|}^{-1}.

(1.2)

More interestingly, in the weighted space setting
L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\omega(x)=\log(2+|x|)
, the decay rate of cosine evolution operator can be improved:

\displaystyle\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim({|t|\log|t|})^{-1},

(1.3)

which is strictly unattainable for both
e^{it\Delta}
and
\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)
in the weighted space. This enhanced phenomenon (
1.3
) exactly happens in these dimensions
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
(see Remark
3.2
).

For a decaying potential
V
, understanding how
\cos(t\sqrt{H})
and
\sin(t\sqrt{H})/(t\sqrt{H})
decay over time requires focusing on delicate behavior at low frequencies. This low-frequency dynamics becomes significant due to the creation of new zero resonances of the operator
H
, which occur when
V
strongly couples with
\Delta^{2}
in low-energy regimes. In fact, as shown in Definition
1.1
,
H=\Delta^{2}+V
admits four distinct zero-energy structures (
the three types of zero resonances plus a zero eigenvalue
), each leading to somehow different decay behaviors.

When zero is either a regular point or a first-kind resonance of
H
, we not only establish the following sharp
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimate for the both perturbed operators (see Theorem
1.2
):

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim{|t|}^{-1},

but also obtain the following improved decay estimates:

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L_{\omega}^{1}\to L_{-\omega}^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\rightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim({|t|\log|t|})^{-1}.

(1.4)

It is worth noting that the free sine evolution
\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)
only satisfies a bound
\sim|t|^{-1}
in logarithmically weighted spaces
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
. Hence this reveals that the potential
V
effectively modified the intrinsic zero resonance of
\Delta^{2}
, so that the perturbed sine evolution
\sin(t\sqrt{H})/t\sqrt{H}
gains an additional logarithmic factor
(\log|t|)^{-1}
in the regular or the first kind resonance case that is distinct from the free case.

When zero is a second-kind resonance of
H
(the bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
belongs to this class), the existence of a non-zero trace moment
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle\neq 0
for some second-kind resonance function
\phi
induces severe zero singularities. Consequently, the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimate becomes
|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{2}
, incurring a logarithmic loss compared to the free evolution (see Theorem
1.4
). In particular, when all second-order moment component
\langle x_{i}x_{j}V,\phi\rangle=0
for every second-kind resonance function
\phi
, we can not only recover the unweighted
|t|^{-1}
bound but also achieve the weighted bound
(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
for the cosine, which perfectly matches the free dynamics of
\Delta^{2}
as shown in (
1.2
) and (
1.3
).

This optimality reveals a clear mathematical rationale: since zero is intrinsically a second-kind resonance of
\Delta^{2}
, the cancellations of all potential-induced threshold obstructions enable the perturbed operator to naturally match the free evolution by vanishing second-order moments.

Finally, in the case of third-kind resonances or a zero eigenvalue, we prove that the presence of a
d
-wave resonance leads to the following optimal
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
decay rate
(\log|t|)^{-1}
(see Theorem
1.7
):

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim({\log|t|})^{-1}.

Moreover, we also obtain several improved estimates in the absence of a
d
-wave resonance. In particular, in the pure
L^{2}
eigenvalue case (i.e., neither
d
-wave nor
p
-wave resonance), we not only recover the unweighted
|t|^{-1}
bound but also
show that the cosine and sine propagators gain the enhanced bound
(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
as in (
1.4
) if and only if the fourth-order moment tensor
T_{ijkl}=\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}V,\phi\rangle
is
completely isotropic
, i.e.,
T_{ijkl}=\lambda(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})
for some constant
\lambda
.

We remark that these results above provide a complete picture of the time-decay behavior for the two-dimensional plate equation with a potential, covering all possible threshold singularities at zero and exhibiting optimal or nearly optimal rates up to logarithmic corrections.

1.2.
Notations

In this subsection, we collect some notations used throughout the paper.

•

We write
f(\lambda)={O}_{k}(g(\lambda))
if

\frac{d^{\ell}}{d\lambda^{\ell}}f(\lambda)=O\left(\frac{d^{\ell}}{d\lambda^{\ell}}g(\lambda)\right),\quad\ell=0,1,\cdots,k.

•

The notation
\mathcal{O}(f(t))
refers to an operator satisfying
\|\mathcal{O}(f(t))\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\rightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim|f(t)|.

•

Let
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\chi_{1}(\lambda)=1
for
|\lambda|<\lambda_{0}\ll 1
and
\chi_{1}(\lambda)=0
if
|\lambda|>2\lambda_{0}
, where
\lambda_{0}
is some sufficiently small positive constant depending on low energy expansion of
\left(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)^{-1}
in Theorem
2.7
. Define
\chi_{2}(\lambda):=1-\chi_{1}(\lambda)
and
\widetilde{\chi}_{j}(\lambda):=\chi_{j}(\lambda^{4})
for
j=1,2.

•

\langle f,g\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\,dx
denotes the inner product in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

•

Let
\langle\cdot\rangle=1+|\cdot|
.
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the weighted
L^{2}
spaces:

L^{2,s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\{f\in L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\mid\langle\cdot\rangle^{s}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\}.

•

P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)
denote the projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum space of
H=\Delta^{2}+V
, where
V(x)
is a real-valued potential satisfying
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
.

1.3.
Main results

In the following, we first give the precise definition of zero resonance types of
H=\Delta^{2}+V(x)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, let
W_{-\sigma}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
denote the intersection space

W_{-\sigma}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)=\bigcap_{s>\sigma}L^{2,-s}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right),

which is increasing in
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies
L^{2,-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset W_{-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In particular,
W_{0}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)\supset L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V(x)
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
. We say that

(i)

Zero is a
first kind resonance
of
H
if the equation
H\phi=0
has a nonzero solution
\phi\in W_{-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\big(\phi-\sum_{|\alpha|=1}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\big)\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, but only has a zero solution in
W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

(ii)

Zero is a
second kind resonance
of
H
if
the equation
H\phi=0
has a nonzero solution in
W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, but only has a zero solution
\phi
in
W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

(iii)

Zero is a
third kind resonance
of
H
if the equation
H\phi=0
has a nonzero solution in
W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, but there exists only a zero solution
\phi
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

(iv)

Zero is an
eigenvalue
of
H
if there exists a nonzero
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
H\phi=0
.

(v)

Zero is a
regular point
of
H
if
H
has neither zero eigenvalues nor zero resonances.

For convenience, we use
\mathbf{k}\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
to denote zero energy types:
\mathbf{k}=0
(regular),
\mathbf{k}=1,2,3
(resonances),
\mathbf{k}=4
(eigenvalue).
Moreover, an equivalent characterization of zero resonances for
H
will be provided in Proposition
2.5
and Proposition
2.6
in Section
2.2
.

We now state our main results, which are organized into the following four theorems based on the nature of the zero-energy resonances. To begin, we define the logarithmic weight function
\omega(x)=\log(2+|x|)
and the associated weighted spaces

L^{p}_{\pm\omega}:=\bigl\{f\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigm|\omega(x)^{\pm 1}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr\},\qquad p=1,\infty.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-11-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is
a regular point or a first-kind resonance
of
H
.
Then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

(1.5)

Moreover, in the weighted space setting
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
, we can obtain the improved decay estimate:

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|},\quad|t|\geq 2.

(1.6)

Remark 1.3
.

We provide several comments regarding the decay estimates (
1.5
) and (
1.6
):

•

For the unweighted estimate (
1.5
), the
|t|^{-1}
decay rate exactly matches the intrinsic dispersive rate of the free operator in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see Proposition
3.1
):

\displaystyle\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

(1.7)

Consequently, we expect the bound (
1.5
) to be sharp in
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
. Physically, since a short-range potential
V(x)
is highly localized in space, it is not expected to alter the global asymptotic dispersion of wave packets in the unweighted setting.

•

For the weighted estimate (
1.6
), it is noteworthy that the perturbed sine evolution exhibits an extra logarithmic decay factor of
(\log|t|)^{-1}
compared to its free counterpart in
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
(see Proposition
3.1
):

\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1},\quad|t|\geq 2.

This logarithmic gain can be understood via their asymptotic expansions. The free sine evolution contains a leading-order term
{1}/{(8|t|)}G_{0}
that dictates its exact
|t|^{-1}
decay:

\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}=\frac{1}{8|t|}G_{0}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad G_{0}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f\,dy.

However, when zero is a regular point or a first-kind resonance of
H
,
the presence of the potential
V
effectively cancels this leading-order term
{1}/{(8|t|)}G_{0}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)=\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}-\frac{1}{8|t|}G_{0}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}\right).

Based on this spectral mechanism, we believe the weighted estimate (
1.6
) is strictly sharp.

To consider the case of
the second-kind zero resonance
, let us introduce the space of resonance solutions in
W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\big\{\phi\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ \big|\ H\phi=0\big\}.

(1.8)

When zero is a second-kind resonance of
H
, Definition
1.1
implies that
\mathcal{E}\neq\{0\}
and
\mathcal{E}\cap W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{0\}
.
To classify the dispersive behavior based on the second-order potential moments on
\mathcal{E}
, we define the fully moment-vanishing subspace:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}=\Big\{\phi\in\mathcal{E}\ \Big|\ \langle x_{i}x_{j}V,\phi\rangle=0\text{ for all }i,j=1,2\Big\},\ \text{with}\ x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(1.9)

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-14-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is a second-kind resonance of
H
. We distinguish two cases:

(i)

If there exists some
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
such that
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log(2+|t|))^{2}}{|t|}.

(1.10)

In particular, if the additional condition
\mathcal{E}_{0}\neq\{0\}
holds, then

\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-{\omega}}}\sim\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|},\quad|t|\geq 2.

(ii)

Conversely, if
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle=0
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
, then the unweighted estimate (
1.10
) remains valid and the following improved weighted estimate holds:

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

Furthermore, assume that each
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
satisfies
\langle x_{i}x_{j}V,\phi\rangle=0
for all
i,j=1,2
, then the estimates further improve to match the optimal bounds for the free case
\Delta^{2}
, namely,

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1},\quad|t|\geq 2.

(1.11)

Remark 1.5
.

It is instructive to compare the dispersive estimates in Theorem
1.4
with the free case (
V=0
), where zero is a second-kind resonance of
\Delta^{2}
with
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathbb{C}
. This comparison reveals several interesting threshold phenomena:

•

In the generic case where
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle\neq 0
for some
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
, the unweighted
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
decay rate is
(\log|t|)^{2}/|t|
, which is slower than the free decay rate
|t|^{-1}
. Moreover, under the additional condition
\mathcal{E}_{0}\neq\{0\}
, the sine propagator satisfies the sharp weighted estimate
(\log|t|)^{2}/|t|
; by the continuous embeddings
L^{1}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow L^{1}
and
L^{\infty}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
, this sharpness transfers to the unweighted bound, namely:

\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|},\quad|t|\geq 2.

Conversely, when the orthogonality condition
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle=0
holds for all
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
, the threshold singularities are partially mitigated. This leads to improved weighted bounds
|t|^{-1}
for both propagators, while the unweighted estimates still incur the logarithmic loss
(\log|t|)^{2}/|t|
.

•

The most striking feature occurs in the subcase
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{0}
(which strengthens the condition to
\langle x_{i}x_{j}V,\phi\rangle=0
for all
i,j
and
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
). Here, additional orthogonality allows the perturbed operators to exactly recover the optimal unweighted free bounds (
1.7
). Moreover, Theorem
1.4
precisely captures the free case (
1.3
): when
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{0}
, the weighted cosine propagator gains an extra logarithmic improvement
(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
, while the sine propagator remains saturated at
|t|^{-1}
.

Remark 1.6
.

The spatial moment and orthogonality conditions on the resonance space
\mathcal{E}
in Theorem
1.4
correspond precisely to the algebraic non-vanishing of certain spectral projections
S_{j}
defined in Definition
2.2
. Specifically, by Proposition
8.1
, when
V\neq 0
, the assumption of a second-kind resonance is equivalent to
S_{3}L^{2}\neq\{0\}
and
S_{4}L^{2}=\{0\}
. Consequently:

•

The existence of
\phi\in\mathcal{E}
such that
\langle|x|^{2}V,\phi\rangle\neq 0
is equivalent to
S_{3}L^{2}\neq S_{4}^{0}L^{2}
, meaning that
Q_{4}^{0}=S_{3}-S_{4}^{0}\neq 0
.

•

The condition
\mathcal{E}_{0}\neq\{0\}
is equivalent to
S_{4}^{1}L^{2}\neq\{0\}
, which implies that
Q_{42}^{1}=S_{4}^{1}-S_{4}\neq 0
.

Finally, it remains to analyze the scenario where zero is
a third-kind resonance or an eigenvalue
of
H
. By Definition
1.1
, this is equivalent to

\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\big\{\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ \big|\ H\phi=0\big\}\neq\{0\},

which means there exists at least one nonzero solution
\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
H\phi=0.

Below we split our results into Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
by the spatial decay of the zero-energy states of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
:

•

Presence of a
d
-wave resonance:

\mathcal{E}_{1}
is not fully contained in
L^{2}
, meaning there exists a nonzero solution
\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
H\phi=0
.

•

Absence of a
d
-wave resonance:
Zero is an eigenvalue of
H
but exhibits no
d
-wave resonance (i.e., every solution
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
strictly belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
).

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-18-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is either a third-kind resonance or an eigenvalue accompanied by a d-wave resonance. Then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim\frac{1}{\log|t|},\quad|t|\gg 1.

To state the results for the case where zero is an eigenvalue but exhibits no
d
-wave resonance, we denote the
L^{2}
zero-energy eigenspace by

\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\bigl\{\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\mid H\phi=0\bigr\}.

To further characterize the precise algebraic cancellations, we introduce the following subspace:

\mathcal{E}_{3}:=\left\{\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &\langle x_{1}^{3}x_{2}V,\phi\rangle=\langle x_{1}x_{2}^{3}V,\phi\rangle=0,\\
&\langle x_{1}^{4}V,\phi\rangle=\langle x_{2}^{4}V,\phi\rangle=3\langle x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}V,\phi\rangle\end{aligned}\right\}.

This subspace consists of those
\phi
for which the fourth-order moment tensor
T_{ijkl}=\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}V,\phi\rangle
is
completely isotropic
, i.e.,
T_{ijkl}=\lambda(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})
for some constant
\lambda
.

Indeed, a direct calculation yields
T_{1111}=T_{2222}=3\lambda
,
T_{1122}=\lambda
, and all off-diagonal terms such as
T_{1112}
and
T_{1222}
vanish. These relations correspond exactly to the algebraic conditions
\langle x_{1}^{4}V,\phi\rangle=\langle x_{2}^{4}V,\phi\rangle=3\langle x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}V,\phi\rangle
and
\langle x_{1}^{3}x_{2}V,\phi\rangle=\langle x_{1}x_{2}^{3}V,\phi\rangle=0
.

Recalling the definitions of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
(see (
1.8
) and (
1.9
)), together with Definitions
1.1
and
2.2
, and Propositions
2.5
and
8.1
, we obtain the inclusion relations

\mathcal{E}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-22-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is an eigenvalue of
H
but exhibits no d-wave resonance (i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{2}
).

(1)

If
H
admits a p-wave resonance (i.e., there exists a nonzero
\phi\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\setminus W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
H\phi=0
), then the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimate (
1.10
) holds.

(2)

If
H
lacks a p-wave resonance, then the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimate (
1.5
) holds.
Moreover, we can further distinguish the following two subcases:

•

If
\mathcal{E}_{2}\neq\mathcal{E}_{3}
, then we obtain the same weighted estimate (
1.11
) as in the free case.

•

If
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{3},
then the enhanced weighted estimate (
1.6
) holds as in the regular case.

Remark 1.9
.

We provide some further comments for Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
:

•

Theorem
1.7
shows that a
d
-wave resonance causes a serious low-energy obstruction. It slows down the decay rate to a logarithmic
(\log|t|)^{-1}
, which acts as a universal bottleneck for both cosine and sine evolutions, regardless of any eigenvalue contributions.

•

Without
d
-wave and
p
-wave resonances, threshold eigenvalues do not affect the unweighted decay, which remains optimal at
|t|^{-1}
. In weighted spaces, if
\mathcal{E}_{2}\neq\mathcal{E}_{3}
,
the lack of full isotropy in the fourth-order moments of
V\phi
prevents the exact cancellation of leading singular operators in the resolvent expansion. The residual singularity restricts the sine evolution to
|t|^{-1}
, which reflects the second-kind resonance obstruction.
If
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{3},

the perfect isotropy guarantees the cancellation of the leading threshold singularities and both propagators achieve the enhanced rate
(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
, matching the same weighted bounds as for regular or first-kind threshold.

1.4.
Further background

1.4.1.
Embedded positive eigenvalue

According to Kato’s theorem
[
35
]
, the Schrödinger operator
-\Delta+V
has no positive eigenvalues if
V
is bounded and
V=o(|x|^{-1})
as
|x|\to\infty
(see also
[
24
,
32
,
36
,
45
]
). This criterion, however, fails for the fourth-order operator
H=\Delta^{2}+V
. For any
n\geq 1
, one can construct
V\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
H
possesses positive eigenvalues (see Feng et al.
[
22
, Section 7.1]
). These results show that the absence of positive eigenvalues for fourth-order Schrödinger operators is more subtle and less stable than in the second-order case under potential perturbations.
Nevertheless, if
V\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is repulsive, i.e.,
(x\cdot\nabla)V\leq 0
, then
H
has no eigenvalues (see
[
22
, Theorem 1.11]
). This criterion also applies to general higher-order elliptic operators
P(D)+V
in any dimension
n\geq 1
.

1.4.2.
Wave equations

For the classical wave equation with a real-valued decaying potential,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}^{2}u+(-\Delta+V)u=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
u(0,x)=f(x),\quad\partial_{t}u(0,x)=g(x),\end{array}\right.

decay estimates have been extensively studied. When
V=0
, the solution operators
\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta})
and
\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{-\Delta})}{\sqrt{-\Delta}}
satisfy
W^{k+1,1}\to L^{\infty}
and
W^{k,1}\to L^{\infty}
estimates with
O(|t|^{-1/2})
in dimension two for
k>\frac{1}{2}
; sharp smoothness indices often require Hardy or Besov spaces (see, e.g.,
[
39
]
), while odd dimensions allow Sobolev spaces (cf.
[
48
]
).

For
V\neq 0
and
n\geq 3
, Beals and Strauss
[
4
]
initiated
L^{\infty}
decay estimates (see also
[
3
,
12
,
26
]
). In dimension two, fewer
W^{k,1}\to L^{\infty}
or regularized
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimates exist. Moulin
[
43
]
studied high-frequency estimates; Kopylova
[
37
]
obtained
t^{-1}(\log t)^{-2}
decay when zero is regular; Green
[
30
]
improved weighted
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
bounds. Other dimensions include Cardoso–Vodev
[
8
]
for
4\leq n\leq 7
, Erdoğan–Goldberg–Green
[
13
]
for
n=4
, and further work in
[
5
,
27
,
6
,
7
,
26
,
12
]
.

Higher-order Schrödinger operators.

Recent progress has been made on time decay estimates for
e^{-itH}
with
H=\Delta^{2}+V
, where
V
is a real-valued decaying potential. Feng–Soffer–Yao
[
23
]
first obtained a
(1+|t|)^{-n/4}
decay bound for
n\geq 5
in the regular case. For
n=3
and
n=4
, Erdoğan–Green–Toprak
[
21
]
and Green–Toprak
[
31
]
showed
O(|t|^{-n/4})

L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimates when zero is regular or a first-kind resonance, and observed rate changes for other zero-energy resonances.

For
n=1
, Soffer–Wu–Yao
[
46
]
proved
O(|t|^{-1/4})
estimates regardless of zero regularity. For
n=2
, Li–Soffer–Yao
[
38
]
obtained optimal
O(|t|^{-1/2})
decay in the regular and first-kind resonance cases. For higher-order operators
H=(-\Delta)^{m}+V
with
2m<n<4m
, Erdoğan–Goldberg–Green
[
15
]
established
O(|t|^{-\frac{n}{2m}})

L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
bounds in the regular case.

Substantial advances have also been made on
L^{p}
-boundedness of wave operators for
H=(-\Delta)^{m}+V
with
m>1
: see
[
28
,
41
,
40
,
42
]
for
n<2m
with
m=2
,
[
25
]
for
n=2m
with
m=2
,
[
18
,
19
,
14
,
20
]
for
n>2m
with
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. Recently, Cheng et al.
[
11
]
established sharp boundedness of wave operators for all zero-resonance singularities in odd dimensions
1\leq n\leq 4m-1
with
m\geq 2
.

1.5.
Outline of the proof

In this subsection, we outline the main ideas underlying the proofs of our main theorems.
The starting point is the Stone formulas:

\displaystyle\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{3}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\left[R_{V}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{V}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\right]d\lambda,

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\sin(t\lambda^{2})\left[R_{V}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{V}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\right]d\lambda.

To make these identities precise, we recall that
R_{0}(z)=(\Delta^{2}-z)^{-1}
and
R_{V}(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
denote the resolvents of the free operator
\Delta^{2}
and the perturbed operator
H=\Delta^{2}+V
, respectively, for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
. Their boundary values (limiting resolvents) on
(0,\infty)
are defined as

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}R_{0}(\lambda\pm i\epsilon),\qquad R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}R_{V}(\lambda\pm i\epsilon),\quad\lambda>0.

The existence of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)
as bounded operators from
L^{2,s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
L^{2,-s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
s>1/2
follows from the limiting absorption principle for the resolvent
(-\Delta-z)^{-1}
(see, e.g., Agmon
[
2
]
) and the splitting identity

R_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{z}}\left[(-\Delta-\sqrt{z})^{-1}-(-\Delta+\sqrt{z})^{-1}\right],\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty),\ \Im\sqrt{z}>0.

The existence of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda)
under suitable potential decay conditions is established in the existing literature (see, e.g.,
[
23
]
).

To evaluate the Stone formulas, we decompose the integrals into a low-energy part (
0<\lambda\ll 1
) and a high-energy part (
\lambda\gtrsim 1
). The high-energy part is straightforward to treat since the free resolvent (see (
2.3
) and (
2.4
)) possesses no singularities for
\lambda\gtrsim 1
. The primary analytical difficulty lies in the low-energy part, which requires a delicate analysis of the singularities of
R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
near
\lambda=0
.

Setting
v(x)=\sqrt{|V(x)|}
and
U(x)=\operatorname{sgn}(V(x))
, we define the Birman-Schwinger operator
M^{\pm}(\lambda)=U+vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})v
. The symmetric resolvent identity,

R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})=R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})v\big(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4}),

(1.12)

reduces the problem to analyzing the asymptotic expansion of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
.

If zero is a resonance of the
\mathbf{k}
-th kind (
0\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 4
), Theorem
2.7
guarantees that
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
admits the following expansion:

\bigl(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr)^{-1}=\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2}\lambda^{2-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}Q_{\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta},

(1.13)

where the singularity indices are given by
k_{\alpha}\in\{0,1,1,1,2,3,3\}
for
\alpha=0,\dots,6
. The operators
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
exhibit additional decay as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
for certain indices
\alpha,\beta
. The orthogonal projections
Q_{\alpha}
satisfy the important cancellation properties (see Remark
2.4
), which are applied to establish Lemma
2.8
to analyze the expansion for
Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
. Lemma
2.8
demonstrates how the projections extract compensatory positive powers of
\lambda
, effectively annihilating the zero-energy singularities of the free resolvent.

Substituting the identities (
1.12
) and (
1.13
) into the low-energy part of the Stone formulas, we reduce the analysis to bounding a finite number of kernel differences

\big(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}\big)(t,x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}\big)(t,x,y),

where the component kernels are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\left[\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{\alpha}B^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\right](x,y)\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\left[\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{\alpha}B^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\right](x,y)\,d\lambda,

with
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mid 0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2\}
. By applying the expansion of
(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4}))(x,y)
established in Lemma
2.8
, the intricate integrals
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
can be reduced to canonical oscillatory forms systematically studied in Lemma
2.9
.
Consequently, the desired temporal decay estimates are ultimately derived by invoking the bounds of Lemma
2.9
.

To illustrate our general strategy for controlling these oscillatory integrals, let us examine a representative term where
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{1,1}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Using the Taylor expansion for
F_{\pm}
and the cancellation property
Q_{1}v=0
, we extract an additional positive power of
\lambda
(see Lemma
2.8
):

\bigl(Q_{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=-\lambda^{-1}Q_{1}\Bigl(|\cdot|v\int_{0}^{1}F_{\pm}^{\prime}(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)\cos\xi\,d\theta\Bigr)(x)=:\lambda^{-1}\Omega_{1,\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

where
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\lambda^{-2}F_{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
(see (
2.6
)). Hence, the term
Q_{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
is less singular than the free resolvent. Furthermore, we derive the bound

\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\Omega_{1,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell},\qquad\ell=0,1,2.

With these properties, the kernels can be recast into the canonical forms appearing in Lemma
2.9
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r}\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r}\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

with
r(x,y)=|x|+|y|
and the effective amplitude factor

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=e^{\mp i\lambda r}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\langle\mathcal{M}_{1,1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\Omega_{1,\pm}(\cdot,y),\Omega_{1,\mp}(\cdot,x)\rangle.

Applying Leibniz’s rule along with the bound
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{1,1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
yields

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\bigl\langle\lambda(|x|+|y|)\bigr\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2),\qquad\ell=0,1,2.

Then
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
satisfies condition (
2.26
) of Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
and
h(x,y)=1
, yielding the bound
|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}|+|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}|\lesssim|t|^{-1}
uniformly in
x,y
.

To establish the spatially weighted estimates for
|t|\geq 2
, we utilize the following expansion:

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}+\bigl(a_{1}^{\pm}+b_{0}\log\lambda\bigr)|x-y|^{2}+b_{0}|x-y|^{2}\log|x-y|+{O}_{2}\big(\lambda^{2}|x-y|^{4}\big)

\displaystyle=:\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}+E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y).

The cancellation property
Q_{1}v=0
annihilates the highly singular leading term
\lambda^{-2}
, meaning
\bigl(Q_{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)=\bigl(Q_{1}vE_{1}^{\pm}\bigr)
. This cancellation mitigates the low-energy singularity by a factor of
\lambda^{2-}
relative to
R_{0}^{\pm}
but introduces spatial growth. Provided that the potential
V
decays sufficiently fast, we deduce the bound:

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(Q_{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|v\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\langle y\rangle^{4}\lambda^{-\ell-},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

In this weighted scenario, we align the phase to
r(x,y)=0
and define the amplitude factor as

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\lambda^{2}\bigl\langle\mathcal{M}_{1,1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigl(Q_{1}vE_{1}^{\pm}\bigr)(\cdot,y),\bigl(Q_{1}vE_{1}^{\pm}\bigr)(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle.

Consequently, we derive the weighted bound

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{1/2-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

An application of Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(3/2,0)
and
h(x,y)=\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
yields the improved temporal decay
|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}|+|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}|\lesssim|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
. Combining this with the uniform
O(|t|^{-1})
bound via the inequality
\min(1,a/b)\lesssim\log a/\log b
(
a,b\geq 2
), we obtain the logarithmically weighted bound:

\min\left(\frac{1}{|t|},\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{5/4}}\right)=\frac{1}{|t|}\min\left(1,\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{1/4}}\right)\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},

which gives the desired result
\omega(x)\omega(y)(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
.

For the remaining operators
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mid 0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2\}
, the essential strategy remains unchanged. By exploiting the structural bounds of
Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
in conjunction with the operator norm bounds for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, the analysis is reduced to estimating canonical oscillatory integrals in Lemma
2.9
.

However, the analysis becomes more intricate and subtle when higher-order resonances occur (
\mathbf{k}\geq 2
). To achieve the sharp estimates for
\mathbf{k}\geq 2
, one must conduct a deeper investigation into the expansion structures of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and show that certain intricate operators do not vanish (see Proposition
6.4
, Proposition
6.8
, Proposition
7.5
and Lemma
7.9
). Despite these technical challenges, our unified framework resolves all threshold singularities, thereby providing a complete picture of the time-decay behavior for the two-dimensional plate equation across all resonance scenarios.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.

Section
2
studies the free and perturbed resolvent expansions near zero energy. This section also provides the equivalent characterizations of zero resonances and introduces two key lemmas (Lemmas
2.8
and
2.9
).

Sections
3
and
4
establish decay estimates for the free case and the high-energy part, respectively.

Sections
5
through
7
constitute the low-energy analysis, systematically treating the regular case and the first (Section
5
), second (Section
6
), and third kind resonance/eigenvalue cases (Section
7
).

Finally, Sections
8
through
10
are reserved for the deferred proofs concerning the resonance characterizations, the expansions of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
, and the oscillatory integral estimates.

2.
Resolvent expansions

2.1.
Free resolvent

In this subsection, we derive the asymptotic expansions of the free resolvent
R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
for the bi-Laplace operator
\Delta^{2}
.

We first recall the expression of the free resolvent for the Laplacian (see, e.g.,
[
17
]
):

R^{\pm}(-\Delta;\lambda^{2})(x,y)=\pm\frac{i}{4}H_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|),

(2.1)

where
H_{0}^{\pm}(z)=J_{0}(z)\pm iY_{0}(z)
are Hankel functions of order zero. From the series expansions for Bessel functions
(see, e.g.,
[
1
]
), we have

\displaystyle J_{0}(z)=1-\frac{1}{4}z^{2}+\frac{1}{64}z^{4}-\frac{1}{2304}z^{6}+\frac{1}{147456}z^{8}+O(z^{10}),

\displaystyle Y_{0}(z)=\frac{2}{\pi}\bigl(\log\frac{z}{2}+\gamma\bigr)J_{0}(z)+\frac{2}{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}z^{2}-\frac{3}{128}z^{4}+\frac{11}{13824}z^{6}-\frac{25}{1769472}z^{8}+O(z^{10})\Bigr),

where
\gamma
is Euler’s constant. In addition, for
|z|\gtrsim 1
,

H^{\pm}_{0}(z)=e^{\pm iz}w_{\pm}(z),\qquad|w_{\pm}^{(\ell)}(z)|\lesssim(1+|z|)^{-1/2-\ell},\ \ell=0,1,2,\cdots.

(2.2)

Recall the identity

R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})=\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}}\left(R^{\pm}(-\Delta;\lambda^{2})-R(-\Delta;-\lambda^{2})\right),\quad\lambda>0.

Together with (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), it follows that

R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\frac{i}{8\lambda^{2}}\left[\pm e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}w_{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)-e^{-\lambda|x-y|}w_{+}(i\lambda|x-y|)\right],\ \ \ \lambda|x-y|\gtrsim 1.

(2.3)

For
\lambda|x-y|\ll 1
, the series expansions of Bessel functions give

\begin{split}R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=&\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}G_{0}(x,y)+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)G_{2}(x,y)+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}(x,y)+a_{2}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}G_{4}(x,y)\\
&+g_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\lambda^{4}G_{6}(x,y)+b_{1}\lambda^{4}G_{6}^{0}(x,y)+a_{4}^{\pm}\lambda^{6}G_{8}(x,y)+O_{2}\bigl(\lambda^{8-\varepsilon}|x-y|^{10-\varepsilon}\bigr),\end{split}

(2.4)

where

b_{0}=\frac{1}{8\pi},\ b_{1}=\frac{1}{4608\pi},\ a_{0}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{8},\ a_{2}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{512},\ a_{4}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{1179648},
and

g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=b_{0}\log\lambda+a_{1}^{\pm},\ g_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda)=b_{1}\log\lambda+a_{3}^{\pm},\ \ a_{1}^{\pm},a_{3}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

G_{k}(x,y)=|x-y|^{k},\,G_{k}^{0}(x,y)=|x-y|^{k}\log|x-y|.

(2.5)

Combining (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), we obtain the following expansion of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)
for any
\lambda>0
:

Lemma 2.1

(
[
38
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

For any
\lambda>0
, we have the following expansions:

\begin{split}R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=&\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}G_{0}(x,y)+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)G_{2}(x,y)+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}(x,y)+a_{2}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}G_{4}(x,y)\\
&+g_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\lambda^{4}G_{6}(x,y)+b_{1}\lambda^{4}G_{6}^{0}(x,y)+O_{2}\bigl(\lambda^{6}|x-y|^{8}\bigr).\end{split}

For convenience in the subsequent analysis, we define

R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y):=\lambda^{-2}F_{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|),\quad\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|):=\lambda^{2}e^{\mp i\lambda|x-y|}R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y).

(2.6)

Then
F_{\pm}(p)=e^{\pm ip}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(p)
. From (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(p)=

\displaystyle e^{\mp ip}\Bigl[a_{0}^{\pm}+b_{0}p^{2}\log p+a_{1}^{\pm}p^{2}+a_{2}^{\pm}p^{4}+b_{1}p^{6}\log p+a_{3}^{\pm}p^{6}+O_{2}\bigl(p^{8}\bigr)\Bigr]\chi_{1}(p)

(2.7)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{8}\Bigl(\pm w_{\pm}(p)-e^{\mp ip}e^{-p}w_{+}(ip)\Bigr)\chi_{2}(p).

In the sequel, we denote by
G_{k}^{0}
(resp.
G_{k}
) the integral operator with kernel
G_{k}^{0}(x,y)
(resp.
G_{k}(x,y)
) defined in (
2.5
).
In particular,
b_{0}G_{2}^{0}
is a fundamental solution of
\Delta^{2}
, i.e.,
\Delta^{2}(b_{0}G_{2}^{0})=\delta
in the sense of distributions. Consequently, on the orthogonal complement of
\ker(\Delta^{2})
, we may identify
b_{0}G_{2}^{0}
with
(\Delta^{2})^{-1}
.

2.2.
Asymptotic expansion of
R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
near zero

This subsection is devoted to the asymptotic expansion of the resolvent
R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
. We begin by introducing several key projection subspaces of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
v=\sqrt{|V|}
. Define
P=\|V\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{-1}\langle\cdot,v\rangle v
and
T_{0}:=U+b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v
. Set
S_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits
,
S_{6}=0
, and for
j=0,\dots,5
, let
S_{j}
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
S_{j}L^{2}
defined as follows:

•

S_{0}L^{2}=\{f\in L^{2}\mid\langle v,f\rangle=0\}
.

•

S_{1}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{0}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{j}v,f\rangle=0,\ j=1,2\}
.

•

S_{2}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{1}L^{2}\mid S_{1}T_{0}f=0\}
.

•

S_{3}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{2}L^{2}\mid S_{0}T_{0}f=0\}
.

•

S_{4}^{0}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{3}L^{2}\mid\langle|x|^{2}v,f\rangle=0\}
.

•

S_{4}^{1}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{3}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{i}x_{j}v,f\rangle=0,\ i,j=1,2\}
.

•

S_{4}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{3}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{i}x_{j}v,f\rangle=0,\ i,j=1,2,\ \text{and}\ PT_{0}f=0\}
.

•

S_{5}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{4}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}v,f\rangle=0,\ i,j,k=1,2\}
.

Note that these projections
S_{j}
are well-defined due to the decay condition (
2.8
) on
V
. Since
vG_{2}^{0}v
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator and

T_{0}=U+b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v

is a compact perturbation of
U
, Fredholm alternative then ensures that
S_{2}
is a finite-rank projection. Moreover, observe that
P+S_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits
, then it follows that
S_{j}T_{0}=T_{0}S_{j}=0
for
j=4,5.

From the definitions above, we have the chain of inclusions

S_{0}L^{2}\supseteq S_{1}L^{2}\supseteq S_{2}L^{2}\supseteq S_{3}L^{2}\supseteq S_{4}^{0}L^{2}\supseteq S_{4}^{1}L^{2}\supseteq S_{4}L^{2}\supseteq S_{5}L^{2},

and the projections
S_{j}
(for
j=2,\cdots,5
) and
S_{4}^{0},S_{4}^{1}
are finite-rank operators.

We now introduce the following complementary family of orthogonal projections.

Definition 2.3
.

Define

Q_{j}:=S_{j-1}-S_{j}\quad\text{for }j=0,1,\dots,6,\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{4}^{0}=S_{3}-S_{4}^{0},\quad Q_{4}^{1}=S_{4}^{0}-S_{4}.

and further decompose
Q_{4}^{1}
into the sum of the following projectors:

Q_{41}^{1}:=S_{4}^{0}-S_{4}^{1},\qquad Q_{42}^{1}:=S_{4}^{1}-S_{4}.

In particular, we observe that
Q_{0}=P.

According to Definitions
2.2
and
2.3
, we obtain the following cancellation properties:

Remark 2.4
.

The orthogonal projections
Q_{4}^{0}
,
Q_{4}^{1}
, and
Q_{\alpha}
(for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
0\leq\alpha\leq 6
) satisfy:

\displaystyle Q_{1}v=0\quad(\text{i.e., }\langle v,Q_{1}f\rangle=0,\ \forall f\in L^{2}),

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}v=Q_{\alpha}(x_{j}v)=0\quad(\alpha=2,3,4),

\displaystyle Q_{4}^{0}v=Q_{4}^{0}(x_{j}v)=0,

\displaystyle Q_{4}^{1}v=Q_{4}^{1}(x_{j}v)=Q_{4}^{1}(|x|^{2}v)=0,\ \ Q_{41}^{1}v=Q_{41}^{1}(x_{j}v)=Q_{41}^{1}(|x|^{2}v)=0,

\displaystyle Q_{42}^{1}v=Q_{42}^{1}(x_{j}v)=Q_{42}^{1}(x_{i}x_{j}v)=0,

\displaystyle Q_{5}v=Q_{5}(x_{j}v)=Q_{5}(x_{i}x_{j}v)=0,\,Q_{5}T_{0}=T_{0}Q_{5}=0,

\displaystyle Q_{6}v=Q_{6}(x_{j}v)=Q_{6}(x_{i}x_{j}v)=Q_{6}(x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}v)=0,\ Q_{6}T_{0}=T_{0}Q_{6}=0.

Besides Definition
1.1
, zero-energy resonances of
H
can also be characterized by the orthogonal projections
S_{j}
; the proof is given in Section
8
.
Recall that
\mathbf{k}\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
denotes the zero-energy spectral types:
\mathbf{k}=0
for a regular point,
\mathbf{k}\in\{1,2,3\}
for zero resonances, and
\mathbf{k}=4
for a zero eigenvalue. Throughout this paper, the decay index
\mu
of the potential
V
is required to satisfy

\mu>\begin{cases}11,&\mathbf{k}=0,1,\\
14,&\mathbf{k}=2,\\
18,&\mathbf{k}=3,4.\end{cases}

(2.8)

Proposition 2.5
.

Assume that
\left|V(x)\right|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0

satisfying the condition (
2.8
).
Then

(i)

Zero is a regular point of
H

(\text{i.e.,}\ \mathbf{k}=0)
if and only if
S_{2}=0
;

(ii)

Zero is a
\mathbf{k}
-th kind resonance with
1\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 4
if and only if
S_{\mathbf{k}+1}\neq 0
and
S_{\mathbf{k}+2}=0
.

The relationship between the orthogonal projections
Q_{j}
and
S_{j}
, as given in Definition
2.3
, yields the following equivalent characterizations in terms of these
\{Q_{j}\}
:

Proposition 2.6
.

Assume that
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
satisfying the condition (
2.8
). Then

(i)

Zero is a regular point of
H

(i.e.,\mathbf{k}=0)
if and only if

Q_{0}+Q_{1}+Q_{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits.

(ii)

Zero is a
\mathbf{k}
-th kind resonance of
H
with
1\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 4
, if and only if

\sum_{j=0}^{\mathbf{k}+1}Q_{j}\neq\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{j}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits.

Next we turn to study the asymptotic expansions of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda^{4})
near
\lambda=0.

Let
U(x)=\hbox{sign}\big(V(x)\big)
and
v(x)=|V(x)|^{1/2}
, then we have
V=Uv^{2}
and
the following symmetric resolvent identity:

R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda^{4})=R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})v(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4}),

(2.9)

where
M^{\pm}(\lambda)=U+vR^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})v
. Hence, we need to establish the expansions of

(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
.

Recall that
T_{0}:=U+v(\Delta^{2})^{-1}v=U+b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v
, where
b_{0}G_{2}^{0}
is the fundamental solution operator of
\Delta^{2}
with a constant
b_{0}
given in Lemma
2.1
.
Using the expansions of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)
from Lemma
2.1
, we obtain the following asymptotic expansions of
M^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

•

If
\mu>11
, then

\begin{split}M^{\pm}(\lambda)=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}vG_{0}v+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)vG_{2}v+T_{0}+\Gamma_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda);\end{split}

(2.10)

•

If
\mu>14
, then

\begin{split}M^{\pm}(\lambda)=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}vG_{0}v+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)vG_{2}v+T_{0}+a_{2}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}vG_{4}v+\Gamma_{4-\epsilon}^{\pm}(\lambda);\end{split}

(2.11)

•

If
\mu>18
, then

\begin{split}M^{\pm}(\lambda)=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}vG_{0}v+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)vG_{2}v+T_{0}+a_{2}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}vG_{4}v+g_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda)\lambda^{4}vG_{6}v+b_{1}\lambda^{4}vG_{6}^{0}v+\Gamma_{6}^{\pm}(\lambda).\end{split}

(2.12)

Here,
\Gamma_{s}^{\pm}(\lambda)
denote
\lambda
-dependent Hilbert-Schmidt operators satisfying

\big\|\Gamma_{s}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\lambda\big\|\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma_{s}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\lambda^{2}\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\Gamma_{s}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{s}.

We now provide the asymptotic expansion of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
; the proof can be seen in Section
9
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Assume that zero is a
\mathbf{k}
-th kind resonance with
0\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 4
, and
|V(x)|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
satisfying the condition (
2.8
).
Then there exists
0<\lambda_{0}\ll 1
such that for all
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
, the operator
\left(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)^{-1}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
admits the asymptotic expansion:

\left(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)^{-1}=\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2}\lambda^{2-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}Q_{\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta},

(2.13)

where the values of the parameter
k_{\alpha}
are assigned as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c|ccccccc}\alpha&0&1&2&3&4&5&6\\
\hline\cr k_{\alpha}&0&1&1&1&2&3&3\end{array}

(2.14)

Moreover, the operators
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
possess the following properties:

(i)
For all
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\Big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\neq(3,3),\\
|\log\lambda|\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)=(3,3).\end{cases}

In particular, for
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
,
under the additional condition
Q_{4}^{0}=0
, the following refined asymptotic expansion holds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}+\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda),

(2.15)

where
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,2
, and the leading term is given by

\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}+(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-2}\mathcal{W}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}^{*}:=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{\pm}.

(2.16)

Here,
\mathcal{W}=\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}
and

(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}:=\bigl[a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}-(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{4}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}\bigr]^{-1}.

(2.17)

(ii)
For
\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}
, we have

\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{4},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Let
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)
denote a generic operator in
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
(possibly varying at each occurrence) satisfying

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\ \ell=0,1,2.

For
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(0,4),(4,0),(4,4)\}
, we have

Case 1:
Q_{4}^{0}=0
.
We have

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}+\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),

where the operators
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
are explicitly given by

\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}=(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1},\quad\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}=-(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1},\quad\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}=-(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}^{*},

(2.18)

with
\mathcal{W}
and
\bm{d}^{\pm}
defined in (
2.17
).

Case 2:
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
.
This case further splits based on
Q_{42}^{1}
:

•

Subcase
Q_{42}^{1}=0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\sum_{h,l\in\{0,1\}}(\log\lambda)^{-(2-h-l)}Q_{4}^{h}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{l};

•

Subcase
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}+Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}+(\log\lambda)\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}

\displaystyle+(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}

\displaystyle+(\log\lambda)\bigl(Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}.

Here, setting
\mathcal{W}_{1}=(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1},
the operators
\mathcal{C}^{\pm},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}
are given by

\mathcal{C}^{\pm}:=(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*},\quad\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}:=-(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1},\quad\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}:=-(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*},

(2.19)

and
\mathcal{S}
is strictly positive on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}

defined by

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*}\bigl[Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}-Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}(Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1})^{-1}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}\bigr]\mathcal{W}_{1}.

(iii)
For
\alpha\in\{5,6\}
and
0\leq\beta\leq 4
, we have for
\ell=0,1,2
:

\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}+\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}.

For the remaining principal pairs, we have

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda),&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(5,5),(5,6),(6,5)\},\\[4.30554pt]
\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)=(6,6).\end{cases}

Here, the principal terms
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)
are independent of the sign
\pm
, and the remainders take the form

\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda),

with the leading term defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{5,5}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=-a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}^{-2}(Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1},\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{5,6}^{\pm}=-a_{3}^{\pm}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{6,5}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=-a_{3}^{\pm}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5},\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{6,6}^{\pm}=a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6},

where
\mathcal{D}_{5,5}=-(b_{1}Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{6,6}=(b_{1}Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})^{-1}
.

Moreover, all
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)
,
\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, and
\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
belong to
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
and satisfy

\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

2.3.
Two fundamental lemmas

This subsection presents two key lemmas: the first analyzes the kernel of
Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
, and the second establishes oscillatory integral estimates.

We begin with the definition of the operator
Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
:

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})f\bigr)(x)=Q_{\alpha}\!\Bigl(v(\cdot)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y)f(y)\,dy\Bigr)(x).

Its integral kernel is given explicitly by

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=Q_{\alpha}\!\left(v\,R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y)\right)(x).

The structural decompositions and
L^{2}
-estimates of this integral kernel are summarized in the following Lemma
2.8
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
Q_{\alpha}
(
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq\alpha\leq 6
) and
Q_{4}^{0},Q_{4}^{1},Q_{41}^{1},Q_{42}^{1}
be defined by (
2.3
). Assume
\left|V(x)\right|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>0
satisfying the condition (
2.8
). Then, for
0<\lambda\ll 1
, the integral kernels admit the following decompositions:

(i)

For
0\leq\alpha\leq 6
,

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=\lambda^{-1-\delta_{\alpha 0}}\,\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

where
\delta_{\alpha 0}=1
if
\alpha=0
and
0
otherwise.

(ii)

For
2\leq\alpha\leq 6
,

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{0}(\lambda,x,y),\quad\bigl(Q_{4}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{1}(\lambda,x,y),

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{41}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{1,1}(\lambda,x,y),\quad\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,x,y).

(iii)

For
2\leq\alpha\leq 6
,
setting
m_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha=2,3,4
,
m_{5}=1
, and
m_{6}=2
:

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)=(b_{0}Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})(x,y)+b_{0}(\log\lambda)\,Q_{\alpha}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)+\lambda^{m_{\alpha}}(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\pm}+\mathcal{T}_{\alpha})(\lambda,x,y),

where the term
b_{0}(\log\lambda)\,Q_{\alpha}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)
vanishes
for
\alpha=5,6
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}^{0})(x,y)+b_{0}(\log\lambda)\,Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)+(\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{0}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0})(\lambda,x,y),

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{4}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(x,y)+(\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1})(\lambda,x,y),

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{41}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(x,y)+(\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,1}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,1})(\lambda,x,y),

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(x,y)+\lambda\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2})(\lambda,x,y).

Furthermore, for
\ell=0,1,2
, these integral kernels satisfy the following bounds:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\big(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{J}_{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\big)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2},\ \text{for }\mathcal{J}_{\pm}\in\{\mathcal{J}_{\alpha,\pm}|_{2\leq\alpha\leq 4},\,\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{j}|_{j=0,1},\,\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{1,j}|_{j=1,2}\},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\bigl\|(b_{0}QvG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\log(2+|y|),&\text{for }Q\in\{Q_{\alpha}|_{2\leq\alpha\leq 4},Q_{4}^{j}|_{j=0,1},Q_{41}^{1}\},\\[2.0pt]
1,&\text{for }Q\in\{Q_{5},Q_{6},Q_{42}^{1}\},\end{cases}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\big(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}F(\lambda,\cdot,y)\big)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\log(2+|y|),\quad\text{for }\mathcal{T}\in\{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}|_{2\leq\alpha\leq 4},\,\mathcal{T}_{4}^{j}|_{j=0,1},\,\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,1}\},

(2.24)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\alpha=5,6,

(2.25)

where
F
generically denotes any term among
\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}\,(0\leq\alpha\leq 6)
,
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\pm}\,(2\leq\alpha\leq 6)
,
\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{j}\,(j=0,1)
, or
\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,j}\,(j=1,2)
.

The proof of Lemma
2.8
relies heavily on the cancellation properties of the projections
Q_{\alpha}
. We defer it to Section
10
.

Next, we state Lemma
2.9
which serves as a fundamental tool for the oscillatory integrals encountered in the subsequent decay analysis. Its proof is also given in Section
10
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
h(x,y)
and
r:=r(x,y)
be real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
h(x,y)>0
. Define the oscillatory integrals

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r}\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r}\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda.

(i)

Suppose that for
\ell=0,1,2
, the amplitude factor
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right|\lesssim h(x,y)\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\ell}.

(2.26)

Then

\left|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim h(x,y)|t|^{-1}.

(ii)

Suppose that for
\ell=0,1,2
,
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
exhibits a low-frequency behavior localized by the smooth cutoff
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)
supported in
[0,2(2\lambda_{0})^{1/4}]
and

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right|\lesssim h(x,y)\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}\lambda^{-\sigma-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2).

(2.27)

Then the following estimates hold:

\bullet
For
0<\sigma<2
with
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, or
\sigma=0
with
\nu\leq 0
:

\left|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{h(x,y)}{\langle t\rangle^{1-\frac{\sigma}{2}}(\log(2+|t|))^{\nu}}.

\bullet
For
\sigma=2
and
\nu>1
:

\left|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{h(x,y)}{\left(\log(2+|t|)\right)^{\nu-1}}.

Furthermore, in the specific cases where
0<\sigma<2
with
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, or
\sigma=2
with
\nu>1
, the bounds (
2.27
) only require derivative conditions up to
\ell=1
.

3.
The decay estimates for the free case

In this section, we establish the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
and weighted
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
bounds for the free case
V=0
. The corresponding unperturbed operator is
\sqrt{H}=-\Delta
, which corresponds to the evolution propagators
\cos(t\Delta)
and
\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)
.
By Stone’s formula, their kernels can be expressed as

\displaystyle\cos(t\Delta)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{3}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\left[R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\right](x,y)d\lambda,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\sin(t\lambda^{2})\left[R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\right](x,y)d\lambda.

(3.2)

On the other side, the kernels of
\cos(t\Delta)
and
\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)
can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\cos,0}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\sin\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right),\quad K_{t}^{\sin,0}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi|t|}\int_{\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t|}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\,du.

(3.3)

Indeed, the well-known kernel of the free Schrödinger group
e^{it\Delta}
:

e^{it\Delta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi it}\exp\bigl(i\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\bigr),

and the identity
\cos(t\Delta)=(e^{it\Delta}+e^{-it\Delta})/2
yield
K_{t}^{\cos,0}(x,y)
. The sine kernel
K_{t}^{\sin,0}(x,y)
then follows from the relation
\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cos(s\Delta)\,ds
via the substitution
u=\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}
.

Based on these representations, we obtain the following sharp decay estimates.

Proposition 3.1
.

The free operators satisfy the following sharp bounds:

(i)

In the unweighted space
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
:

\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim|t|^{-1},\quad\Bigl\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Bigr\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

(ii)

In the weighted space
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
:

\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{-1},\quad\Bigl\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Bigr\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

Proof.

(i) The sharp uniform bounds
\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim|t|^{-1}
and
\|\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim|t|^{-1}
follow trivially by taking the supremum over the explicit kernels in (
3.3
). However, to set the stage for subsequent perturbation arguments, we provide an alternative proof for the upper bound via oscillatory integrals.

In view of (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), and substituting
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\lambda^{-2}e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
(see (
2.6
) and (
2.7
)), the problem reduces to estimating

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)\,d\lambda.

By virtue of (
2.7
), it is straightforward to verify that for
\ell=0,1,2
,

\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)\right|\lesssim\langle\lambda|x-y|\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}.

Applying Lemma
2.9
(i) with
r(x,y)=|x-y|
and
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
, we deduce that both
|\mathcal{K}^{\pm}|
and
|\mathcal{N}^{\pm}|
are uniformly bounded by
{O}(|t|^{-1})
, concluding the proof of (i).

(ii) We evaluate the weighted operator norm for
\cos(t\Delta)
by taking the spatial supremum:

\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}=\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|K_{t}^{\cos,0}(x,y)|}{\omega(x)\omega(y)}.

Setting
\rho=|x-y|
and utilizing the basic weight inequality
\omega(x)\omega(y)\geq\omega(0)\omega(\rho/2)
, we have

\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\sup_{\rho\geq 0}\frac{|\sin(\rho^{2}/(4t))|}{|t|\,\omega(\rho/2)}=\sup_{u\geq 0}\frac{|\sin u|}{|t|\log\bigl(2+\sqrt{|t|u}\bigr)}.

(3.4)

For
u>1
, we use
|\sin u|\leq 1
and
\log(2+\sqrt{|t|u})\geq\log(2+\sqrt{|t|})\sim\log(2+|t|)
. For
0\leq u\leq 1
, using
|\sin u|\leq u
and the substitution
v=\sqrt{|t|u}
yields

\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\frac{|\sin u|}{\log(2+\sqrt{|t|u})}\leq\sup_{0\leq v\leq\sqrt{|t|}}\frac{v^{2}/|t|}{\log(2+v)}=\frac{1}{\log(2+\sqrt{|t|})}\sim\frac{1}{\log(2+|t|)}.

Inserting these into (
3.4
) establishes the upper bound
\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim|t|^{-1}\bigl(\log(2+|t|)\bigr)^{-1}
. The matching lower bound follows by testing the kernel at
y=0
and
|x|=\sqrt{2\pi|t|}
.

For
\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)
, the unweighted bound from part (i) implies the weighted upper bound. Evaluating
K_{t}^{\sin,0}(0,0)=1/(8|t|)
provides the corresponding lower bound, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

For a general dimension
n
, the kernel of
\cos(t\Delta)
admits the explicit form

\cos(t\Delta)(x,y)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\cos\!\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}-\frac{n\pi}{4}\right),

which trivially yields the sharp decay estimate
\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-n/2}
.
In the weighted setting, the enhanced decay bound

\|\cos(t\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-n/2}\bigl(\log(2+|t|)\bigr)^{-1}

holds if and only if
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
(e.g.,
n=2,6,10,\dots
). For all other dimensions, the logarithmic gain is absent, yielding purely polynomial decay. This dimension-dependent behavior arises because the dimensional phase shift
n\pi/4
determines whether the oscillatory kernel vanishes along the diagonal
x=y
.

Remark 3.3
.

(i) The same analysis as in Proposition
3.1
for
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}(t,x,y)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\pm}(t,x,y)
yields

\big\|\cos(t\Delta)\chi_{j}(\Delta)\big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\chi_{j}(\Delta)\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-1},\qquad j=1,2.

For the high-frequency part, the sine propagator actually satisfies
the stronger bound

\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\,\chi_{2}(\Delta)\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-2}.

Indeed, when
\lambda\gtrsim 1
, Remark
10.2
(i) gives

\Bigl|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}\,\lambda\,\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)\,d\lambda\Bigr|\lesssim|t|^{-1},

which after multiplication by the factor
t^{-1}
present in
\bigl(\sin(t\Delta)/(t\Delta)\bigr)\chi_{2}(\Delta)
produces the
|t|^{-2}
decay. Consequently,
the same bound
|t|^{-2}
also holds in the weighted space
L^{1}_{\omega}\rightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
.

(ii)

\cos(t\Delta)\chi_{j}(\Delta)
satisfies the same weighted decay as
\cos(t\Delta)
:

\|\cos(t\Delta)\chi_{j}(\Delta)\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim(|t|\log(2+|t|))^{-1},\qquad j=1,2.

Finally, we establish the asymptotic expansion for the low-frequency free sine kernel,
which is essential for the subsequent log-weighted estimates in the regular and the first kind resonance cases.

Proposition 3.4
.

For
|t|\geq 2,
the low-frequency free sine kernel admits the asymptotic expansion:

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\chi_{1}(\Delta)(x,y)=\frac{1}{8|t|}+O\left(\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|}\right).

Proof.

Recall the kernel representation of the full free sine propagator from (
3.3
):

\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi|t|}\int_{\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t|}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\,du=\frac{1}{8|t|}-\frac{1}{4\pi|t|}\int^{\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t|}}_{0}\frac{\sin u}{u}\,du,

(3.5)

where we used the standard Dirichlet integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}du=\frac{\pi}{2}
.
Using the uniform bound
|\frac{\sin u}{u}|\leq 1
and the inequality
|x-y|^{2}\leq\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}
, we derive

\frac{1}{4\pi|t|}\Big|\int_{0}^{\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t|}}\frac{\sin u}{u}\,du\Big|\leq\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{16\pi|t|^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}}{|t|^{2}}.

(3.6)

To isolate the low-frequency part, we decompose the operator as
\chi_{1}(\Delta)=\mathrm{Id}-\chi_{2}(\Delta)
. According to Remark
3.3
(i), the high-frequency part satisfies

\Big|\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\chi_{2}(\Delta)(x,y)\Big|\lesssim|t|^{-2}.

(3.7)

Combining (
3.5
), (
3.6
), and (
3.7
), we deduce that

\frac{\sin(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\chi_{1}(\Delta)(x,y)-\frac{1}{8|t|}={O}\!\left(\frac{\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}}{|t|^{2}}\right).

Finally, minimizing with the uniform bound
|t|^{-1}
via using
\min(1,a/b)\lesssim\log a/\log b
for
a,b\geq 2
, we obtain

\min\Bigl(\frac{1}{|t|},\frac{\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}}{|t|^{2}}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{|t|}\min\Bigl(1,\frac{\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}}{|t|}\Bigr)\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},\qquad|t|\geq 2,

which yields the desired result.
∎

4.
High energy decay estimates

In this section, we focus on the high-energy part of
\cos(t\sqrt{H})
and
\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}
, establishing the bounds stated in Theorem
4.1
below.
In view of the identities

\cos(t\sqrt{H})=\frac{e^{it\sqrt{H}}+e^{-it\sqrt{H}}}{2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}=\frac{e^{it\sqrt{H}}-e^{-it\sqrt{H}}}{2i\sqrt{H}},

it suffices to analyze the operator
H^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}e^{-it\sqrt{H}}
for
\gamma\in\{-1,0\}
.
By using Stone’s formula,

H^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}e^{-it\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{3+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)\left[R_{V}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{V}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\right]d\lambda,

(4.1)

where
\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)=\chi_{2}(\lambda^{4})(\lambda>0)
so that
\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}\subset[\lambda_{0}^{1/4},\infty)
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
|V(x)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{-4-}
. Assume that
H=\Delta^{2}+V
has no positive embedded eigenvalue and
P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)
denotes the projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum of
H
. Then

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)\Bigr\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\Bigl\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)\Bigr\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

Moreover, for
|t|\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)\Bigr\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim({|t|\log|t|})^{-1},\quad\Bigl\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)\Bigr\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-2}.

In this section, we use the following resolvent identity:

R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})=R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})+R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4}).

Substituting this expansion into (
4.1
) and
invoking the free estimates in Remark
3.3
,
we only need to analyze the following integral kernels for
\gamma\in\{-1,0\}
:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{3+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)[R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})](x,y)d\lambda,

\displaystyle L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{3+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)[R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})](x,y)d\lambda.

More precisely, the high-energy part of
\cos(t\sqrt{H})
is dictated by
L_{1}^{\pm}(0,t,x,y)
and
L_{2}^{\pm}(0,t,x,y)
, while the high-energy part for
{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}/({t\sqrt{H}})
corresponds to
t^{-1}L_{1}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)
and
t^{-1}L_{2}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)
. The desired bounds for these kernels are established in Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
, respectively.

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume that
|V(x)|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-4-}
. Then for
\gamma=-1,0,

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-1},\ \ \ \ \left|L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}.

Moreover,
|L_{1}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
t,x,y.

Proof.

Recalling that
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\lambda^{-2}e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
(see (
2.6
) and (
2.7
)), we rewrite

L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{1}^{\pm}(t,x,y,y_{1})\,V(y_{1})\,dy_{1},

where

K_{1}^{\pm}(t,x,y,y_{1}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}\lambda\,e^{\pm i\lambda r}\,\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y,y_{1})\,d\lambda,

(4.2)

with
r=r(x,y,y_{1})=|x-y_{1}|+|y-y_{1}|
and

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y,y_{1})=\lambda^{-2+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)\,\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y_{1}|)\,\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|y-y_{1}|).

(4.3)

By virtue of (
2.7
), it can be checked that for
\ell=0,1,2,

\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)\right|\lesssim\langle\lambda|x-y|\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\ell}.

Then for
\ell=0,1,2
,

\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y,y_{1})\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-2+2\gamma-\ell}\langle\lambda|x-y_{1}|\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\langle\lambda|y-y_{1}|\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-2+2\gamma-\ell}\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Recalling of Remark
10.2
(i), we conclude that
