|K_{1}^{\pm}(t,x,y,y_{1})|\lesssim|t|^{-1},

uniformly in
x,y,y_{1}
. Combining this with
\int|V(y_{1})|dy_{1}<\infty
,
we deduce that for
\gamma=-1,0,

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}|L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)|\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

Moreover, when
\gamma=-1
, it follows directly from (
4.3
) that
|\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y,y_{1})|\lesssim\lambda^{-3}
. Then by (
4.2
),
|K_{1}^{\pm}(t,x,y,y_{1})|\lesssim 1
when
\gamma=-1
. Hence
|L_{1}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
t,x,y.

For the pointwise estimate for
L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)
, note that for
\ell=0,1,2
,

\Bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\pm i\lambda r}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y,y_{1})\bigr)\Bigr|\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq\ell}\lambda^{-2+2\gamma-\ell_{1}}|x-y_{1}|^{\ell_{2}}|y-y_{1}|^{\ell_{3}}\lesssim\lambda^{-2+2\gamma}\langle x\rangle^{\ell}\langle y\rangle^{\ell}\langle y_{1}\rangle^{\ell}.

Applying integration by parts twice to the integral (
4.2
)(the boundary terms vanish) yields

|K_{1}^{\pm}(t,x,y,y_{1})|\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}\langle y_{1}\rangle^{2}\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\infty}\lambda^{-3+2\gamma}d\lambda\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}\langle y_{1}\rangle^{2}.

Hence by
\int\langle y_{1}\rangle^{2}|V(y_{1})|dy_{1}<\infty
, it follows that
\left|L_{1}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}.

∎

We next consider the integral kernels associated with
[R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})VR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})](x,y).

To deal with these integral kernels, we utilize the following estimates.

Lemma 4.3

(
[
23
, Theorem 2.23]
)
.

Assume that
|V(x)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{-\mu}
for some
\mu>k+1
with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and that
H=\Delta^{2}+V
has no positive eigenvalues. Then, for any
\sigma>k+\frac{1}{2},R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),L^{2,-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)
are
C^{k}
-continuous for all
\lambda>0
. Furthermore,

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{k}R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow L^{2,-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=O\left(|\lambda|^{-\frac{3(k+1)}{4}}\right),~\lambda\rightarrow+\infty.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume that
|V(x)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{-4-}
. Then for
\gamma=-1,0
,

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1},\ \ \ \ \left|L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}.

Proof.

Recalling that
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\lambda^{-2}e^{\pm i\lambda|x-y|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
, we express the inner product as

\displaystyle\big\langle R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})V(R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(*,y))(\cdot),VR_{0}^{\mp}(\lambda^{4})(x,\cdot)\big\rangle=\lambda^{-4}e^{\pm i\lambda(|x|+|y|)}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

where

E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\big\langle R_{V}^{\pm}V\big(e^{\pm i\lambda(|*-y|-|y|)}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|*-y|)\big)(\cdot),V\big(e^{\mp i\lambda(|x-\cdot|-|x|)}\widetilde{R}^{\mp}(\lambda|x-\cdot|)\big)\big\rangle.

Then we rewrite

L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda^{2}}e^{\pm i\lambda r}\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)d\lambda,\\

(4.4)

with
r=r(x,y)=|x|+|y|
and

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\lambda^{-2+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y).

Note that for
\ell=0,1,2
,

\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}e^{\pm i\lambda(|x-\cdot|-|x|)}\right|\lesssim|\cdot|^{\ell},\ \ \ \left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-\cdot|)\right|\lesssim\langle\lambda x\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\langle\cdot\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}-\ell},

(4.5)

where the second bound utilizes the inequality
\langle\lambda(x-\cdot)\rangle^{-1/2}\lesssim\langle\lambda x\rangle^{-1/2}\langle\cdot\rangle^{1/2}\lambda^{1/2}
for
\lambda\gtrsim 1
.
By Lemma
4.3
, for
\sigma>\ell+\frac{1}{2}
with
\ell=0,1,2
:

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\right\|_{L^{2,\sigma}\rightarrow L^{2,-\sigma}}\lesssim\lambda^{-3}.

(4.6)

Given
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-4-}
, by (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), Hölder’s inequality yields for
\lambda\gtrsim 1
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\left\langle\lambda x\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle\lambda y\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\leq\ell}\lambda\left\|V(\cdot)\langle\cdot\rangle^{\ell_{1}+\sigma+\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\cdot\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{2}}R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\right\|_{L^{2,\sigma}\rightarrow L^{2,-\sigma}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-2}{{\left\langle\lambda x\right\rangle}^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\left\langle\lambda y\right\rangle}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\left\langle\lambda(|x|+|y|)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-2},

with
\sigma>\ell_{2}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Hence for
\gamma=-1,0,

\lambda\gtrsim 1
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right|=\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(\lambda^{-2+2\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}(\lambda)E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right)\right|\lesssim\left\langle\lambda(|x|+|y|)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\ell}.

Invoking Remark
10.2
(i), we immediately deduce that
for
\gamma=-1,0
,

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}|L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)|\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

On the other hand, the bound
|E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)|\lesssim\lambda^{-2}
implies that
|\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)|\lesssim\lambda^{-3+2\gamma}
. Hence by (
4.4
) and
\lambda\gtrsim 1
, we derive that for
\gamma=-1,0,

|L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
t,x,y.

For the pointwise estimate for
L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)
,
it can be checked that for
\ell=0,1,2,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\pm i\lambda r}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq\ell}\lambda^{-2+2\gamma-\ell_{1}}(|x|+|y|)^{\ell_{2}}\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{3}}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq\ell}\lambda^{-2+2\gamma-\ell_{1}}\langle x\rangle^{\ell_{2}}\langle y\rangle^{\ell_{2}}\left\langle\lambda(|x|+|y|)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-2}\lesssim\lambda^{-4+2\gamma}\langle x\rangle^{\ell}\langle y\rangle^{\ell}.

Integrating by parts twice in
\lambda
again gives for
\gamma=-1,0
,

|L_{2}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)|\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\infty}\lambda^{-4+2\gamma}\,d\lambda\lesssim|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
:
With Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
at hand, we
immediately obtain the pointwise estimate
|t|^{-2}\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}

and the uniform bound
|t|^{-1}
of
L_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)
for
j=1,2,
and
\gamma=-1,0.
In particular, we have

|t|^{-1}|L_{j}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)|\lesssim|t|^{-1}\langle t\rangle^{-1}
for
j=1,2.

Moreover, by the inequality
\min(1,a/b)\lesssim\log a/\log b
(valid for
a,b\geq 2
),
it follows that for
|t|\geq 2
,

|L_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma,t,x,y)|\lesssim\min\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{|t|},\frac{\langle x\rangle^{2}\langle y\rangle^{2}}{|t|^{2}}\Bigr)\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|}.

Combine these with Remark
3.3
, and recall that
\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)
corresponds to
L_{j}^{\pm}(0,t,x,y)
, while
{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}/({t\sqrt{H}})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{2}(H)
corresponds to
t^{-1}L_{j}^{\pm}(-1,t,x,y)
, then we complete the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

5.
The regular and first-kind resonance cases

Having established the high-energy dispersive estimates in Theorem
4.1
, the proofs of our main results (Theorems
1.2
,
1.4
,
1.7
, and
1.8
) are reduced to the low-energy part. In this and the subsequent two sections, we systematically derive the required low-energy dispersive bounds.

We begin by recalling Stone’s formulas for the low-energy spectral multipliers:

\displaystyle\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\,\lambda^{3}\,\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\bigl[R_{V}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{V}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\bigr]\,d\lambda,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\,\lambda\,\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\bigl[R_{V}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{V}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\bigr]\,d\lambda,

(5.2)

where
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda):=\chi_{1}(\lambda^{4})(\lambda>0)
is supported in
[0,(2\lambda_{0})^{1/4}]
.

Substituting the expansion (
2.13
) into the identity (
2.9
) yields:

R_{V}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})=R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})v\bigg(\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2}\lambda^{2-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}Q_{\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}\bigg)vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4}).

Inserting this representation back into (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), and recalling the free estimates from Remark
3.3
, the analysis is reduced to bounding a finite sum of perturbed integral kernels. Specifically, it suffices to estimate the kernel differences

\big(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}\big)(t,x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}\big)(t,x,y),

where the component kernels are explicitly given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y),Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\mp}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle\,d\lambda,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y),Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\mp}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle\,d\lambda,

(5.4)

with
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z},\ 0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\mathbf{k}+2\}.

In this section, we restrict our attention to the cases where zero is either a regular point or a first-kind resonance (i.e.,
\mathbf{k}\in\{0,1\}
). Our primary goal is to establish the following Theorem
5.1
:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-11-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is a regular point or a first-kind resonance of
H
. Then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\rightarrow L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\rightarrow L^{\infty}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

(5.5)

Moreover, in the weighted space setting
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
, the decay rate improves:

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\rightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\rightarrow L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|},\quad|t|\geq 2.

(5.6)

The proof proceeds in two steps: we first establish the unweighted
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimates (
5.5
), and then derive the weighted bounds (
5.6
).
Based on the reductions made above, this requires bounding the kernels
(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-})
and
(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-})
for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3
.

5.1.
The
L^{1}\rightarrow L^{\infty}
estimates

Recall from Theorem
2.7
that for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\Big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\neq(3,3),\\
|\log\lambda|\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)=(3,3).\end{cases}

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3
. Then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.8
, the resolvent expansion adjacent to the projection
Q_{j}
admits

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{j}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1-\delta_{j0}}\Omega_{j,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),

(5.7)

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{3}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{3,\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

(5.8)

where
\delta_{j0}=1
if
j=0
and
0
otherwise. Moreover, by (
2.21
) and (
2.23
), we have for
\ell=0,1,2,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\Omega_{j,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},

(5.9)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{J}_{3,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\,\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2}.

(5.10)

We distinguish two cases:
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(3,3)
and
(\alpha,\beta)=(3,3)
.
For the first case we use the expansion (
5.7
), and for the second case we use the expansion (
5.8
).

Case 1:
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(3,3)
.

Using the relation
1+\delta_{\alpha 0}=2-k_{\alpha}
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 3
(see (
2.14
)), we obtain

\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y),\;Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\mp}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle=\lambda^{k_{\alpha}+k_{\beta}-4}\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)\Omega_{\beta,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\;\Omega_{\alpha,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle.

Substituting this into (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), the factor
\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}
cancels exactly, yielding the canonical oscillatory integrals

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\,\lambda\,e^{\pm i\lambda r}\,\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\,\lambda^{-1}e^{\pm i\lambda r}\,\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

where
r=|x|+|y|
, and the common amplitude factor is defined by

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=e^{\mp i\lambda r}\,\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)\Omega_{\beta,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\;\Omega_{\alpha,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle.

In this case the operator satisfies
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}B^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,2
.
Applying Leibniz’s rule together with Hölder’s inequality gives, for
\ell=0,1,2
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}=\ell}

\displaystyle\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{1}}\bigl(\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)B^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}

\displaystyle\times\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{2}}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\Omega_{\beta,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{3}}\bigl(e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{\alpha,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}.

Combining this with the estimate (
5.9
) yields the amplitude factor bound

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\langle\lambda x\rangle^{-1/2}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\lesssim\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2).

Finally, applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
gives the uniform decay
O(\langle t\rangle^{-1})
.

Case 2:
(\alpha,\beta)=(3,3)
.

Here
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}B^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|
.
To neutralize this logarithmic divergence, we employ the expansion (
5.8
),
which in contrast to (
5.7
) provides an additional factor of
\lambda
while preserving the logarithmic singularity (see (
5.10
)).

Substituting the expansion (
5.8
) into (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), and noting that
\lambda^{4-k_{3}-k_{3}}=\lambda^{2}
, we again obtain the canonical oscillatory integral forms (
5.11
), where
r=|x|+|y|
and

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\lambda^{2}e^{\mp i\lambda r}\,\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{J}_{3,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\;\mathcal{J}_{3,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle.

Applying Leibniz’s rule, the
\lambda^{2}
factor neutralizes the combined logarithmic singularities. Specifically,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}=\ell}\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{1}}\bigl(\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)B^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}

\displaystyle\times\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{2}}\big(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{J}_{3,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\big)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell_{3}}\big(e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\mathcal{J}_{3,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{3}\langle\lambda x\rangle^{-1/2}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\lesssim\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2),

where in the last inequality we used
\lambda^{2}|\log\lambda|^{3}\lesssim 1
for
0<\lambda\ll 1
.
Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) once again yields the same
O(\langle t\rangle^{-1})
decay, which completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
The
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
estimates

We now demonstrate that an improved decay rate of
(t\log|t|)^{-1}
can be achieved by incorporating spatial weights.

Recall that
Q_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto the span of
v
(i.e.,
Q_{0}=P
); thus, it inherently lacks any cancellation against
v
. In contrast, for any
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
, at least one index is strictly positive. This allows us to exploit the exact cancellation property
Q_{\alpha}v=0
(or
Q_{\beta}v=0
) on at least one side, thereby neutralizing the highly singular leading-order term of the free resolvent
R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
.

Given this structural difference, we divide the analysis into the following two cases:

(I)

B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
(see Proposition
5.3
);

(II)

B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
(see Proposition
5.4
).

Recall from Theorem
2.7
that for
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
:

\displaystyle\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|,\quad\ell=0,1,2,

(5.12)

and

\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{-1}Q_{0}+\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda),

(5.13)

where
\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
satisfies

\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}

for
\ell=0,1,2.

To obtain the improved decay rates in the weighted setting, we use the following asymptotic expansion of the free resolvent from Lemma
2.1
:

R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}G_{0}(x,y)+E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}+E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

(5.14)

where
E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=a_{1}^{\pm}|x-y|^{2}+b_{0}|x-y|^{2}\log(\lambda|x-y|)+O_{2}\bigl(\lambda^{2}|x-y|^{4}\bigr).
For
0<\lambda\ll 1
and
\ell=0,1,2,
one can verify that

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell-}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}.

(5.15)

In this subsection, we set

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\lambda^{4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}\bigl\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigl(Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y),\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle,

(5.16)

then the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,\ \ \mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda.

(5.17)

Hence, we will focus on the estimate for
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)
to obtain the desired bound of
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
. Then for
|t|\geq 2,

\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|+\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|}.

Proof.

For
\alpha\geq 1
, the exact cancellation property
Q_{\alpha}v=0
(Remark
2.4
) ensures that the highly singular term
a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}
vanishes in (
5.14
), yielding

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y)\bigr)(x)=\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vE_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\bigr)(x).

Thus, applying (
5.15
), we obtain for
0<\lambda\ll 1
and
\ell=0,1,2
:

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|v\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\langle y\rangle^{4}\lambda^{-\ell-}.

Conversely, for
\alpha=0
(where no such cancellation occurs), the
a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}
term remains, then

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(Q_{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-2-\ell}\|v\|_{L^{2}}+\langle y\rangle^{4}\lambda^{-\ell-}\lesssim\langle y\rangle^{4}\lambda^{-2-\ell}.

Combine these bounds with the estimates (
5.12
) for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
and note that the worst case
occurs when exactly one of the indices
\alpha,\beta
is zero, then via Leibniz’s rule and Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{1/2-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4},\ \ \ell=0,1,2,\ \text{and }\ (\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0).

(5.18)

Integration by parts to
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)
(the boundary terms vanish) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{-1}\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr)\,d\lambda.

It follows directly from (
5.18
) that

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(\lambda^{-1}\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr)\bigr|\lesssim\lambda^{-3/2-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4},\qquad\ell=0,1.

Using the identity
\sin(t\lambda^{2})=(e^{it\lambda^{2}}-e^{-it\lambda^{2}})/(2i)
,
and applying Remark
10.2
(i) with
r(x,y)=0
and
(\sigma,\nu)=(3/2,0)
, then

\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|+\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}.

(5.19)

On the other hand, Proposition
5.2
provides the uniform estimate

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-1}.

Combining this with (
5.19
) via the inequality
\min(1,a/b)\lesssim\log a/\log b
(
a,b\geq 2
)
gives that

\min\Bigl(\frac{1}{|t|},\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{5/4}}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{|t|}\min\!\Bigl(1,\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{1/4}}\Bigr)\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},\qquad|t|\geq 2.

(5.20)

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Then for
|t|\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},\quad(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-})(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{8|t|}+O\left(\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|}\right).

(5.21)

Proof.

Substituting
R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}G_{0}(x,y)+E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)
into (
5.16
) and observing that
Q_{0}vG_{0}(\cdot,y)=(Q_{0}v)(\cdot)=v(\cdot)
, we separate an
x,y
-independent principal part and a remainder:

\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(a_{0}^{\pm})^{2}\bigl\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)v,v\bigr\rangle+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y).

Since
E_{1}^{\pm}
satisfies the bounds (
5.15
),
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}^{\pm}
obeys the estimate (
5.18
). By the same analysis as in Proposition
5.3
, its contribution to both kernels is bounded by
|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
.

For the principal part, using the asymptotic expansion (
5.13
) for
\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we obtain that

\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(a_{0}^{\pm})^{2}\bigl\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)v,v\bigr\rangle=a_{0}^{\pm}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}\bigl\langle Q_{0}v,v\bigr\rangle\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)+\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(a_{0}^{\pm})^{2}\bigl\langle\Gamma_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)v,v\bigr\rangle.

The second term on the right satisfies the bounds (
5.18
), ensuring that its contribution to both kernels is bounded by
|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
.

It remains to evaluate the integrals (
5.17
) with
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}=a_{0}^{\pm}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}\bigl\langle Q_{0}v,v\bigr\rangle\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)=a_{0}^{\pm}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)
. For the cosine kernel, by integrating by parts, we have

a_{0}^{\pm}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=O(|t|^{-N}),\ \ \forall N>0.

Combining this with the remainder bounds above gives
|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)|\lesssim|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
. Interpolating this with the uniform
|t|^{-1}
bound exactly as in (
5.20
) yields the desired weighted estimate for
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
.

For the sine kernel difference, extracting the explicit leading-order integral gives

\displaystyle(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-})(t,x,y)=(a_{0}^{+}-a_{0}^{-})\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda+O\Bigl(\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{5/4}}\Bigr).

Using the substitution
\rho=|t|\lambda^{2}
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}du=\frac{\pi}{2},
it follows that

\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=\frac{\pi}{4|t|}-\frac{1}{2|t|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin\rho}{\rho}\widetilde{\chi}_{2}\bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{|t|}}\bigr)\,d\rho=\frac{\pi}{4|t|}+O\bigl(\frac{1}{|t|^{2}}\bigr),

(5.22)

where an integration by parts shows that the
\rho
-integral is
O(|t|^{-1})
.
Combining this with
a_{0}^{+}-a_{0}^{-}=\frac{i}{4}
yields

(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-})(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{8|t|}+O\Bigl(\frac{\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}}{|t|^{5/4}}\Bigr).

Interpolating this spatial remainder with the uniform bound
|t|^{-1}
from Proposition
5.2
provides the desired asymptotic expansion.
∎

By Propositions
5.3
and
5.4
, the perturbed sine kernels satisfy the desired bound for all pairs, save for the contribution (
5.21
) (i.e., the case
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
).
Combining this with Proposition
3.4
, we see that the leading term
{1}/{(8|t|)}
of the free sine propagator is exactly annihilated by the perturbation term (
5.21
), yielding the weighted sine bound in (
5.6
). The cosine bound follows immediately, as both the free and perturbed cosine kernels
intrinsically exhibit the required decay.

6.
The second kind resonance case

This section focuses on the low-energy part for the second kind resonance case, i.e., Theorem
6.1
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-14-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is a second-kind resonance of
H
. We distinguish two cases:

•

If
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
and
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\rightarrow L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{2}.

(6.1)

In particular, the sine evolution exhibits the asymptotic behavior:

\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|},\quad|t|\geq 2.

•

If
Q_{4}^{0}=0
or
Q_{42}^{1}=0
, then the unweighted estimate (
6.1
) remains valid, and the following improved weighted estimate holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

(6.2)

Furthermore, when
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0

(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{4}=Q_{42}^{1})
, the unweighted bound improves to

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1},

(6.3)

and in the weighted setting
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
, for
|t|\geq 2,

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|},\ \ \bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

6.1.
The estimates for the case
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
and
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0

Building upon the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
dispersive bounds already established for the regular and first-kind resonance cases in Subsection
5.1
, we only need to bound the kernel differences
(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-})(t,x,y)
and
(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-})(t,x,y)
defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), restricting our attention to the operators
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with the index pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfying either
\alpha=4
with
0\leq\beta\leq 4
, or
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
with
\beta=4
.

Recall from Theorem
2.7
that for
\alpha=1,2,3
:

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{4},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Moreover, for pairs involving indices 0 and 4, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}+Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}+(\log\lambda)\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}

\displaystyle\quad+(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}

\displaystyle\quad+(\log\lambda)\bigl(Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}.

(6.4)

We categorize the operators
B^{\pm}(\lambda)
into two classes: the operators
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm},\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 3
(handled in Proposition
6.2
) and the operator
\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
which generates the slowest decay (handled in Proposition
6.3
). Moreover, we show the sharp bound for the sine evolution in Proposition
6.4
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. Then

|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)|+|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)|\lesssim\begin{cases}\langle t\rangle^{-1}\min\{\omega(x)\omega(y),\ \log(2+|t|)\},&\alpha=0,\\[4.0pt]
\langle t\rangle^{-1},&\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}.\end{cases}

Proof.

By Lemma
2.8
, the projected free resolvents admit the following low-frequency expansions:

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1-\delta_{\alpha 0}}\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),

(6.5)

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}(v|\cdot|^{2})+(b_{0}Q_{4}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),

(6.7)

where
\delta_{\alpha 0}=1
if
\alpha=0
and
0
otherwise.
By (
2.21
)–(
2.24
), the components satisfy the following derivative bounds for
\ell=0,1,2
:

\begin{split}\|(b_{0}Q_{4}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\omega(z),\quad\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{4}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\omega(z),\\
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}&+\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},\\
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2}.\end{split}

(6.8)

To prove the proposition, we divide the analysis into three parts based on the specified bounds.

Part (i): Establish the
\langle t\rangle^{-1}\omega(x)\omega(y)
bound for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
.

Consider
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
(the symmetric case
\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}
follows identically). Substituting (
6.5
) and the detailed expansion (
6.7
) into
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), we see that the highly singular
\lambda^{-2}
from the
Q_{0}
-expansion is exactly canceled by the explicit
\lambda^{2}
factor (since
4-k_{0}-k_{4}=2
) intrinsic to the integrals. The kernels can thus be decomposed into a finite sum of canonical oscillatory integrals:

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r_{j}(x,y)}\lambda\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,\\
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r_{j}(x,y)}\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda.\end{split}

Here, the amplitude factor is expanded into four distinct cross-terms based on (
6.5
) and (
6.7
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=b_{0}(\log\lambda)\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}(v|\cdot|^{2}),\,e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle r_{1}(x,y)=|x|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=b_{0}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)(Q_{4}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),\,e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle r_{2}(x,y)=|x|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{T}_{4}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\,e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle r_{3}(x,y)=|x|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\,e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle r_{4}(x,y)=|x|+|y|.

It remains to establish the bounds for these amplitude factors
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\pm}
. We first observe that
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}
is the only term with a logarithmic singularity on
\lambda
. We analyze this term individually. Recall that

\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1},\quad\text{with }\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\ell=0,1,2.

(6.9)

Substituting (
6.9
) into
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}
reveals a crucial cancellation. Since
Q_{4}^{1}(v|x|^{2})=0,
then

b_{0}(\log\lambda)\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1}Q_{4}(v|\cdot|^{2}),\,e^{\mp i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle=0.

Consequently,
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}
relies solely on the first term (with
(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}
), where the explicit
(\log\lambda)^{-1}
exactly neutralizes the
(\log\lambda)
multiplier of
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}
.

With the logarithmic singularity thus eliminated in
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\pm}
, and absent in the remaining terms, we can uniformly estimate all four terms. Applying Leibniz’s rule and Hölder’s inequality alongside the bounds in (
6.8
), we obtain

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle\lambda r_{j}\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell},\quad j=1,2,3,4,\ \text{and}\ \ell=0,1,2.

This satisfies the condition of Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
. Thus

\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|+\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}\omega(x)\omega(y).

Part (ii): Establish the
\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|)
bound for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
.

To remove the spatial weights, we utilize the compact representation (
6.6
) rather than (
6.7
). Proceeding as in Part (i), the kernels can be represented via the following canonical oscillatory integral:

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\lambda\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,\\
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,\end{split}

(6.10)

where
r(x,y)=|x|+|y|
and the unified amplitude factor is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y),\,e^{\pm i\lambda|x|}\Omega_{0,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle.

(6.11)

Since
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
, applying Leibniz’s rule and and Hölder’s inequality alongside the bound for
\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}
in (
6.8
) yields:

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|,\quad\ell=0,1,2.

This satisfies Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-1)
, which gives the unweighted bound:

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|).

Part (iii): Establish the uniform
\langle t\rangle^{-1}
bound for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Consider
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
(the symmetric case
\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}
follows identically). By (
6.5
) with
\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}
,

\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\mp}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,x)=\lambda^{-1}\Omega_{\alpha,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x).

When substituted into the oscillatory kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
, the
\lambda^{-1}
singularity is exactly neutralized by the inherent factor
\lambda
(since
4-k_{\alpha}-k_{4}=1
).
The kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
decompose analogously to (
6.10
), with the amplitude factor
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)
taking the exact form of (
6.11
), substituting
\Omega_{0,\mp}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}
with
\Omega_{\alpha,\mp}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}
, respectively.

Recall

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{4}
for
\ell=0,1,2,
then
applying Leibniz’s rule and Hölder’s inequality alongside (
6.8
), the additional
\lambda
factor absorbs the logarithmic singularities:

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{5}\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle\lambda r\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

This satisfies Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
, securing the unweighted bound:

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

This completes the proof. ∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}\bigl(\log(2+|t|)\bigr)^{2}.

(6.12)

In particular, for
|t|\geq 2
, the weighted asymptotic expansions satisfy:

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)\frac{\log|t|}{4i|t|}+{O}\bigl({\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|}^{-1}\bigr),\\
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{8i|t|}+{O}\bigl({\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|}^{-1}\log|t|\bigr),\end{split}

(6.13)

where the kernel
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)
is defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y):={}

\displaystyle b_{0}^{2}\langle\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2}),Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})\rangle+b_{0}^{2}\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})\rangle

\displaystyle+b_{0}^{2}\langle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2}),(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\rangle

\displaystyle+b_{0}^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\bigl\langle\mathcal{S}^{-1}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\bigr\rangle.

(6.14)

Proof.

The proof is divided into two parts: we first establish the uniform bound (
6.12
), and then derive the weighted asymptotic expansions (
6.13
). Since
4-k_{4}-k_{4}=0
, the intrinsic
\lambda
-factor in the definitions (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) is
\lambda^{0}=1
.

Part (i):
This part relies on the following representations derived in Lemma
2.8
:

\begin{split}\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)&=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),\\
\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)&=(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z).\end{split}

(6.15)

For
\ell=0,1,2
, these components satisfy the bounds:

\begin{split}\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},\quad\|(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,\\
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},\quad\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}.\end{split}

(6.16)

Note that the expansion for
Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
contains an
|\log\lambda|
singularity, whereas the expansion for
Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
does not. Moreover, none of the bounds in (
6.16
) carry the spatial weight
\omega(z)
.

By the formula (
6.1
) and the orthogonal sum
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{41}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}
, the operator
\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}+(\log\lambda)\bigl(Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1},

(6.17)

where
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)
denotes a generic operator in
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
(possibly varying at each occurrence) satisfying

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Upon substitution of (
6.15
) and (
6.17
) into (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
reduce to finite linear combinations of canonical oscillatory integrals of the form:

\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{-1}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

(6.18)

where the amplitude factor
\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}
is defined via the inner product:

\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=e^{\mp i\lambda r(x,y)}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle M_{sub}^{\pm}(\lambda)f(\lambda,\cdot,y),\,g(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle.

(6.19)

Here,
M_{sub}^{\pm}(\lambda)
is a constituent operator from (
6.17
), while
f
and
g
are corresponding states selected from the expansions in (
6.15
). The phase function
r(x,y)\in\{0,|x|,|y|,|x|+|y|\}
cancels the exponential phases embedded within
f
and
g
.

To uniformly bound (
6.18
), we apply Hölder’s inequality to (
6.19
), tracing the
\lambda
-logarithmic singularities inherited from each component:

•

For
M_{sub}^{\pm}(\lambda)=Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}
, it acts between the states
f=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}
and
g=\mathcal{J}_{4,\mp}
. The product of their individual
L^{2}
-bounds yields an amplitude factor constrained by
|\log\lambda|\cdot 1\cdot|\log\lambda|=|\log\lambda|^{2}
.

•

For
M_{sub}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}
(the symmetric case
(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}
follows identically),
the operator pairs
f\in\{b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0},\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2},\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}\}
and
g=\mathcal{J}_{4,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)
. The inherent logarithm in the operator multiplies the singularity of
g
, yielding a total
\lambda
-singularity of
|\log\lambda|\cdot 1\cdot|\log\lambda|=|\log\lambda|^{2}
.

•

For
M_{sub}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}
, this operator pairs with the bounded,
\log
-free
Q_{42}^{1}
-expansions on both sides, i.e.,
f,g\in\{b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0},\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2},\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}\}.
The
\lambda
-singularity arises entirely from the operator itself and is bounded by
|\log\lambda|^{2}\cdot 1\cdot 1=|\log\lambda|^{2}
.

Consequently, for all cross-terms, applying Leibniz’s rule and Hölder’s inequality to the net amplitude factor yields the unweighted bound

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle\lambda r(x,y)\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{2},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

An application of Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-2)
then yields the unweighted bound (
6.12
) for the integrals (
6.18
).

Part (ii): Establish the weighted asymptotic expansions (
6.13
).

Here, we require the following detailed, spatially-weighted resolvent expansions from Lemma
2.8
:

\begin{split}\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)&=b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})+(b_{0}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{0}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0}(\lambda,\cdot,z),\\
\bigl(Q_{41}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)&=(b_{0}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,1}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,1}(\lambda,\cdot,z),\\
\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)&=(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z).\end{split}

(6.20)

By (
2.22
)–(
2.24
), we have for
\ell=0,1,2,

\begin{split}&\|(b_{0}QvG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\omega(z)\ \text{with}\ Q=Q_{4}^{0},Q_{41}^{1}\ \text{and}\ \|(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,\\
&\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{T}_{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},\ \ \big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\omega(z),\end{split}

(6.21)

with
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}\in\{\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{0},\,\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,1},\ \mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}\}
and
\mathcal{T}\in\{\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0},\,\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,1},\ \mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}\}
.

we partition the operator into subdominant and dominant categories based on their singular behavior.

Case 1: Subdominant terms.

Consider the following terms in
\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

\begin{split}&(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0},\quad Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1},\quad Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0},\\
&Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1},\quad Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0},\quad Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1},\\
&(\log\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1},\quad(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1},\quad(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}.\end{split}

(6.22)

Similarly, upon substitution of (
6.20
) and the term
B^{\pm}(\lambda)
from (
6.22
) into the integral kernels (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
expand into finite linear combinations of canonical oscillatory integrals of the same form as (
6.18
).

Considering that in (
6.20
) only the term
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})
carries the
(\log\lambda)
singularity, and combining this with (
6.21
) and (
6.22
), for all cross-terms, applying Leibniz’s rule and Hölder’s inequality to the corresponding amplitude factor yields

\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{f,g}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2)\langle\lambda r(x,y)\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\,\omega(x)\omega(y),\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-1)
guarantees that all contributions from this class are absorbed into the bound
{O}\bigl(\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|)\bigr)
.

Case 2: Dominant terms.

The exact asymptotic expansions are driven exclusively by the four principal operators generating
(\log\lambda)^{2}
singularities:

Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0},\quad(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}Q_{42}^{1},\quad(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0},\quad(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}.

To maximize the logarithmic singularity, these operators must rigidly pair with the spatial states that do not carry an extra
\lambda
decay. Given the precise structure of the
Q_{4}^{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
and
Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
expansions in (
6.20
), the dominant amplitude components strictly evaluate as follows:

•

The operator
Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}
pairs with
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})
from both expansions, yielding the amplitude factor:

b_{0}^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\langle Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2}),\,Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})\rangle.

•

For
(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}Q_{42}^{1}
, since
the remainders
\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}
and
\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}
in the
Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
expansion carry explicit
\lambda
decay, the
y
-variable state is forced to pair with
(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y)
, while the
x
-variable state pairs with
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})
. The resulting amplitude factor is:

b_{0}^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\langle Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),\,Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})\rangle.

•

Symmetrically, the operator
(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}
contributes the amplitude factor:

b_{0}^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\langle Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2}),\,(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\rangle.

•

Finally, the intrinsic
(\log\lambda)^{2}
factor in
(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}
dictates that maximum growth is attained if and only if
Q_{42}^{1}
pairs with
(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})
on both sides. This yields:

b_{0}^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\langle\mathcal{S}^{-1}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),\,(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\rangle.

Summing these dominant contributions (all possessing
r(x,y)=0
), and recognizing that all other cross-terms exhibit at most
|\log\lambda|
singularities falling into Case 1, we finally derive that for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda),

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\frac{2}{\pi i}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)I(t)+{O}\bigl(\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|)\bigr),\\
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)&=\frac{2}{\pi i}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)J(t)+\bigl(\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|)\bigr).\end{split}

(6.23)

The temporal integrals are given by:

\displaystyle I(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(\log\lambda)^{2}\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(\log\lambda)^{2}\,d\lambda.

By Lemma
10.3
, these integrals admit the precise asymptotic behaviors for
|t|\geq 2
:

\displaystyle I(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{8}\frac{\log|t|}{|t|}+{O}\big(|t|^{-1}\big),\quad\text{and}\quad J(t)=\frac{\pi}{16}\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|}+{O}\Big(\frac{\log|t|}{|t|}\Big).

Substituting these evaluations into (
6.23
), we obtain (
6.13
). This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we are devoted to the proof of the sharp bound for the sine evolution.

Proposition 6.4
.

If
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
and
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0,
then

\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|},\ \ |t|\geq 2.

(6.24)

Proof.

Collating the results from Propositions
6.2
–
6.3
and the regular and first-kind resonance cases (Subsection
5.1
), we derive the following asymptotic expansions in the weighted space
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}:

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)=-\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{8i|t|}(\mathcal{A}^{+}-\mathcal{A}^{-})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(|t|^{-1}\log|t|\right),\ \ |t|\geq 2,

(6.25)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}
is the integral operator with the kernel
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y).

From the expression (
6.3
) for the kernel
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(x,y)
and the fact that
G_{2}(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
satisfies
(Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2})(x,y)=Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)
, we derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{+}-\mathcal{A}^{-}

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{2}G_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{+}-\mathcal{C}^{-})Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}+b_{0}^{2}G_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{+}-\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-})Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle+b_{0}^{2}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{+}-\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-})Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}+b_{0}^{2}\bigl[(a_{2}^{+})^{-1}-(a_{2}^{-})^{-1}\bigr]G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0}.

Consequently, by (
6.25
), the proof of (
6.24
) reduces to verifying that
\mathcal{A}^{+}-\mathcal{A}^{-}
is not identically zero.

Introduce the operators

\mathcal{W}_{1}=(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1},\quad A=G_{2}vQ_{4}^{0},\quad\mathcal{B}=G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1},

and substitute the expressions defined in (
2.19
) for
\mathcal{C}^{\pm},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}
, then we derive

\mathcal{A}^{+}-\mathcal{A}^{-}=b_{0}^{2}\bigl[(a_{2}^{+})^{-1}-(a_{2}^{-})^{-1}\bigr](A\mathcal{W}_{1}-\mathcal{B})\mathcal{S}^{-1}(A\mathcal{W}_{1}-\mathcal{B})^{*}.

Since
b_{0}^{2}[(a_{2}^{+})^{-1}-(a_{2}^{-})^{-1}]\neq 0
and
\mathcal{S}^{-1}
is positive definite on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, the problem reduces to demonstrating that the operator
A\mathcal{W}_{1}-\mathcal{B}
is injective on the finite-dimensional space
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
.

For any
\Psi\in Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
, orthogonality dictates that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y^{\alpha}v(y)\Psi(y)dy=0
for
|\alpha|\leq 1
. Recalling
G_{2}(x,y)=|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-2x\cdot y
, we see that
A
maps
\Psi
to a constant:

(A\Psi)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|y|^{2}v(y)\Psi(y)\,dy=:C(\Psi).

Thus, setting
\Psi=\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi\in Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
for any
\psi\in Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, we obtain
(A\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi)(x)\equiv C(\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi)
.

Conversely, we claim
\lim_{|x|\to\infty}(\mathcal{B}\psi)(x)=0
. Indeed,
(\mathcal{B}\psi)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G_{2}^{0}(x,y)(vQ_{42}^{1}\psi)(y)dy
. The properties of
Q_{42}^{1}
enforce the cancellation conditions
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y^{\alpha}v(y)\psi(y)dy=0
for
|\alpha|\leq 2
. Consequently, applying Lemma
8.2
yields:

|(\mathcal{B}\psi)(x)|\lesssim\|vQ_{42}^{1}\psi\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\langle x\rangle^{-1}(1+\log\langle x\rangle)\xrightarrow{|x|\to\infty}0.

Now, assume
(A\mathcal{W}_{1}-\mathcal{B})\psi=0
for some
\psi\in Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
. Taking the limit
|x|\to\infty
, we find:

(A\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi)(x)\equiv C(\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi)=\lim_{|x|\to\infty}(A\mathcal{W}_{1}\psi)(x)=\lim_{|x|\to\infty}(\mathcal{B}\psi)(x)=0.

This forces
\mathcal{B}\psi\equiv 0
, meaning
G_{2}^{0}v\psi=0
. Applying
\Delta^{2}
in the distributional sense directly implies
v\psi=0
. By Proposition
8.1
(ii) and the inclusion
\psi\in Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}\subset S_{3}L^{2}
, there exists
\phi\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\psi=Uv\phi
. Hence
V\phi=v\psi=0
, requiring
\phi
to vanish on the support of
V
, which finally yields
\psi=Uv\phi=0
.

Therefore,
A\mathcal{W}_{1}-\mathcal{B}
is injective, then
\mathcal{A}^{+}-\mathcal{A}^{-}
represents a non-trivial operator, concluding the proof of optimality.
∎

6.2.
The estimates for the case
Q_{4}^{0}=0
or
Q_{42}^{1}=0

Recall from Theorem
2.7
that for
\alpha=1,2,3
,

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{4},\quad\ell=0,1,2;

for the pairs
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,4),(4,0),(4,4)
,

•

if
Q_{4}^{0}=0
, then
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{1}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1},\ \ \mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\ \ \mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1}.

(6.26)

•

If
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
and
Q_{42}^{1}=0
, then
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{0}\oplus Q_{41}^{1}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\sum_{h,l\in\{0,1\}}(\log\lambda)^{-(2-h-l)}Q_{4}^{h}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{l}.

(6.27)

We now turn to the estimate (
6.2
). Given the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
dispersive bounds established for the regular and first-kind resonance cases in Subsection
5.1
, it remains to prove Proposition
6.5
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
. Then

\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}\min\big\{\omega(x)\omega(y),\;(\log(2+|t|))^{2}\big\}.

Proof.

For
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, the desired estimates are already covered by the arguments in Proposition
6.2
. Hence, it suffices to consider the case
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Under the hypothesis
Q_{4}^{0}=0
or
Q_{42}^{1}=0
, the expansions (
6.26
) and (
6.27
) show that the operator satisfies the bound
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,2
, with no
\log\lambda
singularities.
To establish the desired estimates, we proceed as in Proposition
6.3
.

Unweighted bound.

We employ the compact expansion
\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)
. As in part (i) of Proposition
6.3
,
\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}
carries a
|\log\lambda|
singularity and introduces no spatial weights. Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-2)
then yields the unweighted estimate
{O}\big(\langle t\rangle^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{2}\big)
.

Weighted bound.

To eliminate the logarithmic growth, we invoke the refined expansions for
Q_{4}^{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
and
Q_{4}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
from Lemma
2.8
(iii). Any
\lambda
-logarithmic divergence in the amplitude factor necessarily originates from the term
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})
.
Crucially, the algebraic structures of (
6.26
) and (
6.27
) guarantee that every occurrence of the projection
Q_{4}^{0}
is paired with a compensating factor
(\log\lambda)^{-1}
. This exactly neutralizes the
\lambda
-logarithmic growth from
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})
. Conversely, the expansions of
Q_{4}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
are free of
\lambda
-logarithmic singularities.
As a result, the amplitude factors governing
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
are entirely free of
\lambda
-logarithmic divergences as
\lambda\to 0
. An application of Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
then yields the weighted bound
{O}\big(\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle t\rangle^{-1}\big)
.

Combining the unweighted and weighted bounds completes the proof.
∎

6.3.
The estimates for the subcase
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0

This condition
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0
(i.e.,
Q_{4}=Q_{42}^{1}
) allows us to establish an unweighted
|t|^{-1}
decay (i.e., (
6.3
)) and the following weighted estimates:

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|},\quad\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

We divide the analysis into two parts, starting with the unweighted
|t|^{-1}
estimate.

Part I. The
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
estimate.

Recall that Proposition
6.5
gives the weighted bound
\omega(x)\omega(y)\langle t\rangle^{-1}
. The spatial weight
\omega
arose exclusively because the
L^{2}
bounds (
2.24
) for
\mathcal{T}_{4},\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0},\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1},
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,1}
(associated with
Q_{4},Q_{4}^{0},Q_{4}^{1},
and
Q_{41}^{1}
) inherently carry the weight
\omega
.

In the present subcase, since
Q_{4}=Q_{42}^{1}
, Lemma
2.8
(iii) simplifies the resolvent expansion to:

\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z).

Crucially, all terms in this expansion satisfy the
L^{2}
bounds free of
\omega(z)
. Specifically,

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2,

while
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2}
and
\bigl\|(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
. Because the entire expansion is free of
\omega(z)
, we obtain the following Proposition
6.6
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
. If
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0
, then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

Combining Proposition
6.6
with the
L^{1}\to L^{\infty}
dispersive bounds established for the regular and first-kind resonance cases in Subsection
5.1
, we obtain the estimate (
6.3
).

Part II. Weighted
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
estimate.

In this part, building upon the
L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
estimates established for the regular and first-kind resonance cases in Subsection
5.2
, we need to consider not only
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
, but also
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, as the leading term in the expansion of
\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
undergoes a change.

Analogous to the previous arguments in Proposition
5.3
, we can obtain that for
|t|\geq 2,

\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},\ B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}\ \text{with}\ \alpha\in\{1,2,3\}.

(6.28)

The analysis is thus restricted to

B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{0,4\}\times\{0,4\}
. When
\mathbf{k}\geq 2
and
Q_{4}^{0}=0
, by Theorem
2.7
, these operators admit

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}+\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\{0,4\},

where the operators
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
are defined in (
2.18
) and are independent of
\lambda
. In particular,

\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{\pm},

and the condition
Q_{4}=Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
yields that

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{\pm}\neq 0.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{0,4\}\times\{0,4\}
. If
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0
, then

\bigl|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|},\quad\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{2i|t|}C^{\pm}_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)+{O}\!\left(\frac{\omega(x)\omega(y)}{|t|\log|t|}\right),\ \ |t|\geq 2,

where the integral kernels
C^{\pm}_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)

are uniformly bounded and explicitly given by:

\begin{split}C^{\pm}_{0,0}(x,y)&=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{2}\Bigl\langle\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}(Q_{0}vG_{0})(\cdot),(Q_{0}vG_{0})(\cdot)\Bigr\rangle,\\
C^{\pm}_{0,4}(x,y)&=b_{0}a_{0}^{\pm}\Bigl\langle\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{0}vG_{0})(\cdot)\Bigr\rangle,\\
C^{\pm}_{4,0}(x,y)&=b_{0}a_{0}^{\pm}\Bigl\langle\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}(Q_{0}vG_{0})(\cdot),(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\Bigr\rangle,\\
C^{\pm}_{4,4}(x,y)&=b_{0}^{2}\Bigl\langle\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\Bigr\rangle.\end{split}

Proof.

For the case
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
, the estimates are obtained by replacing

(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{-1}Q_{0}
with

\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}
in
Proposition
5.4
.

For the remaining cases involving the index
4
, we utilize the expansion (
5.14
) for
R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)
:

R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}(x,y)=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}G_{0}(x,y)+g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)|x-y|^{2}+\widetilde{E}_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y),

where the remainder satisfies
\widetilde{E}_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=O_{2}(\lambda^{2}|x-y|^{4})
.
Crucially, the exact cancellation property
Q_{42}^{1}(x^{\gamma}v)=0
for
|\gamma|\leq 2
annihilates the first two terms. Thus, applying
Q_{42}^{1}v
to the expansion yields an expression governed entirely by the
\lambda^{2}
remainder:

\bigl[Q_{42}^{1}v\bigl(R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}\bigr)(\cdot,y)\bigr](x)=\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}v\widetilde{E}_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\bigr)(x)=O_{2}(\lambda^{2}|x-y|^{4}).

Consequently, the dominant low-energy singularity arises from the product of three components:
b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0},

a_{0}^{\pm}\lambda^{-2}Q_{0}vG_{0}
and the leading term
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

All other cross terms gain a relative energy factor of at least
\lambda^{1-}
.
Employing the same oscillatory integral strategy as in Proposition
5.3
, these remainder combinations lead to a rapid temporal decay of at least
|t|^{-5/4}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
, which is readily bounded by
\omega(x)\omega(y)(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
via (
5.20
).

Extracting the leading singular constituents perfectly recovers the spatial kernels
C^{\pm}_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
, which are uniformly bounded since
\|(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
. For the sine evolution, this kernel
C^{\pm}_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
couples with the following integral (see (
5.22
)):

\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=\frac{1}{2i|t|}+{O}(|t|^{-2}).

Conversely, the corresponding integral for the cosine evolution decays rapidly:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=O(|t|^{-N}),\quad\forall N>0.

This directly establishes the desired bound and asymptotic expansions, completing the proof.
∎

It remains to complete the following proof of the sharp bound for the sine evolution.

Proposition 6.8
.

If
Q_{4}^{0}=Q_{41}^{1}=0,
then

\Big\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1},\ \ |t|\geq 2.

(6.29)

Proof.

Following the framework established for the regular and first-kind resonance cases in Subsection
5.2
, and using the estimates from (
6.28
) and in Proposition
6.7
, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)=-\frac{1}{2i|t|}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}\right),

(6.30)

in the sense of
\mathbb{B}(L^{1}_{\omega},L^{\infty}_{-\omega})
, where the bounded operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is defined as

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=&G_{0}vQ_{0}\bigl((a_{0}^{+})^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{+}-(a_{0}^{-})^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{-}\bigr)Q_{0}vG_{0}+b_{0}G_{0}vQ_{0}\bigl(a_{0}^{+}\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{+}-a_{0}^{-}\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{-}\bigr)Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0}\\
&+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\bigl(a_{0}^{+}\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{+}-a_{0}^{-}\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{-}\bigr)Q_{0}vG_{0}+b_{0}^{2}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{+}-\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{-}\bigr)Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0}.\end{split}

(6.31)

Here
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{+}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
are given in (
2.16
) and (
2.18
). Note that we use
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{\pm}
rather than
\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}
in (
6.31
), because the
Q_{0}
-component
(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}
neutralizes the leading term
{1}/{(8|t|)}
from
(\sin(t\Delta))/({t\Delta})\chi_{1}(\Delta)
, exactly as in the regular and first-kind resonance cases.
By (
6.30
), to establish (
6.29
), it suffices to prove
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\neq 0
.

By the expressions (
2.16
) and (
2.18
) of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0,0}^{+}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
into (
6.31
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\bigl(G_{0}vQ_{0}\mathcal{W}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\bigr)\bigl((\bm{d}^{+})^{-1}-(\bm{d}^{-})^{-1}\bigr)\bigl(G_{0}vQ_{0}\mathcal{W}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\bigr)^{*}.

Here considering
Q_{4}=Q_{42}^{1},
we have

\mathcal{W}=\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1},\ \ \ \ (\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}=-a_{0}^{\pm}\|V\|_{L^{1}}\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1}\bigr)^{-1}.

Note that

i\bigl((\bm{d}^{+})^{-1}-(\bm{d}^{-})^{-1}\bigr)
is strictly positive definite on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
. Consequently, in order to show
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\neq 0
, it reduces to proving
G_{0}vQ_{0}\mathcal{W}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}
is injective on the finite-dimensional space
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
.

Assume
\bigl(G_{0}vQ_{0}\mathcal{W}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\bigr)\psi=0
for some
\psi\in Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
. Then,

b_{0}\bigl(G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}\psi\bigr)(x)=\langle\mathcal{W}\psi,v\rangle.

By Lemma
8.2
, the left-hand side vanishes as
|x|\to\infty
. Thus, taking the limit on both sides forces the constant right-hand side to be zero:
\langle\mathcal{W}\psi,v\rangle=0
. Then
\mathcal{W}\psi\in S_{0}L^{2}\cap Q_{0}L^{2}=\{0\}.

By the definition of
Q_{42}^{1}
, the equality
\mathcal{W}\psi=\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1}\psi=0
necessitates
\psi=0
.

Thus, the operator
G_{0}vQ_{0}\mathcal{W}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vQ_{42}^{1}
is injective on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, confirming
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\neq 0
.
∎

7.
The third kind resonance and eigenvalue cases

This section focuses on the low-energy part for the third kind resonance and eigenvalue cases, that is, the following Theorems
7.1
and
7.2
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-18-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is either a third-kind resonance or an eigenvalue accompanied by a d-wave resonance
(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{5}\neq 0)
. Then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\sim\frac{1}{\log|t|},\quad|t|\gg 1.

(7.1)

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-22-}
.
Assume that
H
has no positive embedded eigenvalues and that zero is an eigenvalue of
H
but exhibits no d-wave resonance
(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{5}=0)
.

(1)

If
H
admits a p-wave resonance
(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{4}\neq 0)
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}{(\log(2+|t|))^{2}}.

(2)

If
H
lacks a p-wave resonance
(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{4}=0)
, then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

Furthermore, we distinguish two subcases for the weighted estimate when
|t|\geq 2
:

•

If
{S}_{5}L^{2}\neq\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
, then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|},\ \ \bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\sim|t|^{-1}.

•

If
{S}_{5}L^{2}=\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
, then

\left\|\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}+\bigg\|\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|t|\log|t|}.

Here, the subspace characterizing the isotropic property is defined as:

\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}=\left\{\psi\in S_{5}L^{2}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &\langle x_{1}^{3}x_{2}v,\psi\rangle=0,\quad\langle x_{1}x_{2}^{3}v,\psi\rangle=0,\\
&\langle x_{1}^{4}v,\psi\rangle=\langle x_{2}^{4}v,\psi\rangle=3\langle x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}v,\psi\rangle\end{aligned}\right\}.

(7.2)

7.1.
Estimates for the case
Q_{5}\neq 0

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.1
. Assume that zero is either a third-kind resonance or an eigenvalue with a
d
-wave resonance. Following the strategy from the previous sections, it suffices to estimate the integral kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}
defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), restricted to the operators
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
where the indices include either
\alpha\in\{5,6\}
or
\beta\in\{5,6\}
.

Recall from Theorem
2.7
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda),\ (\alpha,\beta)=(5,5),(5,6),(6,5),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),

with

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}+\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\ \ell=0,1,2,\ \text{and}\ \alpha,\beta=5,6.

Moreover, for
\alpha=5,6,\ 0\leq\beta\leq 4
and
\ell=0,1,2,

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}+\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}\left(L^{2}\right)}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}.

Therefore it suffices to analyze
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}
for the following cases:

•

B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\ \text{with}\ (\alpha,\beta)=(5,5),(5,6),(6,5),(6,6);

•

B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\ \text{with}\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\{\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid\alpha=5,6,\ 0\leq\beta\leq 4\ \text{or}\ \beta=5,6,\ 0\leq\alpha\leq 4\}.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
\alpha,\beta\in\{5,6\}
. Then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\log(2+|t|)}.

Proof.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\{5,6\}
, the operators
B^{\pm}(\lambda)
are decomposed into a
\pm
-independent principal term
(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)
(or
\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)
) and a
\pm
-dependent remainder:
(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. We analyze the integral kernels generated by these two parts separately.

Recall from Lemma
2.8
(iii) that

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{j}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{j}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda^{m_{j}}\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{j}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{j,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr),\quad j=5,6,

(7.3)

where
m_{5}=1
and
m_{6}=2
. By (
2.22
)–(
2.24
), one has

\begin{split}\|b_{0}Q_{j}vG_{2}^{0}(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim 1,\\
\big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|z|}\mathcal{T}_{j,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr)\big\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda z\rangle^{-1/2},\ \ \big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{j}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\ \ j=5,6.\end{split}

By (
7.3
), the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) decompose into finite sums of canonical oscillatory integrals of the form:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\cos(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{\pi it}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{-1}\sin(t\lambda^{2})e^{\pm i\lambda r(x,y)}\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda,

(7.4)

where the common amplitude factor
\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)
is defined by
