\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)=e^{\mp i\lambda r(x,y)}\lambda^{\gamma}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle B^{\pm}(\lambda)f(\lambda,\cdot,y),g(\lambda,\cdot,x)\big\rangle.

(7.5)

Here,
f,g\in\{b_{0}Q_{j}vG_{2}^{0},\mathcal{T}_{j,\pm},\mathcal{T}_{j}\}
for
j\in\{5,6\}
. Moreover,
r(x,y)
isolates the inherent spatial oscillations of
f
and
g
: it contributes
|y|
(resp.
|x|
) if the state is
\mathcal{T}_{j,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{j,\mp}(\lambda,\cdot,x)
), and contributes
0
otherwise. The exponent
\gamma
is given by
\gamma=4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}+p=-2+p
for
\alpha,\beta\in\{5,6\}
, where
p\geq 0
is the total
\lambda
-power contributed by
f
and
g
. For example if
(f,g)=(b_{0}Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0},\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\mp}),
then
r=|x|
,
p=m_{\alpha}.

Case 1: The
\pm
-independent principal terms
(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\beta}
(or
\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)
).

For these terms,
B^{+}(\lambda)=B^{-}(\lambda):=B(\lambda)
. The kernel difference
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}
can be
rewritten as a sum of terms involving
the resolvent difference:

\displaystyle\bigl[R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{\alpha}B(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)-\bigl[R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{\alpha}B(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)

\displaystyle=\bigl[(R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4}))vQ_{\alpha}B(\lambda)Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)+\big[R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{\alpha}B(\lambda)Q_{\beta}v(R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4}))\bigr](x,y).

By (
7.3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\bigl[Q_{\alpha}v\bigl(R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)\bigr](\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{m_{\alpha}}\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,+}(\lambda,\cdot,z)-\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,-}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr).

(7.6)

Consequently, in the amplitude factor (
7.5
), at least one state in the pairing
(f,g)
must be a
\mathcal{T}_{j,\pm}
remainder with
j\in\{5,6\}
, which guarantees a factor
\lambda^{m_{j}}
(
m_{j}\geq 1
).
Thus, we deduce that
p\geq 1
, yielding
\gamma\geq-1
.

The most singular amplitude factor (
7.5
) occurs when
p=1
. Since
m_{5}=1
and
m_{6}=2
, this requires
\mathcal{T}_{5,+}-\mathcal{T}_{5,-}
to be paired with
b_{0}Q_{j}vG_{2}^{0}
or
\mathcal{T}_{j}
(
j\in\{5,6\}
) (e.g.,
f=\mathcal{T}_{5,\pm}
,
g=b_{0}Q_{j}vG_{2}^{0}
, yielding
r(x,y)=|y|
and
B(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{5,6}
or
(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{6,5}
). Such amplitude factor satisfies:

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\bigr|\lesssim\langle\lambda r(x,y)\rangle^{-1/2}\lambda^{-1-\ell}|\log\lambda|^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2),\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(1,1)
yields the bound
\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}(\log(2+|t|))^{-1}
.

In particular, when we take
B(\lambda)=\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)
, the expansion (
7.6
) guarantees a factor
\lambda^{2}
(note that
m_{6}=2
), yielding
\gamma\geq 0.
Hence, we can apply Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
to obtain that

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1},\ \text{with}\ B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda).

(7.7)

Case 2: The
\pm
-dependent remainder terms
(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Since these operators depend on
\pm
, the algebraic identity (
7.6
) is inapplicable. Thus we evaluate the kernels directly using the expansion (
7.3
). The dominant contribution to the decay rate arises from
f=b_{0}Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0}
and
g=b_{0}Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0}
.

In this scenario, no
\lambda
-powers are gained (
p=0
, hence
\gamma=-2
), and
r(x,y)=0
. Incorporating the operator norm
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
, the corresponding amplitude factor obeys:

|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{fg}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)|\lesssim|\log\lambda|^{-2}\lambda^{-2-\ell}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda/2),\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(2,2)
dictates the slowest decay rate
(\log(2+|t|))^{-1}
. All other cross-pairings contain remainder powers (
p\geq 1
), which yield the bound
\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}(\log(2+|t|))^{-2}
by Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(1,2).

Summing the bounds from Part 1 and Part 2 concludes the proof. In particular, we derive
for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
\alpha,\beta\in\{5,6\}
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}K_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)+O\bigl(\langle t\rangle^{-{1}/{2}}(\log(2+|t|))^{-1}\bigr),

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}\right)(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}N_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)+O\bigl(\langle t\rangle^{-{1}/{2}}(\log(2+|t|))^{-1}\bigr),

where

\begin{split}K_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)&=b_{0}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}(\log\lambda)^{-2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)(Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\big\rangle d\lambda,\\
N_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)&=\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-3}(\log\lambda)^{-2}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\big\langle\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)(Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\big\rangle d\lambda,\end{split}

(7.8)

satisfying
|K_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)|+|N_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)|\lesssim(\log(2+|t|))^{-1},
uniformly in
x,y
.
∎

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
\alpha=5,6
,
0\leq\beta\leq 4
, or
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
,
\beta=5,6
. Then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim{\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}}\log(2+|t|).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we consider
\alpha\in\{5,6\}
and
0\leq\beta\leq 4
(the symmetric case follows identically). For the resolvent adjacent to
Q_{5}
or
Q_{6}
, we employ the expansion (
7.3
).
For the resolvent adjacent to
Q_{4}
and
Q_{\beta}
with
\beta\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, we use the following expansions, respectively:

\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),\quad\bigl(Q_{\beta}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=\lambda^{-1-\delta_{\beta 0}}\Omega_{\beta,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z),

where
\delta_{\beta 0}=1
if
\beta=0
and
\delta_{\beta 0}=0
otherwise.

Following the same method as before, we obtain the canonical oscillatory integral forms (
7.4
).
Moreover,
recall the operator estimates
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,2,
and the crucial exponents are as follows:

4-k_{\alpha}-k_{0}=1\;\;(\alpha=5,6),\quad 4-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}=0\;\;(\alpha=5,6,\;\beta=1,2,3),\quad 4-k_{\alpha}-k_{4}=-1\;\;(\alpha=5,6).

These dictate the behavior of the amplitude factor, leading to
the following estimates.

(i) For
0\leq\alpha\leq 3
,
\beta=5,6
or
\alpha=5,6
,
0\leq\beta\leq 3
, Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(1,0)
yields

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}.

(ii) For
\alpha=4
,
\beta=5,6
or
\alpha=5,6
,
\beta=4
, Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(1,-1)
gives

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}\log(2+|t|).

∎

It remains to establish the sharpness of the
(\log|t|)^{-1}
bound.
Synthesizing the preceding analysis, it is evident that for
|t|\gg 1
,
the decay rate is determined by the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)
and
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)
defined in (
7.8
).
To derive the asymptotic expansion, we invoke Theorem
2.7
to decompose the operator
\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda).

This decomposition isolates the dominant singularity:

•

Replacing
\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
in (
7.8
) yields an improved decay rate of
(\log(2+|t|))^{-3/2}
, obtained via Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(2,5/2)
.

•

The term
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
in
\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
produces the slowest decay
(\log(2+|t|))^{-1}
, given by Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(2,2)
. And by Remark
10.2
, this rate comes from low-frequency region
|t|\lambda^{2}\ll 1
, while high-frequency region
|t|\lambda^{2}\gtrsim 1
yields the faster
(\log(2+|t|))^{-2}
decay.

By isolating this primary low-frequency contribution (specifically, the regime
|t|\lambda^{2}\ll 1
), we deduce the asymptotic expansion in
\mathbb{B}(L^{1},L^{\infty})
:

\displaystyle\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{6}T_{\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)+{O}\Big((\log|t|)^{-3/2}\Big),

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{6}T_{\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)+{O}\Big((\log|t|)^{-3/2}\Big),

where
T_{\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)
and
T_{\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)
are integral operators defined by the respective kernels:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})(\log\lambda)^{-2}\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle={c_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)}{t}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-3}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})(\log\lambda)^{-2}\,d\lambda,

with
c_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=b_{0}^{2}\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{+}-\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{-})(Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y),(Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,x)\big\rangle
.
Here the summation over
\alpha,\beta\in\{5,6\}
accommodates the third-kind resonance scenario, where
Q_{6}=0
implies
c_{\alpha,\beta}\equiv 0
for all pairs
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(5,5)
.

Thus, establishing the optimal bounds in (
7.1
) reduces to proving the following Proposition
7.5
.

Proposition 7.5
.

For
|t|\gg 1
, the leading operators satisfy the lower bounds:

\Big\|\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{6}T_{\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log|t|},\quad\Big\|\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{6}T_{\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)\Big\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log|t|}.

(7.9)

Proof.

By continuous embeddings
L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{2,-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\sigma>1
, it is sufficient to construct a specific test function
\varphi\in L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that:

\Big|\Big\langle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{\mathbf{k}+2}T_{\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)\varphi,\varphi\Big\rangle\Big|\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log|t|},\quad\text{and}\quad\Big|\Big\langle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=5}^{\mathbf{k}+2}T_{\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,\beta}}(t)\varphi,\varphi\Big\rangle\Big|\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log|t|}.

(7.10)

Under the hypothesis that zero is a third-kind resonance or an eigenvalue accompanied by a
d
-wave resonance, the subspace
Q_{5}L^{2}\neq\{0\}
. We fix
\psi\in Q_{5}L^{2}
and explicitly construct our test function as
\varphi:=vU\psi=-b_{0}VG_{2}^{0}v\psi
. By Lemma
8.2
,
the estimate
|\varphi(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu-1}(1+\log\langle x\rangle)
guarantees that
\varphi\in L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Since
Q_{5}T_{0}=0
, we have
Q_{5}(U+b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v)U\psi=0
, which dictates that

b_{0}Q_{5}vG_{2}^{0}\varphi=b_{0}Q_{5}vG_{2}^{0}vU\psi=-Q_{5}\psi=-\psi.

Furthermore, by identical logic, using
Q_{6}T_{0}=0
and the orthogonality of
\psi\in Q_{5}L^{2}
to
Q_{6}L^{2}
, we have

b_{0}Q_{6}vG_{2}^{0}\varphi=b_{0}Q_{6}vG_{2}^{0}vU\psi=-Q_{6}\psi=0.

Consequently,
\langle c_{\alpha,\beta}(\cdot,*)\varphi(*),\varphi(\cdot)\rangle=b_{0}^{2}\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{+}-\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{-})(Q_{\beta}vG_{2}^{0})\varphi,(Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})\varphi\big\rangle=0

for all
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(5,5)
.
The only surviving term corresponds to
(\alpha,\beta)=(5,5)
. Utilizing the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{5,5}^{\pm}=-a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}^{-2}(Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1}
on
Q_{5}L^{2}
(defined in (
2.20
)) and the fact that
\text{Im}(a_{3}^{\pm})\neq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle c_{5,5}(\cdot,*)\varphi(*),\varphi(\cdot)\rangle

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{2}\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{5,5}^{+}-\mathcal{L}_{5,5}^{-})Q_{5}vG_{2}^{0}\varphi,Q_{5}vG_{2}^{0}\varphi\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-(a_{3}^{+}-a_{3}^{-})b_{1}^{-2}\big\langle(Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1}\psi,\psi\big\rangle:=c_{0}\neq 0.

Finally, evaluating the temporal integrals for
|t|\gg 1
, we explicitly extract the logarithmic decay:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})(\log\lambda)^{-2}\,d\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\geq\cos 1\int_{0}^{|t|^{-1/2}\lambda_{0}^{1/2}}\lambda^{-1}(\log\lambda)^{-2}\,d\lambda\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log|t|}.

An analogous estimate using
{\sin x}/{x}\geq\sin 1
on
[0,1]
holds for the sine integral. Summing the non-vanishing
(5,5)
contribution secures the lower bounds (
7.10
), which, via the embeddings, yields (
7.9
).
∎

7.2.
Estimates for the case
Q_{5}=0

We next turn to the case where zero is an eigenvalue of
H
(i.e.,
\mathbf{k}=4
) and all solutions
\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
H\phi=0
are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, i.e.,
Q_{5}L^{2}=\{0\}
.

The condition
Q_{5}L^{2}=\{0\}
causes certain terms in
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
to vanish, leading to the improved decay rate in Theorem
7.2
.
Since the asymptotic expansion of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
under the assumption
Q_{5}L^{2}=\{0\}
follows by an argument analogous to that in the proof of Theorem
2.7
, we omit the details and directly state the result in Lemma
7.6
below.

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume that zero is an eigenvalue of
H
but exhibits no
d
-wave resonance
(\text{i.e.,}\ Q_{5}=0)
. Suppose that
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-22-}
and
0<\lambda\ll 1
. Then

(i)

For
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 4
, the estimates for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
established in Theorem
2.7
remain valid.

(ii)

For
1\leq\alpha\leq 3
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\bigl\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}.

(7.11)

(iii)

For
(\alpha,\beta)=(6,0),\,(0,6),\,(6,4),\,(4,6),\,(6,6)
, the operators admit the following expansions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}+\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{4}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\lambda^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}.

Moreover, if we additionally assume
Q_{4}=0
, then the operators
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
vanish identically for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 6
, and the following refined expansions hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-a_{2}^{\pm}(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-a_{2}^{\pm}(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}\lambda Q_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}-a_{4}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{8}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle+(a_{2}^{\pm})^{2}(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}\lambda^{2}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\lambda^{3}(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}.

In the above,
\mathcal{D}_{6,6}=(b_{1}Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})^{-1}
and
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)
denotes a generic
\lambda
-dependent operator in
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
(possibly differing at each occurrence) satisfying

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,2.

Based on the expansions provided by Lemma
7.6
, we now proceed to prove Theorem
7.2
. In view of the extensive analysis carried out in the previous sections, the proof reduces to the study of the difference kernels

\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{+}-\mathcal{N}_{B}^{-}
,
with

B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\bigl\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mid\alpha=6,\ 0\leq\beta\leq 4\ \text{or}\ 0\leq\alpha\leq 4,\ \beta=6\ \text{or}\ \alpha=\beta=6\bigr\},

where the kernels
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
,
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
are as defined in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), respectively.
We divide the proof into two parts according to
whether a
p
-wave resonance is present or absent.

Part I: The Case
Q_{4}\neq 0
(Presence of
p
-wave resonance).

We establish the kernel estimates for all components involving the index 6 in the following Proposition
7.7
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Assume
Q_{5}=0
and
Q_{4}\neq 0
. For any operator
B^{\pm}(\lambda)
containing the index
6
, the associated integral kernels satisfy the following estimates:

(i)

If
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
for
1\leq\alpha\leq 3
, then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

Moreover, both
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}
satisfy the improved weighted bound
\omega(x)\omega(y)(|t|\log|t|)^{-1}
for
|t|\geq 2
.
In particular, the same unweighted and weighted bounds remain valid under the alternative assumption
Q_{5}=Q_{4}=0
, since in this case
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
also satisfy the estimate (
7.11
).

(ii)

If
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
, then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|).

(iii)

If
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{4,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,4}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
, then

\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\pm}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{2}.

Proof.

(i) Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
for
1\leq\alpha\leq 3
.
We utilize the following expansions for the projected free resolvents:

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{6}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=(b_{0}Q_{6}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda^{2}\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{6}(\lambda,\cdot,z)+\mathcal{T}_{6,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)\bigr),

(7.12)

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z).

(7.13)

Upon substitution into (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), the
\lambda^{-1}
singularity originating from the
Q_{\alpha}
-expansion (
7.13
) is canceled by
4-k_{6}-k_{\alpha}=0
and
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}+\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{1-\ell}
from Lemma
7.6
(ii). Applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,0)
yields the uniform unweighted bound
O\bigl(\langle t\rangle^{-1}\bigr)
.

To establish the improved weighted estimate, we replace (
7.13
) with the expansion
\bigl(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)=\bigl(Q_{\alpha}v{E}_{1}^{\pm}\bigr)
(see (
5.14
)) to extract an extra
\lambda^{1-}
factor at the cost of spatial weights, mirroring the procedure in Proposition
5.3
. This ensures the accelerated decay rate
O\bigl(\omega(x)\omega(y)|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr)
.

(ii) Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
.
We pair the
Q_{6}
expansion (
7.12
) with the singular
Q_{0}
expansion
\bigl(Q_{0}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=\lambda^{-2}\Omega_{0,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)
.
The inherent factor for these indices is
\lambda^{4-k_{6}-k_{0}}=\lambda
in (
5.3
) and (
5.4
). According to Lemma
7.6
(iii), the operator introduces an extra
\lambda
and a logarithmic singularity:
\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}
(and symmetrically for
\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}
).
Multiplying these factors together, and applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-1)
yields the uniform bound
{O}\big(\langle t\rangle^{-1}\log(2+|t|)\big)
.

(iii) Let
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{6,4}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{4,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
.
We employ (
7.12
) for
Q_{6}
, the identity
\bigl(Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,z)
for
Q_{4}
, and the expansion

\bigl(Q_{42}^{1}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,z)=(b_{0}Q_{42}^{1}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,z)+\lambda(\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2})(\lambda,\cdot,z)

for
Q_{42}^{1}
. Recall from Lemma
2.8
that
\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}
exhibits a
|\log\lambda|
growth, whereas
\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}
carry no logarithmic singularity.

For
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{6,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}
(and symmetrically for
\mathcal{M}_{4,6}^{\pm}
), the operator
R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{6}\mathcal{M}_{6,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})
yields the bound
\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}
from both terms: the first term
\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}
explicitly, and the second term
\lambda(\log\lambda)Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}
also yields
\lambda(\log\lambda)^{2}
because the
|\log\lambda|
growth of
\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}
multiplies the existing
\lambda(\log\lambda)
factor. Combining this with
\lambda^{4-k_{6}-k_{4}}=\lambda^{-1}
, we apply Lemma
2.9
(ii) with parameters
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-2)
. This yields the desired bound
O\bigl(\langle t\rangle^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{2}\bigr)
.

Finally, for
B^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\lambda^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}
, we have
\lambda^{4-k_{6}-k_{6}}=\lambda^{-2}
. The leading term
\mathcal{D}_{6,6}
is independent of the sign
\pm
; consequently, the difference
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{D}_{6,6}}^{+}-\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{D}_{6,6}}^{-}
is bounded by
\langle t\rangle^{-1}
(see (
7.7
) in Proposition
7.3
for details). For the remaining term involving
\lambda^{2}(\log\lambda)^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}
, applying Lemma
2.9
(ii) with
(\sigma,\nu)=(0,-2)
gives the uniform bound
\langle t\rangle^{-1}(\log(2+|t|))^{2}
. Summing these contributions completes the proof of (iii).
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.2
(1)
.

Combining Proposition
7.7
with the estimates established in the regular, first-kind, and second-kind resonance cases,
we conclude the proof of Theorem
7.2
(1).
∎

Part II: The Case
Q_{4}=0
(Absence of
p
-wave resonance).

In this part, returning to the expansions detailed in Lemma
7.6
, the absence of
Q_{4}
guarantees that
\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
generate an explicit
\lambda
factor rather than
\lambda\log\lambda
. Concurrently, the remainder in
\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
scales as
{O}(\lambda^{2})
instead of
{O}(\lambda^{2}\log\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6},\quad\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\lambda^{2}Q_{6}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{6}.

(7.14)

Following the same method in Proposition
7.7
, we obtain for all
B^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{M}_{6,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\}
with
\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3,6\}
, the associated kernels exhibit a uniform
{O}(\langle t\rangle^{-1})
decay. Synthesizing this with the regular, first-kind, and second-kind resonance cases, immediately establishes the unweighted estimate
{O}(\langle t\rangle^{-1})
stated in Theorem
7.2
(2).

To obtain the refined weighted asymptotic expansion, we isolate the precise leading-order constants generated by these operators. We formalize this in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.8
.

Assume
Q_{5}=0
and
Q_{4}=0
. For
|t|\geq 2
, the low-energy part of the time evolution admits the following asymptotic expansions in
\mathbb{B}(L^{1}_{\omega},L^{\infty}_{-\omega})
:

\displaystyle\cos(t\sqrt{H})P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\widetilde{{\mathcal{B}}}I_{c}(t)+\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr),

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{\mathrm{ac}}(H)\chi_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}I_{s}(t)+\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr),

where
I_{c}(t)={O}(|t|^{-N})
for
\forall N>0
,
I_{s}(t)=\frac{\pi}{4|t|}+{O}(|t|^{-2})
and the time-independent operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is given by:

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}&=-b_{0}^{2}(a_{4}^{+}-a_{4}^{-})G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{8}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}\\
&\quad+b_{0}^{2}\bigl((a_{2}^{+})^{2}(a_{0}^{+})^{-1}-(a_{2}^{-})^{2}(a_{0}^{-})^{-1}\bigr)\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}.\end{split}

(7.15)

Proof.

To establish the weighted estimates, it suffices to focus on
B^{\pm}\in\{\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm},\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm},\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}\}
. By synthesizing Proposition
7.7
(i) with the results for the regular and first-kind resonance cases, all the remaining terms have already been shown to obey the bound
\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr)
.

We start to evaluate the leading differences
T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}}(t)-T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}}(t)
for
B^{\pm}\in\{\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm},\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm},\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}\}
when
|t|\geq 2
. Proceeding analogously to the proof of Proposition
6.7
, we utilize the free resolvent expansion (
2.1
):

\begin{split}\bigl(Q_{0}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y)&=\frac{a_{0}^{\pm}}{\lambda^{2}}(Q_{0}vG_{0})(\cdot,y)+(Q_{0}vE^{\pm}_{1})(\lambda,\cdot,y),\\
\bigl(Q_{6}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})\bigr)(\cdot,y)&=b_{0}(Q_{6}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y)+a_{2}^{\pm}\lambda^{2}(Q_{6}vG_{4})(\cdot,y)+(Q_{6}vE^{\pm}_{2})(\lambda,\cdot,y),\end{split}

where
|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell-}\langle x\rangle^{4}\langle y\rangle^{4}
and
|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}E_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell+4-}\langle x\rangle^{6-}\langle y\rangle^{6-}
for
\ell=0,1,2
.
Moreover, for the leading sign-independent term
\mathcal{D}_{6,6}
within
\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we also need exploit the difference:

\displaystyle\bigl[R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vR_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)-\bigl[R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vR_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)

\displaystyle=\bigl[(R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4}))vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vR_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})\bigr](x,y)+\bigl[R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4})vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}v(R_{0}^{+}(\lambda^{4})-R_{0}^{-}(\lambda^{4}))\bigr](x,y).

Synthesizing this analysis and extracting the dominant constants, we derive the leading-order expansions for the integral operators:

•

For
\mathcal{M}_{6,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}}-T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}}=-\frac{2b_{0}}{\pi i}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{0}\,I_{c}(t)+\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr)
.

•

For
\mathcal{M}_{0,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}}-T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}}=-\frac{2b_{0}}{\pi i}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{0}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}\,I_{c}(t)+\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr)
.

•

For
\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
:

T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{+}}-T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{-}}=\frac{2}{\pi i}\Theta I_{c}(t)+\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1}\bigr)
, where

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=b_{0}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}+b_{0}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})G_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{0}^{2}(a_{4}^{+}-a_{4}^{-})G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{8}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle\quad+b_{0}^{2}\bigl((a_{2}^{+})^{2}(a_{0}^{+})^{-1}-(a_{2}^{-})^{2}(a_{0}^{-})^{-1}\bigr)\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}.

Here
T_{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}}(t)
denotes the integral operator with the kernel
{\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\pm}}(t,x,y)
and

I_{c}(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=O(|t|^{-N}),\ \quad\forall N>0.

By summing these contributions, the total coefficient of
I_{c}(t)
is
\frac{2}{\pi i}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
where

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=-b_{0}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{0}-b_{0}(a_{2}^{+}-a_{2}^{-})\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}G_{0}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}+\Theta.

Crucially, since
Q_{0}
is the projection onto
v
, we have the identities:

G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{0}=\|V\|_{L^{1}}G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}Q_{6}vG_{4},\quad G_{0}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}=\|V\|_{L^{1}}G_{4}vQ_{6}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0}.

Thus we obtain the expression (
7.15
) for
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}.
The argument for the sine evolution is identical by substituting
I_{c}(t)
with
I_{s}(t)
, where

I_{s}(t)=t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)\,d\lambda=\frac{\pi}{4|t|}+O(|t|^{-2}),

(see (
5.22
)). This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.2
(2)
.

We have established the unweighted estimate
{O}(\langle t\rangle^{-1})
by utilizing (
7.14
).
Regarding the weighted estimates, Proposition
7.8
establishes that the time decay is dictated by whether
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
vanishes. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\neq 0
, the
I_{s}(t)
term dominates, yielding
\sim|t|^{-1}
decay for the sine evolution. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=0
, the remaining terms yield the improved bound
\mathcal{O}(|t|^{-1}(\log|t|)^{-1})
. The theorem is thus established if we can prove the algebraic characterization of
\ker\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
detailed in Lemma
7.9
.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

The operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\colon L^{1}_{\omega}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
is non-zero if and only if
S_{5}L^{2}\neq\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
.

Proof.

Plugging
a_{0}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{8}
,
a_{2}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{512}
, and
a_{4}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i}{1179648}
into the expression for
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
yields:

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=-b_{0}^{2}\cdot\frac{i}{16384}\,G_{2}^{0}v\mathcal{D}_{6,6}\mathbf{K}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}vG_{2}^{0},

where the operator
\mathbf{K}\colon Q_{6}L^{2}\to Q_{6}L^{2}
is defined by

\mathbf{K}=\frac{1}{36}Q_{6}vG_{8}vQ_{6}-\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{6}vG_{4}vQ_{0}vG_{4}vQ_{6}.

We first prove the operator
G_{2}^{0}vQ_{6}\colon Q_{6}L^{2}\to L^{\infty}_{-\omega}
is injective. Suppose
G_{2}^{0}v\psi=0
for some
\psi\in Q_{6}L^{2}
. Applying
\Delta^{2}
in the distributional sense yields
v\psi=0
. By Proposition
8.1
(iv) and the fact that
\psi\in Q_{6}L^{2}=S_{5}L^{2}
, there exists
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\psi=Uv\phi
. Then
V\phi=v\psi=0
, so
\phi
vanishes on the support of
V
. Hence
\psi=Uv\phi=0
, confirming injectivity. By duality,
Q_{6}vG_{2}^{0}
is surjective onto
Q_{6}L^{2}
.

Since
\mathcal{D}_{6,6}
is invertible on
Q_{6}L^{2}
, the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is the zero operator if and only if
\mathbf{K}=0
on
Q_{6}L^{2}
, i.e.,
\ker\mathbf{K}=Q_{6}L^{2}=S_{5}L^{2}
. Thus, to prove this lemma, it suffices to show
\ker\mathbf{K}=\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
.

Indeed, for any
f\in Q_{6}L^{2}
, a direct computation of the associated quadratic form yields:

\langle\mathbf{K}f,f\rangle=\frac{4}{9}\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{2}\bigl|\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}v,f\rangle\bigr|^{2}+\frac{4}{3}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\bigl|\langle x_{i}x_{j}|x|^{2}v,f\rangle\bigr|^{2}-\frac{5}{6}\bigl|\langle|x|^{4}v,f\rangle\bigr|^{2}.

By parameterizing the moments as
A=\langle x_{1}^{4}v,f\rangle
,
B=\langle x_{2}^{4}v,f\rangle
,
C=\langle x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}v,f\rangle
,
D=\langle x_{1}^{3}x_{2}v,f\rangle
, and
E=\langle x_{1}x_{2}^{3}v,f\rangle
, we decouple the form into non-negative squares:

\langle\mathbf{K}f,f\rangle=\frac{8}{9}|A-B|^{2}+\frac{1}{18}|A+B-6C|^{2}+\frac{32}{9}|D+E|^{2}+\frac{8}{9}|D-E|^{2}\geq 0.

(7.16)

Therefore,
\mathbf{K}
is positive semi-definite yielding
f\in\ker\mathbf{K}\iff\langle\mathbf{K}f,f\rangle=0
. According to (
7.16
), this happens if and only if
A=B=3C
and
D=E=0
. These equations coincide exactly with the definition of
\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
(see (
7.2
)), yielding that
\ker\mathbf{K}=\widetilde{S}_{5}L^{2}
. The proof is complete.
∎

8.
A characterization of zero resonances

In this section, we give a precise characterization of the zero-energy resonances of
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, stated in the proof of Proposition
2.5
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, recall the intersection space

W_{-\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\bigcap_{s>\sigma}L^{2,-s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

and the projection subspaces
S_{j}L^{2}
associated with the orthogonal projections
S_{j}
for
0\leq j\leq 5
given in Definition
2.2
:

•

S_{0}L^{2}=\{f\in L^{2}\mid\langle v,f\rangle=0\}
;

•

S_{1}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{0}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{j}v,f\rangle=0,\ j=1,2\}
;

•

S_{2}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{1}L^{2}\mid S_{1}T_{0}f=0\}
;

•

S_{3}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{2}L^{2}\mid S_{0}T_{0}f=0\}
;

•

S_{4}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{3}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{i}x_{j}v,f\rangle=0,\ i,j=1,2,\ \text{and}\ PT_{0}f=0\}
;

•

S_{5}L^{2}=\{f\in S_{4}L^{2}\mid\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}v,f\rangle=0,\ i,j,k=1,2\}
.

Here
v=\sqrt{|V|}
,
P=\|V\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{-1}\langle\cdot,v\rangle v
, and
T_{0}:=U+b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v
.

By the definition of the projection subspaces
S_{j}L^{2}
and the zero-energy types introduced in Definition
1.1
, Proposition
2.5
follows directly from Proposition
8.1
below. Therefore, establishing Proposition
8.1
is the primary focus of this section.

Proposition 8.1
.

Assume that
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
|V(x)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{-\mu}
with
\mu>0
.

(i)

If
\mu>11
, then

\psi\in S_{2}L^{2}
if and only if
\psi=Uv\phi
with
\phi\in W_{-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
H\phi=0
and

\phi(x)=\phi_{0}(x)+\sum_{|\alpha|=1}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\quad\text{for some }\phi_{0}\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

(8.1)

(ii)

If
\mu>14
, then

\psi\in S_{3}L^{2}
if and only if
\psi=Uv\phi
with
\phi\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
H\phi=0
.

(iii)

If
\mu>18
, then

\psi\in S_{4}L^{2}
if and only if
\psi=Uv\phi
with
\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
H\phi=0
.

(iv)

If
\mu>18
, then

\psi\in S_{5}L^{2}
if and only if
\psi=Uv\phi
with
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
H\phi=0
.

From Proposition
8.1
, we also obtain the following spectral characterizations:

•

p
-wave resonance:

S_{3}\neq S_{4}
iff there exists a nonzero solution
\phi\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\setminus W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
H\phi=0
. In other words,
Q_{4}\neq 0
is equivalent to
H
admitting a
p
-wave resonance.

•

d
-wave resonance:

S_{4}\neq S_{5}
iff there exists a nonzero solution
\phi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
H\phi=0
. In other words,
Q_{5}\neq 0
is equivalent to
H
admitting a
d
-wave resonance.

8.1.
Preliminary estimates for
G_{2}^{0}

Prior to the proof of Proposition
8.1
, we establish two technical lemmas concerning the fundamental solution
G_{2}^{0}(x)=|x|^{2}\log|x|
. These are crucial for deriving the decay rates and cancellation properties.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
f\in L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\sigma>\max\{j,2\}+1
. Suppose
f
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y^{\alpha}f(y)\,dy=0\quad\text{for all multi-indices }\alpha\text{ with }|\alpha|\leq j-1.

(8.2)

Then

\left|(G_{2}^{0}f)(x)\right|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\,\langle x\rangle^{2-j}(1+\log\langle x\rangle),\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

For
|x|\leq 2
, fix
0<\epsilon<\min(\sigma-3,2)
. Then
|G_{2}^{0}(x-y)|\lesssim\langle y\rangle^{2+\epsilon}
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
|G_{2}^{0}f(x)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}
, which verifies the bound locally.

For
|x|>2
, note that
\langle x\rangle\sim|x|
. We decompose the integration domain into
A_{1}(x)=\{y:|y|>|x|/2\}
and
A_{2}(x)=\{y:|y|\leq|x|/2\}
, writing
G_{2}^{0}f(x)=\mathcal{I}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{I}_{2}(x)
.

Estimate of
\mathcal{I}_{1}(x)
:
For
y\in A_{1}(x)
,
|G_{2}^{0}(x-y)|\lesssim|y|^{2}(1+\log|y|)
. By Cauchy–Schwarz and switching to polar coordinates
r=|x|\rho
:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1}(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\Bigl(\int_{|y|>|x|/2}|y|^{4}(1+\log|y|)^{2}\langle y\rangle^{-2\sigma}\,dy\Bigr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\,|x|^{3-\sigma}\Bigl(\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\rho^{5-2\sigma}(1+\log(|x|\rho))^{2}\,d\rho\Bigr)^{1/2}.

Using
1+\log(ab)\lesssim(1+\log a)(1+\log b)
for
a,b>1
, and noting that
\sigma>\max\{j,2\}+1
, we obtain

|\mathcal{I}_{1}(x)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\langle x\rangle^{3-\sigma}\Big(1+\log\frac{|x|}{2}\Big)\Big(\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\rho^{5-2\sigma}(1+\log(2\rho))^{2}d\rho\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\langle x\rangle^{2-j}(1+\log\langle x\rangle).

Estimate of
\mathcal{I}_{2}(x)
:
Taylor expanding
G_{2}^{0}(x-y)
about
y=0
to order
j-1
and utilizing the cancellation (
8.2
) on
A_{2}(x)=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus A_{1}(x)
, we obtain

\mathcal{I}_{2}(x)=-\sum_{|\alpha|\leq j-1}\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x)\int_{A_{1}(x)}y^{\alpha}f(y)dy+\mathcal{R}(x),

where the remainder
\mathcal{R}(x)
is given by

\mathcal{R}(x)=\sum_{|\alpha|=j}\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\int_{A_{2}(x)}y^{\alpha}f(y)\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}j(1-\theta)^{j-1}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x-\theta y)d\theta\Bigr)dy.

Since
|\int_{A_{1}(x)}y^{\alpha}f(y)dy|\lesssim|x|^{|\alpha|+1-\sigma}\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}
and
|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x)|\lesssim|x|^{2-|\alpha|}\log|x|
,
then the first part of
\mathcal{I}_{2}(x)
is bounded by

\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}|x|^{2-j}\log|x|
.
For the remainder
\mathcal{R}(x)
, since
|x-\theta y|\sim|x|
for
y\in A_{2}(x)
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, we have
|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x-\theta y)|\lesssim|x|^{2-j}(1+\log|x|)
for
|\alpha|=j
. The condition
\sigma>j+1
ensures integrability, yielding
|\mathcal{R}(x)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,\sigma}}\langle x\rangle^{2-j}(1+\log\langle x\rangle)
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
f\in L^{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\sigma>5
. If
G_{2}^{0}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then
f
must satisfy

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y^{\alpha}f(y)\,dy=0,\quad\text{for every multi-index}\ \alpha\ \text{with}\ |\alpha|\leq 3.

Proof.

For
|x|>2
, analogous to the argument in Lemma
8.2
with
j=4
and
\sigma>5
, we can obtain

\displaystyle(G_{2}^{0}f)(x)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 3}\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}M_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x)+O(|x|^{-2}\log|x|)=:\Psi_{0}(x)+O(|x|^{-2}\log|x|),

where
M_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y^{\alpha}f(y)\,dy
.
Since both
G_{2}^{0}f
and the remainder are in
L^{2}(\{|x|>2\})
, we must have
\Psi_{0}(x)\in L^{2}(\{|x|>2\})
. Computing
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G_{2}^{0}(x)
explicitly gives:

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=|x|^{2}\log|x|M_{0}+(-2\log|x|-1)\sum_{|\alpha|=1}x^{\alpha}M_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big[(\frac{1}{2}+\log|x|)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|y|^{2}f(y)dy+\sum_{|\alpha|=2}\frac{x^{\alpha}}{|x|^{2}}M_{\alpha}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\Big[-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{x_{i}}{|x|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y_{i}|y|^{2}f(y)dy+\frac{2}{3}\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{x^{\alpha}}{|x|^{4}}M_{\alpha}\Big].

Note that for
|x|>2,
we have
|x|^{2}\log|x|\in W_{-3}\setminus W_{-2}
and

\displaystyle(2\log|x|+1)x^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\in W_{-2}\setminus W_{-1}\ \ \text{for}\ |\alpha|=1,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\log|x|,\ \frac{x^{\alpha}}{|x|^{2}}

\displaystyle\in W_{-1}\setminus W_{0}\ \ \text{for}\ |\alpha|=2,

\displaystyle\frac{x_{i}}{|x|^{2}},\ \frac{x^{\alpha}}{|x|^{4}}

\displaystyle\in W_{0}\setminus L^{2}\ \ \text{for}\ |\alpha|=3.

Hence, the only possibility for
\Psi_{0}(x)\in L^{2}(\{|x|>2\})
is that all the constant coefficients multiplying these profiles must be exactly zero, i.e.,
M_{\alpha}=0
for all
|\alpha|\leq 3
. This yields the desired cancellations.
∎

8.2.
Proof of Proposition
8.1

We present a unified proof of the equivalence. Specifically, we will establish that
\psi\in S_{k+1}L^{2}
if and only if
H\phi=0
, where
\phi=Uv\psi\in W_{k-3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
k\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\phi=Uv\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
k=4
. Furthermore, for the case
k=1
, we will show that this equivalence additionally requires
\phi
to satisfy the structural form (
8.1
).

Proof of “
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” (From Subspace to Resonance):

Assume
\psi\in S_{{k}+1}L^{2}
.
For
{k}=1,2
, Definition
2.2
dictates
S_{2-{k}}T_{0}\psi=0
. Thus
T_{0}\psi=(I-S_{2-{k}})T_{0}\psi=v\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2-{k}}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
. Then

\displaystyle U\psi=-b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi+T_{0}\psi=-b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi+v\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2-{k}}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}.

(8.3)

For
{k}=3,4
, the condition
PT_{0}\psi=0
yields
T_{0}\psi=(P+S_{0})T_{0}\psi=0
, implying

\displaystyle U\psi=-b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi.

(8.4)

By the cancellation properties of
S_{{k}+1}
(Definition
2.2
),
f=v\psi
satisfies (
8.2
) with
j=2
(for
{k}=1
) and
j={k}
(for
{k}=2,3,4
). Applying Lemma
8.2
, we obtain:

\left|G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)\right|\lesssim\begin{cases}1+\log\langle x\rangle,&{k}=1,2,\\
\langle x\rangle^{2-{k}}\big(1+\log\langle x\rangle\big),&{k}=3,4.\end{cases}

(8.5)

This confirms
G_{2}^{0}v\psi\in W_{-1}
(for
{k}=1
) and
G_{2}^{0}v\psi\in W_{{k}-3}
(for
{k}=2,3,4
).

We now define the function
\phi(x)

\phi(x):=\begin{cases}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2-{k}}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha},&{k}=1,2,\\
-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x),&{k}=3,4.\end{cases}

(8.6)

Combine this with (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), then
\psi=Uv\phi
. Moreover,
\phi
falls exactly into the required spaces
W_{{k}-3}
(or
L^{2}
for
{k}=4
), with the special structure for
{k}=1
automatically satisfied. Finally, since
\Delta^{2}G_{2}^{0}=b_{0}^{-1}\delta_{0}
, we verify the equation:

(\Delta^{2}+V)\phi=-b_{0}\Delta^{2}G_{2}^{0}v\psi+V\phi=-v(Uv\phi)+V\phi=-V\phi+V\phi=0,

which completes the forward implication.

Proof of “
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” (From Resonance to Subspace):

Conversely, suppose there exists
\phi
satisfying
(\Delta^{2}+V)\phi=0
in the respective space for index
{k}
. Define
\psi=Uv\phi
. We proceed in three steps to show
\psi\in S_{{k}+1}L^{2}
.

Step 1: Establish the cancellation conditions.

We first prove
\langle x^{\alpha}v,\psi\rangle=0
for
|\alpha|\leq 1
when
{k}=1
, for
|\alpha|\leq{k}-1
when
{k}=2,3
, and for
|\alpha|\leq 2
when
{k}=4
.
Let
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a cutoff function satisfying
\eta(x)=1
for
|x|\leq 1
. Using
(\Delta^{2}+V)\phi=0
and integrating by parts against
\eta(\rho x)
with
\rho\in(0,1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{\alpha}v(x)\psi(x)\eta(\rho x)dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{\alpha}\eta(\rho x)\Delta^{2}\phi(x)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{i}\geq\beta_{i}\text{ for }i=1,2\\
|\beta|+|\gamma|=4,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\end{subarray}}C_{\beta,\gamma}\rho^{|\gamma|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{\alpha-\beta}\phi(x)(\partial^{\gamma}\eta)(\rho x)dx.

(8.7)

Then applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and noting that the support of
(\partial^{\gamma}\eta)(y)
is contained in
\{|y|\leq 2\}
for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, we deduce that for
|\alpha|-|\beta|+s\geq 0
(ensured by taking
s\geq 0
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{\alpha-\beta}\phi(x)(\partial^{\gamma}\eta)(\rho x)\,dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\langle x\rangle^{|\alpha|-|\beta|+s}(\partial^{\gamma}\eta)(\rho x)\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{-s}\phi\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\rho^{-|\alpha|+|\beta|-s-1}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{-s}\phi\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Summing up, we obtain the integral (
8.7
) is bounded by
\rho^{3-|\alpha|-s}\|\langle x\rangle^{-s}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
.
Since
\phi\in W_{{k}-3}
, we can select
s=3-{k}+\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that
\phi\in L^{2,-s}
and
3-|\alpha|-s>0
for all strictly required
|\alpha|
. Taking the limit
\rho\to 0
, the integral vanishes, giving the cancellations for
{k}=2,3
. (For
{k}=1
, substituting
\phi
with
\phi_{0}\in W_{-1}
and taking
s=1+\varepsilon
yields the same).

For
{k}=4
, since
\phi\in L^{2}
, then
s=0
. The exponent
3-|\alpha|>0
dictates that the integral vanishes for
|\alpha|\leq 2
. The missing cancellation for
|\alpha|=3
will be deduced via a bootstrap argument below.

Step 2: Deduce the structural forms.

Using the cancellation properties established in Step 1 and arguing as in the derivation of (
8.5
) for
{k}=1,2,3
, we conclude that
G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)\in W_{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
{k}=1
, and
G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)\in W_{{k}-3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
{k}=2,3
. Similarly, for
{k}=4
, the vanishing of the moments
\langle x^{\alpha}v,\psi\rangle
for
|\alpha|\leq 2
, already obtained in Step 1, at least yields
G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Thus by
\phi\in W_{{k}-3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
{k}=1,2,3
and
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

we have

\begin{split}\phi+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi\in W_{{k}-3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ \text{for}\ {k}=1,2,3,\ \text{and at least }\phi+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ \text{for}\ {k}=4.\end{split}

(8.8)

Note that

\Delta^{2}\left(\phi+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi\right)=\Delta^{2}\left(\phi+(\Delta^{2})^{-1}v\psi\right)=\Delta^{2}\phi+v\psi=\left(\Delta^{2}+V\right)\phi=0.

It follows that Fourier transform of the function
\phi+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi
is supported
at the origin, which is the sum of finite derivatives of Dirac distribution
\delta
(see, e.g.,
[
28
]
).
This immediately implies that
\phi+b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi
is a polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Combining this with (
8.8
), we conclude that
\phi
indeed admits the representation stated in (
8.6
) for
{k}=1,2,3,4
.

Step 3: Finalize
{k}=4
cancellations and projection conditions.

Note that
v\psi=V\phi
and

\displaystyle|(V\phi)(x)|=|b_{0}V(x)G_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}\langle x\rangle^{-1}\big(1+\log\langle x\rangle\big)\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\mu}.

Together with
\phi=G_{2}^{0}v\psi\in L^{2}
for
{k}=4
, Lemma
8.3
with
f=v\psi
grants the remaining cancellation condition for
|\alpha|=3
.
Finally, we verify
S_{2-{k}}T_{0}\psi
for
{k}=1,2
and
PT_{0}\psi=0
for
{k}=3,4.
Indeed,
for
{k}=1,2
, applying
S_{2-{k}}
to
T_{0}\psi
yields:

\displaystyle S_{2-{k}}T_{0}\psi

\displaystyle=S_{2-k}U\psi+b_{0}S_{2-{k}}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi=S_{2-{k}}v\phi+b_{0}S_{2-{k}}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi

\displaystyle=S_{2-{k}}\Big(-b_{0}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi+v\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2-{k}}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\Big)+b_{0}S_{2-{k}}vG_{2}^{0}v\psi=0,

due to the intrinsic orthogonality of
S_{2-{k}}
. For
{k}=3,4
, since
\phi=-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}v\psi
, then

\displaystyle PT_{0}\psi(x)

\displaystyle=PU\psi(x)+b_{0}PvG_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)

\displaystyle=Pv\phi+b_{0}PvG_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)=-b_{0}PvG_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)+b_{0}PvG_{2}^{0}v\psi(x)=0.

This verifies all required conditions for
S_{{k}+1}L^{2}
, concluding the proof.

\square

9.
Asymptotic expansions of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
at zero energy

We begin by presenting two abstract algebraic lemmas that are essential for inverting the operator matrices in the proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Lemma 9.1

(
[
33
, Lemma 3.12]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y},X,Y
be vector spaces. Let
\mathbf{A}:\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Y},\mathbf{B}:X\rightarrow\mathfrak{X},\mathbf{C}:\mathfrak{Y}\rightarrow Y
be linear operators. Define
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}
. If
\mathbf{D}^{-1}
exists, then

\mathbf{A}^{-1}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}

provided
\mathbf{B}
is surjective and
\mathbf{C}
is injective.

Lemma 9.2

(
[
34
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

Let
A
be an operator matrix on
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}\mathcal{H}_{2}
:

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{11}&a_{12}\\
a_{21}&a_{22}\end{array}\right),\quad a_{ij}:\mathcal{H}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{i},1\leq i,j\leq 2

where
a_{11}
and
a_{22}
are closed, and
a_{12}
,
a_{21}
are bounded. Suppose
a_{11}
has a bounded inverse. Define

\bm{d}:=a_{22}-a_{21}a_{11}^{-1}a_{12}.

Then the following statements hold:

(i)

A
has a bounded inverse if and only if
\bm{d}

has a bounded inverse. Furthermore, if
\bm{d}
has a bounded inverse, we have

A^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{11}^{-1}a_{12}\bm{d}^{-1}a_{21}a_{11}^{-1}+a_{11}^{-1}&-a_{11}^{-1}a_{12}\bm{d}^{-1}\\
-\bm{d}^{-1}a_{21}a_{11}^{-1}&\bm{d}^{-1}\end{array}\right).

(9.1)

(ii)

If
A
is self-adjoint, then
A>0
(strictly positive) if and only if
a_{11}>0
and
\bm{d}>0
.

Equipped with these algebraic tools, we now proceed with the proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

To invert
M^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we organize the proof into four steps.

Step 1. Preconditioning and Matrix Representation.

We first recall from Proposition
2.6
that if zero is a
\mathbf{k}
-th kind resonance of
H
, the space
L^{2}
admits the orthogonal decomposition
L^{2}\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}
.
For
0<\lambda\ll 1
, we define an isomorphism
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}:\bigoplus_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}\to L^{2}
by

\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}:=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}\lambda^{-k_{\alpha}}\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)Q_{\alpha}f_{\alpha},

where the scaling factors
\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)
are given by

\begin{array}[]{c|ccccccc}\alpha&0&1&2&3&4&5&6\\
\hline\cr\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)&1&(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}&1&(-\log\lambda)^{1/2}&1&(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}&1\end{array}

(9.2)

and the exponents
k_{\alpha}
are defined by

\alpha

0

1

2

3

4

5

6

k_{\alpha}

0

1

1

1

2

3

3

Since
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}
is bijective, Lemma
9.1
dictates that if
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}
is invertible on
\bigoplus_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}
for sufficiently small
\lambda>0
, then

\displaystyle(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}=\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda})^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T},

(9.3)

where
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}
denotes the transpose of
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}
.
Consequently, inverting
M^{\pm}(\lambda)
on
L^{2}
reduces to studying the invertibility of
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}
.

When zero is a
\mathbf{k}
-th kind resonance for some integer
0\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 4
, combining the expansions (
2.10
)–(
2.12
) of
M^{\pm}(\lambda)
with the cancellation properties of
Q_{\alpha}
(Remark
2.4
) yields the decomposition:

\lambda^{-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\sigma_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{\alpha}M^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta}=\lambda^{-2}\left(E_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}+r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right),

where
E_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
and
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
are given by following (
9.6
) and (
9.4
)-(
9.5
), respectively.
Consequently, on the direct sum space
\bigoplus_{\alpha=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}
, the operator matrix
\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}
admits the expansion

\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}=\lambda^{-2}\left(\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}+\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right),

where
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}=\bigl(E_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}\bigr)_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}
is the leading matrix and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\bigl(r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigr)_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{\mathbf{k}+2}
is the remainder.

We specify the exact structures of these matrices below:

•

Asymptotic expansion of the remainder.
The entries
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
exhibit the following asymptotic behaviors:

\begin{split}r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}Q_{\alpha}T_{0}Q_{\beta}+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\quad\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(1,2),(2,1)\};\\
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=b_{1}(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}Q_{\alpha}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{\beta}+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\quad\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(5,6),(6,5)\};\\
r_{1,1}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\left(a_{1}^{\pm}Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1}+Q_{1}T_{0}Q_{1}\right)+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda);\\
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(-\log\lambda)^{-1}a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{\alpha}vG_{4}vQ_{\beta}+\lambda^{2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\quad\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(1,5),(5,1)\};\\
r_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\left(a_{3}^{\pm}Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5}+b_{1}Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5}\right)+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda);\\
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=b_{1}\lambda\log\lambda Q_{\alpha}vG_{6}vQ_{\beta}+\lambda\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\quad\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(4,6),(6,4)\};\\
r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=\lambda a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{\alpha}vG_{4}vQ_{\beta}+\lambda^{3}(-\log\lambda)\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\quad\text{for }(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(0,6),(6,0)\};\end{split}

(9.4)

and for all other index pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
,

\displaystyle r_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

(9.5)

Here and throughout the subsequent proofs,
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)
denotes a generic operator in
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
(possibly varying at each occurrence) that satisfies the estimates:

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

•

Explicit form for
\mathbf{k}=4
.
The leading matrix
\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}=(E_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm})_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{6}
admits the structure:

\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0}&0&E_{0,4}^{\pm}&0\\
0&\mathbf{D}_{1,1}&0&0\\
E_{4,0}^{\pm}&0&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}vG_{4}vQ_{4}&0\\
0&0&0&\mathbf{D}_{2,2}\end{pmatrix},

(9.6)

where
E_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}=g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}vQ_{\beta}+Q_{\alpha}T_{0}Q_{\beta}
for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(0,4),(4,0)\}
, and

\mathbf{D}_{1,1}=\begin{pmatrix}-b_{0}Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1}&0&Q_{1}T_{0}Q_{3}\\
0&Q_{2}T_{0}Q_{2}&0\\
Q_{3}T_{0}Q_{1}&0&0\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{D}_{2,2}=\begin{pmatrix}-b_{1}Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5}&0\\
0&b_{1}Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6}\end{pmatrix}.

(9.7)

We abbreviate the entries of
\mathbf{D}_{1,1}
and
\mathbf{D}_{2,2}
as
E_{\alpha,\beta}
(dropping the
\pm
), since they are independent of the sign
\pm
.

•

General case for
0\leq\mathbf{k}\leq 3
.

For general
\mathbf{k}
, the operator matrix
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}

can be formally understood as the restriction of
\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}
onto
\bigoplus_{\alpha\leq\mathbf{k}+2}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}
.

To streamline the presentation, we will detail the subsequent inversion explicitly for the maximal resonance case
\mathbf{k}=4
. By the algebraic nature of our construction, the explicit formula for
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
when
\mathbf{k}=4
recovers the general
\mathbf{k}
case upon setting
Q_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha>\mathbf{k}+2
.
We now establish the theorem for the case
\mathbf{k}=4
.

Step 2. Exact Invertibility of the Leading Matrix
\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}
.

By the structure of
\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}
, its invertibility reduces to establishing the invertibility of the following three independent components:

(i)

\mathbf{D}_{1,1}
on
Q_{1}L^{2}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}Q_{2}L^{2}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}Q_{3}L^{2}
;

(ii)

\mathbf{D}_{2,2}
on
Q_{5}L^{2}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}Q_{6}L^{2}
;

(iii)

\mathbf{A}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}E_{0,0}^{\pm}&E_{0,4}^{\pm}\\
E_{4,0}^{\pm}&E_{4,4}^{\pm}\end{pmatrix}
on
Q_{0}L^{2}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}Q_{4}L^{2}
.

Note that because the relevant subspaces
Q_{j}L^{2}
are finite-dimensional for
j\neq 2
, injectivity is equivalent to invertibility, a fact we utilize implicitly below.

(i) Invertibility of
\mathbf{D}_{1,1}
:

First,
E_{2,2}=Q_{2}T_{0}Q_{2}
is invertible on
Q_{2}L^{2}
by the definition of
Q_{2}
and Riesz-Fredholm theory. By Lemma
9.2
, it reduces to showing that the Schur complement
Q_{3}T_{0}Q_{1}(Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1})^{-1}Q_{1}T_{0}Q_{3}
is invertible on
Q_{3}L^{2}
. For any nonzero
f\in Q_{1}L^{2}
, we have

\langle Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1}f,f\rangle=-2\sum_{j=1}^{2}|\langle x_{j}v,f\rangle|^{2}<0,

implying that
(Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1})^{-1}
is strictly negative definite. If
f\in Q_{3}L^{2}
satisfies

\langle Q_{3}T_{0}Q_{1}(Q_{1}vG_{2}vQ_{1})^{-1}Q_{1}T_{0}Q_{3}f,f\rangle=0,

this strict negativity enforces
Q_{1}T_{0}Q_{3}f=0
. Since
f\in Q_{3}L^{2}\subseteq S_{2}L^{2}
, we already have
S_{1}T_{0}f=0
. Hence,
0=Q_{1}T_{0}f=(S_{0}-S_{1})T_{0}f=S_{0}T_{0}f
. This implies
f\in S_{3}L^{2}
, forcing
f\in Q_{3}L^{2}\cap S_{3}L^{2}=\{0\}
. Thus,
\mathbf{D}_{1,1}
is invertible.

(ii) Invertibility of
\mathbf{D}_{2,2}
.
Since
\mathbf{D}_{2,2}
is a diagonal operator matrix, it suffices to show
E_{5,5}
and
E_{6,6}
are invertible on
Q_{5}L^{2}
and
Q_{6}L^{2},
respectively.
For any nonzero
f\in Q_{5}L^{2}
, we have

\langle E_{5,5}f,f\rangle=-b_{1}\langle Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5}f,f\rangle=b_{1}\Big(12\sum_{j=1}^{2}|\langle|x|^{2}x_{j}v,f\rangle|^{2}+8\sum_{i,j,\ell=1}^{2}|\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}v,f\rangle|^{2}\Big)>0,

with
b_{1}={1}/({4608\pi}).
Hence
E_{5,5}
is strictly positive definite on
Q_{5}L^{2}
.
We next verify the invertibility of
E_{6,6}=b_{1}Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6}
. For any
f\in Q_{6}L^{2}\subset S_{5}L^{2}
, the associated quadratic form evaluates to

\langle E_{6,6}f,f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|(\Delta^{2})^{-1}vf(x)|^{2}\,dx=\|b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vf\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Proposition
8.1
(iv) and (
8.6
) guarantee that
b_{0}G_{2}^{0}vf\in L^{2}
and that
f=-b_{0}UvG_{2}^{0}vf
. Thus, if
\langle E_{6,6}f,f\rangle=0
, then
G_{2}^{0}vf=0
, which yields
f=0
. This establishes the strict positivity of
E_{6,6}
on
Q_{6}L^{2}
, ensuring its invertibility (see also
[
38
, Lemma 4.9]
for proof).

(iii) Invertibility of
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
.
In this part, we not only establish the invertibility of
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
, but also derive the formula for
(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}
, which is essential for obtaining the refined expansion of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
in the subsequent analysis.
Our discussion proceeds according to whether
Q_{4}^{0}=0
or not.

Case 1:
Q_{4}^{0}=0
.
Here
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{1}
(see Definition
2.3
that
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{4}^{1}
), so

Q_{4}v=Q_{4}(x_{j}v)=Q_{4}(|x|^{2}v)=0.

(9.8)

Consequently,
Q_{4}vG_{2}vQ_{0}=Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}=0
, reducing
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
to the form

\mathbf{A}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0}&Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}\\
Q_{4}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0}=a_{0}^{\pm}\|V\|_{L^{1}}Q_{0}
with
a_{0}^{\pm}=\pm i/8
, then
(a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0})^{-1}=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}.

Using Lemma
9.2
,
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
is invertible if and only if the Schur complement

\bm{d}^{\pm}:=a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}-Q_{4}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}(a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}

is invertible on
Q_{4}L^{2}
(here
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{1}
). For any
f\in Q_{4}L^{2}
, using (
9.8
), we
derive that

\langle\bm{d}^{\pm}f,f\rangle=\pm i\Bigl[\sum_{j,k=1}^{2}\tfrac{1}{128}|\langle x_{j}x_{k}v,f\rangle|^{2}+\langle 8(Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}f,Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}f\rangle\Bigr].

Because
(Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0})^{-1}
is strictly positive on
Q_{0}L^{2}
, the condition
\langle\bm{d}^{\pm}f,f\rangle=0
forces
\langle x_{j}x_{k}v,f\rangle=0
for all
j,k
, as well as
Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}f=0
. This implies
f\in S_{4}L^{2}
. Since
Q_{4}L^{2}\cap S_{4}L^{2}=\{0\}
, it follows that
f=0
, thereby confirming the invertibility of
\bm{d}^{\pm}
. The inverse
(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}:=\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{i,j}^{\pm}\bigr)_{i,j\in\{0,4\}}
can then be explicitly constructed via (
9.1
), yielding

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}+(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-2}\mathcal{W}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}^{*},\quad\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}=-(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1},

(9.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=-(a_{0}^{\pm})^{-1}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}^{*},\quad\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}=(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1},

where we have denoted
\mathcal{W}:=\|V\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}
.

Case 2:
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
.
Utilizing the projection splitting
Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{4}^{1}
, we represent
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
as a
2\times 2
block matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}=\bigl(\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{\pm}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
with respect to the orthogonal decomposition
(Q_{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{4}^{0}L^{2})\oplus Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}
. The block components are explicitly given by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0}&g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{0}\\[6.0pt]
g_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0}+Q_{4}^{0}T_{0}Q_{0}&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}\\
a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{4}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{A}_{22}^{\pm}=a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}.

(9.10)

First, we show that
\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm}
is invertible on
Q_{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
. Write
\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm}=(\log\lambda)\mathbf{A}_{0}+\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\pm}
with

\mathbf{A}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}\\
b_{0}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{0}vQ_{0}&Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{0}+a_{1}^{\pm}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}\\
Q_{4}^{0}T_{0}Q_{0}+a_{1}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0}&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Observe that the operators
Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0}\colon Q_{0}L^{2}\to Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
and
Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}\colon Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}\to Q_{0}L^{2}
are both invertible. Indeed, for any
f\in Q_{0}L^{2}
,

Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0}f=\langle f,v\rangle Q_{4}^{0}(|x|^{2}v)=0\Longleftrightarrow\langle f,v\rangle=0\Longleftrightarrow f=0.

Similarly, for any
f\in Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
,

Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0}f=\langle f,|x|^{2}v\rangle v=0\Longleftrightarrow\langle f,|x|^{2}v\rangle=0\Longleftrightarrow f\in Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}.

Since
Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}\cap Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}=\{0\}
, it follows that
f=0
. Because
\dim(Q_{0}L^{2})=\dim(Q_{4}^{0}L^{2})=1
, this injectivity guarantees that both operators are invertible. Thus
\mathbf{A}_{0}
is invertible with

\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&(b_{0}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0})^{-1}\\
(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.

For sufficiently small
\lambda>0
, the Neumann series yields

(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}-(\log\lambda)^{-2}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\pm}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}+(\log\lambda)^{-3}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

(9.11)

By Lemma
9.2
,
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}=\bigl(\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{\pm}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
is invertible if and only if

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}-\begin{pmatrix}Q_{4}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}&a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{4}^{1}\\
a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}\end{pmatrix}

is invertible on
Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}
. We analyze this invertibility by splitting
Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}=Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}\oplus Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
(see Definition
2.3
and Remark
2.4
for more details):

\bullet

Subcase 2a (
Q_{42}^{1}=0
):

Here
Q_{4}^{1}=Q_{41}^{1}
. For any
f\in Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}=Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}
,

a_{2}^{\pm}\langle Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}f,f\rangle=a_{2}^{\pm}\langle Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}f,f\rangle=a_{2}^{\pm}\sum_{j,\ell=1}^{2}4|\langle x_{j}x_{\ell}v,f\rangle|^{2},

(9.12)

with
a_{2}^{\pm}=\pm i/512
. The definition of
Q_{41}^{1}
ensures that this quadratic form is nondegenerate, so
a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1}
is invertible on
Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}
. Combining this with
\|(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}=O(|\log\lambda|^{-1})

yields

\bm{d}^{\pm}
is invertible and

(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}=(a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1})^{-1}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda):=(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}D_{0}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Substituting the expressions (
9.11
) for
(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}
and (
9
) for
\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{\pm}
into (
9.1
), we deduce the block representation

(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}=\big(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big)_{\alpha,\beta\in\{0,4\}},

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-2}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\\
\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\left[Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1}\right],\\
\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\left[Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}+Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}\right],\\
\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-2}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\left[Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1}+Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\right]\\
&\quad+(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1}+(a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{1})^{-1}.\end{split}

(9.13)

\bullet

Subcase 2b (
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
):
Observe that
Q_{4}vG_{4}vQ_{42}^{1}=0
. With respect to the decomposition
Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}=Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}\oplus Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, we can express
\bm{d}^{\pm}=\bigl(d^{\pm}_{ij}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
, where

\begin{split}d_{11}^{\pm}&=a_{2}^{\pm}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\ \ d_{12}^{\pm}=-(\log\lambda)^{-1}a_{2}^{\pm}B+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\\
d_{21}^{\pm}&=-(\log\lambda)^{-1}a_{2}^{\pm}B^{*}+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\ \ d_{22}^{\pm}=(\log\lambda)^{-2}a_{2}^{\pm}\mathcal{D}+(\log\lambda)^{-3}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),\end{split}

(9.14)

with

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle=Q_{42}^{1}T_{0}Q_{0}(b_{0}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{2}vQ_{0})^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1}.

By (
9.12
), the leading operator
Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}
is invertible on
Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}
. Defining
D_{0}:=(Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1})^{-1}
, it follows that for sufficiently small
\lambda>0
,
d_{11}^{\pm}
is invertible on
Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}
with its inverse given by

(d_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}=(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}D_{0}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Applying Lemma
9.2
,
\bm{d}^{\pm}=\bigl(d^{\pm}_{ij}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
is invertible if and only if

\bm{d}^{\pm}_{1}:=d_{22}^{\pm}-d_{21}^{\pm}(d_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}d_{12}^{\pm}=(\log\lambda)^{-2}a_{2}^{\pm}\mathcal{S}+(\log\lambda)^{-3}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)

(9.15)

is invertible on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, where

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{D}-B^{*}D_{0}B=\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*}\bm{d}_{2}\mathcal{W}_{1},

with
\mathcal{W}_{1}=(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1}
and

\bm{d}_{2}=Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}-Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}(Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1})^{-1}Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}.

Observe that
Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}
is strictly positive on
Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}
and the block matrix

\begin{pmatrix}Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}&Q_{4}^{0}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}\\
Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{4}^{0}&Q_{41}^{1}vG_{4}vQ_{41}^{1}\end{pmatrix}

(9.16)

is strictly positive on
Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}
. Here, (
9.16
) is exactly the block matrix representation of the strictly positive operator
(Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{41}^{1})vG_{4}v(Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{41}^{1})
with respect to
Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}
. By Lemma
9.2
(ii), it follows that
\bm{d}_{2}
is strictly positive on
Q_{4}^{0}L^{2}
.

Therefore, for any
f\in Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, the condition
\langle\mathcal{S}f,f\rangle=\langle\bm{d}_{2}\mathcal{W}_{1}f,\mathcal{W}_{1}f\rangle=0
implies that
\mathcal{W}_{1}f=0
. This yields
Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1}f=PT_{0}f=0
, which means
f\in S_{4}L^{2}
. Then
f\in S_{4}L^{2}\cap Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}=\{0\}
.
This proves that
\mathcal{S}
is positive definite. Consequently, by (
9.15
),
\bm{d}_{1}^{\pm}
is invertible on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
and

(\bm{d}_{1}^{\pm})^{-1}=(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}+(\log\lambda)\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Thus
\bm{d}_{1}^{\pm}
is invertible on
Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, which in turn guarantees the invertibility of
\bm{d}^{\pm}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
.

Next, we turn to explicitly deriving the formula for
(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}.

Under the decomposition
Q_{4}^{1}L^{2}=Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}\oplus Q_{42}^{1}L^{2}
, using the expression (
9.14
) for
d_{ij}^{\pm}
together with (
9.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\bigl(D_{0}+D_{0}B\mathcal{S}^{-1}B^{*}D_{0}\bigr)+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&(\log\lambda)(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}D_{0}B\mathcal{S}^{-1}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\\[4.0pt]
(\log\lambda)(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}B^{*}D_{0}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}+(\log\lambda)\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover, recall that
\bm{d}^{\pm}=\mathbf{A}_{22}^{\pm}-\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}=\bigl(\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{\pm}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{\pm}
are defined in (
9
). Then, by (
9.1
), under the decomposition
(Q_{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{4}^{0}L^{2})\oplus(Q_{41}^{1}L^{2}\oplus Q_{42}^{1}L^{2})
we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}+(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}&-(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\\
-(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}&(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Direct computations based on the previous inverse bounds reveal that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}+(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\\[4.0pt]
(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&P_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle-(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{12}^{\pm}(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\\[4.0pt]
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&K_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle-(\bm{d}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{\pm}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\\[4.0pt]
\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)&N_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.

with
\mathcal{W}_{1}=(b_{0}Q_{0}vG_{2}vQ_{4}^{0})^{-1}Q_{0}T_{0}Q_{42}^{1},
and

\displaystyle K_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-(\log\lambda)(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda):=(\log\lambda)\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),

\displaystyle N_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-(\log\lambda)(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda):=(\log\lambda)\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}+\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),

\displaystyle P_{22}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{*}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda):=\mathcal{C}^{\pm}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Consequently, condense back to the representation
(\mathbf{A}^{\pm})^{-1}=\big(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big)_{\alpha,\beta\in\{0,4\}}
on
Q_{0}L^{2}\oplus Q_{4}L^{2}
, then

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-2}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\\
\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+Q_{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{1},\\
\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0}+Q_{4}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{0},\\
\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)\\
&+(\log\lambda)\Bigl[(Q_{4}^{0}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\pm}Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\pm}Q_{4}^{0})+(Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1})\Bigr]\\
&+\bigl(Q_{4}^{0}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}+Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{4}^{0}\bigr)+Q_{41}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{41}^{1}+(\log\lambda)^{2}(a_{2}^{\pm})^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}\mathcal{S}^{-1}Q_{42}^{1}\\
&+(\log\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{42}^{1}.\end{split}

(9.17)

Thus, we have established the invertibility of
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
across all three possible cases:
Q^{0}_{4}=0
;
Q^{0}_{4}\neq 0
with
Q_{42}^{1}=0
; and
Q^{0}_{4}\neq 0
with
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
. Based on the invertibility of the respective components established in parts (i)–(iii), we conclude that
\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}
is invertible on
\mathbin{\scalebox{1.2}{$\oplus$}}_{\alpha=0}^{6}Q_{\alpha}L^{2}
, and its inverse is given by

\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&0&\mathcal{D}_{0,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&0\\
0&\left(\mathbf{D}_{1,1}\right)^{-1}&0&0\\
\mathcal{D}_{4,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)&0&\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)&0\\
0&0&0&\left(\mathbf{D}_{2,2}\right)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}:=\left(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{6},

(9.18)

where
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
for all
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 6
. Specifically, the entries
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
\alpha,\beta\in\{0,4\}
are defined in (
9.9
), (
9.13
), and (
9.17
), respectively corresponding to the three distinct cases.
Given the structures of
\mathbf{D}_{1,1}
and
\mathbf{D}_{2,2}
in (
9.7
), their inverses take the forms

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{D}_{1,1}\right)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}_{1,1}&0&\mathcal{D}_{1,3}\\
0&\mathcal{D}_{2,2}&0\\
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}&0&\mathcal{D}_{3,3}\end{pmatrix},\quad\left(\mathbf{D}_{2,2}\right)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}&0\\
0&\mathcal{D}_{6,6}\end{pmatrix}.

(9.19)

Here, we write
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
instead of
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for brevity, as the entries of
\left(\mathbf{D}_{1,1}\right)^{-1}
and
\left(\mathbf{D}_{2,2}\right)^{-1}
depend on neither
\lambda
nor the choice of sign
\pm
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2,2}=(Q_{2}T_{0}Q_{2})^{-1},\quad\mathcal{D}_{5,5}=(-b_{1}Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1},\quad\mathcal{D}_{6,6}=(b_{1}Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})^{-1}.

Step 3. Asymptotic expansion of
(M^{\pm}(\lambda))^{-1}
via Neumann series.

We begin by estimating the matrix operators
\mathbf{S}_{4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigl(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\bigr)^{-1}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{6}r_{\alpha,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{j,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{6}
. From (
9.9
), (
9.13
), and (
9.17
), we observe that all leading entries satisfy

\Big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{if }(\beta,\alpha)\neq(4,4),\\[4.0pt]
|\log\lambda|^{2}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{if }(\beta,\alpha)=(4,4),\end{cases}

for
\ell=0,1,2
. Combining this with the remainder estimates (
9.4
)–(
9.5
) for
r_{\alpha,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, and noting that each factor
\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
is paired with a remainder entry
r_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
satisfying
r_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=O\bigl(\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\bigr)
, we obtain

\mathbf{S}_{4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigl(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\bigr)^{-1}=O\bigl((-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\bigr).

We then employ the Neumann series expansion in the small parameter
(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}
:

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}^{T}M^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathbf{B}_{\lambda}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\nolimits+\mathbf{S}_{4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\Bigl[\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{S}_{4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\left(\mathbf{D}_{4}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\right)^{n}\Bigr]

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{6}.

(9.20)

To bound the remainder operators
\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we examine the structure of matrix multiplication in the Neumann series. Any term contributing to
\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
that involves the factor
\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
must be multiplied by an adjacent remainder entry of the form
r_{4,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)
or
r_{j,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. According to (
9.4
)–(
9.5
), all such entries carrying an index
4
satisfy
r_{4,j}^{\pm}(\lambda),r_{i,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=O\bigl(\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\bigr)
. This polynomial decay in
\lambda
compensates the
|\log\lambda|^{2}
growth originating from
\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Consequently, from (
9.4
)–(
9.5
) it follows that every remainder component
\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
belongs to
\mathbb{B}(L^{2})
and obeys the decay estimate

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\lambda^{-\ell},\qquad\ell=0,1,2.

(9.21)

Finally, reverting the preconditioning step via (
9.3
), we obtain the full inverse operator on
L^{2}
:

\left(M^{\pm}(\lambda)\right)^{-1}=\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 6}\lambda^{2-k_{\alpha}-k_{\beta}}Q_{\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)Q_{\beta},

where the unified block operators are defined by

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda):=\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\sigma_{\beta}(\lambda)\left(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right).

Step 4. Extraction of Decay Rates for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Our final task is to track the powers of
\lambda
and
\log\lambda
appearing in
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
,
\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}
and establish the asymptotic expansion for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.
The analysis reduces to examining the structure of these components.

(1) Analysis of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 4.

From (
9.18
)–(
9.19
), we have
