\mathcal{D}_{0,3}^{\pm}=\mathcal{D}_{3,0}^{\pm}=\mathcal{D}_{2,3}^{\pm}=\mathcal{D}_{3,2}^{\pm}=0,

which implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(-\log\lambda)^{1/2}\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for the pairs
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(0,3),(3,0),(2,3),(3,2)\}
.
Combining this with the definition of
\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)
in (
9.2
) and the bound (
9.21
) for
\Upsilon_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we deduce that for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3
and
\ell=0,1,2
,

\Big\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\beta,\alpha)\neq(3,3),\\
|\log\lambda|\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{for }(\beta,\alpha)=(3,3).\end{cases}

For
\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\Upsilon_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we now examine the refined structure under the condition
Q_{4}^{0}=0
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{0,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\beta,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
are nonzero only for
\beta\in\{0,4\}
, every term in
\Upsilon_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)
contains at least a factor
r_{i,j}^{\pm}
with
i
or
j
in
\{0,4\}
. Combining this fact with the estimates in (
9.4
)–(
9.5
), and noting that the operator norms of all entries
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
are bounded without any logarithmic factor (see (
9.9
)), we obtain
\Upsilon_{0,0}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Consequently, by (
9.9
), we arrive at the refined expansion (
2.15
).

Next, we analyze the operators
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{M}_{4,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
. By symmetry, we focus only on
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\Upsilon_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{j,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
is nonzero only for
j\in\{0,4\}
, every term in
\Upsilon_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
contains at least one factor
r_{i,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
j\in\{0,4\}
. Combining this fact with the estimates in (
9.4
)–(
9.5
), and noting that all leading entries satisfy
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
, with the sole exception

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\begin{cases}|\log\lambda|^{2}\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{if }Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0\text{ and }Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0,\\
\lambda^{-\ell},&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

for
\ell=0,1,2
, we obtain the following refined behavior.

If
Q_{4}^{0}=0
or
Q_{42}^{1}=0
, then

\Upsilon_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{3/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}.

If
Q_{4}^{0}\neq 0
and
Q_{42}^{1}\neq 0
, the slowest-decaying term in
\Upsilon_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
is

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{j,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\quad\text{for }\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3\},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\quad\text{for }\alpha=4.

More precisely, under this condition we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{j,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{7/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\quad\text{for }\alpha\in\{0,1,2,3\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{4,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(-\log\lambda)^{11/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Thus, combining these estimates with the definition of
\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)
and the expansions of
\mathcal{D}_{i,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)
(
i,j\in\{0,4\}
) from (
9.9
), (
9.13
), and (
9.17
), we obtain the desired results for all
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\Upsilon_{\alpha,4}^{\pm}(\lambda)
with
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
.

(2) Analysis of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
for
\beta\in\{5,6\}
and
0\leq\alpha\leq 6.

Firstly, consider the cases
0\leq\alpha\leq 4.

Here we restrict our attention to
\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, as the corresponding results for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
follow from identical arguments by symmetry.
From (
9.18
)–(
9.19
), when
\beta\in\{5,6\}
, the operators
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
are non-vanishing only if
\alpha=\beta
. Thus, for
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
and
\beta\in\{5,6\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\beta}(\lambda)\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)\Upsilon_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda).

By the definition of
\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda)
in (
9.2
) and the estimate
(
9.21
)
, we deduce that for
0\leq\alpha\leq 4
and
\beta\in\{5,6\}
,

\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\beta,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2})}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

Secondly, consider the case
(5,5).

Note that
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{D}_{5,\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
are nonzero only for
\alpha=5
. Combining this with (
9.20
) and the structure of matrix multiplication, we derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\Big[\mathcal{D}_{5,5}-\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+\sum_{j,l=0}^{6}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{j,l}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{l,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+(-\log\lambda)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big].

By the expression (
9.4
)–(
9.5
) of
r_{i,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)
and most
\mathcal{D}_{j,l}^{\pm}(\lambda)=0
from (
9.18
)–(
9.19
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}=(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\Big[a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}^{-2}(Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1}+b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}\Big]+\lambda^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\sum_{j,l=0}^{6}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,j}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{j,l}^{\pm}(\lambda)r_{l,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}=b_{1}^{2}(-\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +(-\log\lambda)^{-2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)
admits the representation

\mathcal{M}_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{5,5}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{5,5}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\left[-b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+b_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}\right],

\displaystyle\Gamma_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}^{-2}(Q_{5}vG_{6}vQ_{5})^{-1}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

This establishes the desired bound for
\mathcal{M}_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Thirdly, consider the cases
(5,6)
and
(6,5).

The analysis follows a similar pattern to the previous case, so we omit some details.
For
\mathcal{M}_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Upsilon_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Big[-\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle\quad-\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}r_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(-\log\lambda)^{-2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)\Big]

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{5,6}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{5,6}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Big[b_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{1}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}\Big],

\displaystyle\Gamma_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-a_{3}^{\pm}\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

This establishes the desired result for
\mathcal{M}_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.
Similarly, for
\mathcal{M}_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\log\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{6,5}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{6,5}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+(\log\lambda)^{-1}\Big[b_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{1}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}\Big],

\displaystyle\Gamma_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-a_{3}^{\pm}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

This completes the analysis for
\mathcal{M}_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.

Finally,
it remains to analyze the case
\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\Upsilon_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+\mathcal{D}_{6,6}r_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}-\mathcal{D}_{6,6}r_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{D}_{6,6}r_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}r_{6,5}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{5,5}r_{5,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(-\log\lambda)^{-5/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)+(\log\lambda)^{-2}\Gamma_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{6,6}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1}b_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle+(-\log\lambda)^{-2}\Big[-b_{1}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}

\displaystyle+b_{1}^{4}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}\left((Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}\right)^{2}\Big],

\displaystyle\Gamma_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=a_{3}^{\pm}b_{1}\mathcal{D}_{6,6}(Q_{6}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{5})\mathcal{D}_{5,5}(Q_{5}vG_{6}^{0}vQ_{6})\mathcal{D}_{6,6}+(-\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\Lambda^{\pm}(\lambda).

This establishes the desired result for
\mathcal{M}_{6,6}^{\pm}(\lambda)
.
∎

10.
The proof of Lemmas
2.8
and Lemma
2.9

This section is primarily devoted to completing the proofs of Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
. Moreover, we also provide the asymptotic expansion of two special oscillatory integrals.

10.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.8

The proof of Lemma
2.8
relies heavily on the directional Taylor expansion provided by Lemma
10.1
(see
[
38
, Lemma 3.5]
), which allows us to systematically exploit the cancellation properties of the projections
Q_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 10.1

(
[
38
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

Assume that
\lambda>0
,
\tilde{x}=(-x_{2},x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\rho\equiv\rho(x)=\frac{x}{|x|}
for
x\neq 0
and
\rho(x)=0
for
x=0
.
Let
\theta\in[0,1]
and
|x|\cos\xi=|x|\cos\xi(x,y,\theta)=\langle x,\rho(y-\theta x)\rangle
,
where
\xi\equiv\xi(x,y,\theta)
is the angle between the vectors
x
and
y-\theta x
.
Then

(i)

If
F\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, then

F(\lambda|x-y|)=F(\lambda|y|)-\lambda|x|\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{L}_{1}[F](\lambda|y-\theta x|,\xi)\,d\theta.

(ii)

If
F\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
F^{\prime}(0)=0
, then

F(\lambda|x-y|)=F(\lambda|y|)-\lambda F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle+\lambda^{2}|x|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\mathcal{L}_{2}[F](\lambda|y-\theta x|,\xi)\,d\theta.

(iii)

If
F\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with
F^{\prime}(0)=F^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
, then

\displaystyle F(\lambda|x-y|)

\displaystyle=F(\lambda|y|)-\lambda F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)}{\lambda|y|}\langle\rho(y),\tilde{x}\rangle^{2}+F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda^{3}|x|^{3}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)^{2}\mathcal{L}_{3}[F](\lambda|y-\theta x|,\xi)\,d\theta,

where
\langle\rho(y),\tilde{x}\rangle^{2}=|x|^{2}-\langle\rho(y),x\rangle^{2}
.

(iv)

If
F\in C^{4}(\mathbb{R})
with
F^{(k)}(0)=0
for
k=1,2,3
, then

\displaystyle F(\lambda|x-y|)

\displaystyle=F(\lambda|y|)-\lambda F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)}{\lambda|y|}\langle\rho(y),\tilde{x}\rangle^{2}+F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda^{3}}{3!}\Bigl[\Bigl(-\frac{F^{\prime}(\lambda|y|)}{\lambda^{2}|y|^{2}}+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda|y|)}{\lambda|y|}\Bigr)3\langle\rho(y),x\rangle\langle\rho(y),\tilde{x}\rangle^{2}+F^{(3)}(\lambda|y|)\langle\rho(y),x\rangle^{3}\Bigr]

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda^{4}|x|^{4}}{3!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)^{3}\mathcal{L}_{4}[F](\lambda|y-\theta x|,\xi)\,d\theta.

Here
F^{(\ell)}
denotes the
\ell
-th derivative of
F
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}[F](r,\xi)

\displaystyle:=F^{\prime}(r)\cos\xi,\ \ \ \mathcal{L}_{2}[F](r,\xi):=F^{\prime\prime}(r)\cos^{2}\xi+\frac{F^{\prime}(r)}{r}\sin^{2}\xi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{3}[F](r,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(-\frac{F^{\prime}(r)}{r^{2}}+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(r)}{r}\Bigr)3\cos\xi\sin^{2}\xi+F^{(3)}(r)\cos^{3}\xi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{4}[F](r,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\frac{F^{\prime}(r)}{r^{3}}-\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(r)}{r^{2}}\Bigr)(15\cos^{2}\xi\sin^{2}\xi-3\sin^{4}\xi)+\frac{F^{(3)}(r)}{r}6\cos^{2}\xi\sin^{2}\xi+F^{(4)}(r)\cos^{4}\xi.

Now, we start to use Lemma
10.1
to prove Lemma
2.8
.

Proof.

To streamline the analysis, we first formulate a universal bound for the derivatives of the kernel. For any smooth profile
\mathcal{G}
satisfying
|\partial_{p}^{\ell}\big(e^{\mp ip}\mathcal{G}(p)\big)|\lesssim\langle p\rangle^{-1/2}|p|^{-\ell}
, applying Leibniz’s rule together with the inequality
\langle\lambda(\theta x-y)\rangle^{-1/2}\lesssim\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\langle x\rangle^{1/2}
(
\theta\in[0,1]
) yields:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{G}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left[e^{\pm i\lambda(|\theta x-y|-|y|)}\left(e^{\mp i\lambda|\theta x-y|}\mathcal{G}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell}|x|^{\ell_{1}}\lambda^{-\ell_{2}}\langle\lambda(\theta x-y)\rangle^{-1/2}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\langle x\rangle^{\ell+1/2}.

(10.1)

Provided that
|V(x)|
decays sufficiently fast, this spatial growth
\langle x\rangle^{\ell+1/2}
is harmlessly absorbed when taking the
L^{2}_{x}
norm against
v(x)
. This principle will be invoked repeatedly.

Proof of Part (i).

Recall from (
2.6
) that
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda^{4})(x,y)=\lambda^{-2}F_{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)
, where

\displaystyle{F}_{\pm}(p)=

\displaystyle\Bigl(a_{0}^{\pm}+b_{0}p^{2}\log p+a_{1}^{\pm}p^{2}+a_{2}^{\pm}p^{4}+O_{2}\bigl(p^{5}\bigr)\Bigr)\chi_{1}(p)+\frac{i}{8}\Bigl(\pm e^{\pm ip}w_{\pm}(p)-e^{-p}w_{+}(ip)\Bigr)\chi_{2}(p).

Observe that
F_{\pm}(p)
comprising both low-frequency polynomial expansions and high-frequency oscillations satisfies:

\bigl|\partial_{p}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp ip}F_{\pm}(p)\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|\partial_{p}^{\ell}\bigl(e^{\mp ip}F_{\pm}^{\prime}(p)\bigr)\bigr|\lesssim\langle p\rangle^{-1/2}|p|^{-\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,2.

For
\alpha=0
, defining
\Omega_{0,\pm}(\lambda,x,y):=\left(Q_{0}vF_{\pm}(\lambda|\cdot-y|)\right)(x)
and directly applying (
10.1
) gives:

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\Omega_{0,\pm}(\lambda,x,y)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\left\|v(x)\langle x\rangle^{\ell+1/2}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}.

For
\alpha\geq 1
, we exploit Lemma
10.1
(i) to write

F_{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|)=F_{\pm}(\lambda|y|)-\lambda|x|\int_{0}^{1}F^{\prime}_{\pm}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\cos\xi\,d\theta.

Since
Q_{\alpha}v=0
for
\alpha\geq 1
(see Remark
2.4
), the leading term
F_{\pm}(\lambda|y|)
is annihilated, yielding:

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})(\cdot,y)\right)(x)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{-1}Q_{\alpha}\Big(|\cdot|v\int_{0}^{1}F^{\prime}_{\pm}(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)\cos\xi\,d\theta\Big)(x):=\lambda^{-1}\Omega_{\alpha,\pm}(\lambda,x,y).

Applying (
10.1
) to
F^{\prime}_{\pm}
guarantees
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\Omega_{\alpha,\pm})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}
, concluding Part (i).

Proof of Part (ii).

Utilize
Qv=Qx_{1}v=Qx_{2}v=0
for
Q\in\{Q_{\alpha}|_{2\leq\alpha\leq 4},Q_{4}^{j}|_{j=0,1},Q_{4j}^{1}|_{j=1,2}\},
then we exploit Lemma
10.1
(ii) to write

\displaystyle Q\bigl(vF_{\pm}(\lambda|\cdot-y|)\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}Q\Bigl(v|\cdot|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\Bigl(F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)\cos^{2}\xi+\frac{F^{\prime}(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)}{\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|}\sin^{2}\xi\Bigr)d\theta\Bigr)(x)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}Q\Bigl(v|\cdot|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\Bigl(\Theta^{\pm}(\lambda,\theta,\cdot,y)+2b_{0}\log(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)\chi_{1}(\lambda|\theta\cdot-y|)\Bigr)d\theta\Bigr)(x)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\mathcal{J}_{\pm}(\lambda,x,y).

Here,
\Theta^{\pm}
consolidates the bounded low-frequency derivatives and the high-frequency oscillatory components, which satisfies the bound (
10.1
). In contrast, for
0<\lambda\ll 1
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, the logarithmic factor induces the following pointwise bound

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\big[e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\log(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\chi_{1}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\big]\big|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\bigl[e^{\pm i\lambda(|\theta x-y|-|y|)}\bigl(e^{\mp i\lambda|\theta x-y|}\log(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\chi_{1}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)\bigr)\bigr]\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}(|\log\lambda|+|\theta x-y|^{-1/2})\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\langle x\rangle^{\ell+1/2}.

Combining this with
\|v|{}\cdot|{}^{2}\langle\cdot\rangle^{1/2+\ell}|{}\theta\cdot-y|{}^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\theta^{-1/2}\langle\theta^{-1}y\rangle^{-1/2}
(valid since
|{}V(x)|{}\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-11-}
), it follows that for
\ell=0,1,2
:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\big(e^{\mp i\lambda|y|}\mathcal{J}_{\pm}(\lambda,\cdot,y)\big)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\theta^{-1/2}\,d\theta\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}|\log\lambda|\langle\lambda y\rangle^{-1/2}.

Denote
\mathcal{J}_{\pm}=\mathcal{J}_{\alpha,\pm}
if
Q=Q_{\alpha}
(with
2\leq\alpha\leq 6
);

\mathcal{J}_{\pm}=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{j}
if
Q=Q_{4}^{j}
(with
j=0,1
) and
\mathcal{J}_{\pm}=\mathcal{J}_{4,\pm}^{1,j}
if
Q=Q_{4j}^{1}
(with
j=1,2
), thereby completing the proof of Part (ii).

Proof of Part (iii).

We decompose
Q_{\alpha}vR_{0}^{\pm}
into
\left(b_{0}Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0}\right)
and
\left[Q_{\alpha}v\left(R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}\right)\right]
.

Step 1: Estimates for
\left(b_{0}Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0}\right)(x,y)
.

We partition the fundamental solution as

b_{0}G_{2}^{0}(x,y)=\widetilde{G}_{1}(|x-y|)+\widetilde{G}_{2}(|x-y|),

where
\widetilde{G}_{j}(r):=b_{0}\chi_{j}(r)r^{2}\log r
. The compactly supported piece
\widetilde{G}_{1}
is uniformly bounded, trivially contributing
{O}(1)
to the
L^{2}_{x}
norm.

For the non-compact component
\widetilde{G}_{2}
, applying Lemma
10.1
to order
m_{\alpha}+2
invokes the corresponding operator
\mathcal{L}_{m_{\alpha}+2}[\widetilde{G}_{2}]
. For
\alpha\in\{2,3,4\}
(
m_{\alpha}=0
), the second-order derivative produces a logarithmic growth:

\left|\mathcal{L}_{2}[\widetilde{G}_{2}](|\theta x-y|,\xi)\right|\lesssim 1+|\log(|\theta x-y|)|\chi_{2}(|\theta x-y|)\lesssim\log(2+|x|)\log(2+|y|).

Upon evaluating the
L^{2}_{x}
-norm against
v(x)
, the local
\log(2+|x|)
factor is absorbed, resulting in

\|(b_{0}Q_{\alpha}v\widetilde{G}_{2})(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\log(2+|y|),\quad\text{for }\alpha=2,3,4.

Conversely, for the projections
Q_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{5,6\}
(
m_{\alpha}\in\{1,2\}
), the operators
\mathcal{L}_{3}
and
\mathcal{L}_{4}
entail third- and fourth-order derivatives of
r^{2}\log r
. These higher-order derivatives introduce rigorous decay (
O(r^{-1})
or better),
eliminating any logarithmic growth, guaranteeing
|\mathcal{L}_{m_{\alpha}+2}[\widetilde{G}_{2}]|\lesssim 1
. This secures the uniform spatial bound:

\bigl\|(b_{0}Q_{\alpha}vG_{2}^{0})(\cdot,y)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,\quad\text{for }\alpha=5,6.

By identical orthogonality logic,
Q_{4}^{0},Q_{4}^{1},
and
Q_{41}^{1}
emulate the first-order cancellation of
Q_{2}
(yielding a logarithmic bound), while
Q_{42}^{1}
embodies the second-order cancellation of
Q_{5}
(securing a uniform bound).

Step 2: Estimates for
\left[Q_{\alpha}v\left(R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}\right)(\cdot,y)\right](x)
.

We articulate the proof explicitly for
Q_{4}^{0}
; the analogous algebraic structures seamlessly govern the remaining cases. Note that

\displaystyle[R_{0}^{\pm}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}](x,y)=b_{0}(\log\lambda)|x-y|^{2}+\lambda^{-2}F_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda|x-y|),

(10.2)

where

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\pm}(p)

\displaystyle=\left(a_{0}^{\pm}+a_{1}^{\pm}p^{2}+a_{2}^{\pm}p^{4}+O(p^{5})\right)\chi_{1}(p)+\Big(\pm\frac{i}{8}e^{\pm ip}w_{\pm}(p)-\frac{i}{8}e^{-p}w_{+}(ip)-b_{0}p^{2}\log p\Big)\chi_{2}(p).

Subjecting
F_{1}^{\pm}
to
\mathcal{L}_{2}
naturally splits the kernel into two components:

\mathcal{L}_{2}[F_{1}^{\pm}](\lambda|\theta x-y|,\xi)=\Phi^{\pm}(\lambda,\theta,x,y)+\Psi(\lambda,\theta,x,y),

(10.3)

where, the well-behaved profile
\Phi^{\pm}
collects the bounded low-frequency derivatives and the high-frequency oscillatory terms, ensuring it satisfies the bound (
10.1
). In contrast, the remainder
\Psi
specifically isolates the logarithmic singularity emanating from differentiating
-b_{0}p^{2}\log p
for
p\gtrsim 1
:

\displaystyle\Psi(\lambda,\theta,x,y)=\left(-2b_{0}\log(\lambda|\theta x-y|)-b_{0}-2b_{0}\cos^{2}\xi\right)\chi_{2}(\lambda|\theta x-y|).

For
0<\lambda\ll 1
, we explicitly bound
\Psi
by:

\displaystyle|\Psi|\lesssim\big(1+|\log(\lambda|\theta x-y|)|\big)\chi_{2}(\lambda|\theta x-y|)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+|\log(|\theta x-y|)|\big)\chi_{2}(|\theta x-y|)

\displaystyle\lesssim\log(2+|x|)\log(2+|y|).

This rigid algebraic structure dictates that:

|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Psi(\lambda,\theta,x,y)|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\log(2+|x|)\log(2+|y|).

The specific orthogonality of
Q_{4}^{0}
(
Q_{4}^{0}v=Q_{4}^{0}(x_{j}v)=0
) rewrites the quadratic singularity precisely as
Q_{4}^{0}\bigl(v|\cdot-y|^{2}\bigr)(x)=Q_{4}^{0}\bigl(v|\cdot|^{2}\bigr)(x)
. Integrating (
10.2
) and (
10.3
) into Lemma
10.1
(ii) produces:

\displaystyle\bigl[Q_{4}^{0}v\bigl(R_{0}^{\pm}(\lambda^{4})-b_{0}G_{2}^{0}\bigr)\bigr](x,y)

\displaystyle=b_{0}(\log\lambda)\,Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)+Q_{4}^{0}\Big(v|\cdot|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\big(\Phi^{\pm}+\Psi\big)d\theta\Big)(x)

\displaystyle:=b_{0}(\log\lambda)\,Q_{4}^{0}(v|\cdot|^{2})(x)+\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{0}(\lambda,x,y)+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0}(\lambda,x,y).

The spatial weight
\log(2+|x|)
intrinsic to
\Psi
is entirely suppressed by
v(x)
, cementing the bound
\|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{4}^{0}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}\log(2+|y|)
. Concurrently,
\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{0}
governed strictly by
\Phi^{\pm}
, inherits the bound (
2.23
).

The arguments for
Q_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{2,3,4\}
strictly parallel
Q_{4}^{0}
, isolating
b_{0}(\log\lambda)Q_{\alpha}(v|\cdot|^{2})
and establishing identical bounds for
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\pm}
.

For
Q_{4}^{1}
, the augmented orthogonality
Q_{4}^{1}(v|\cdot|^{2})=0
(Remark
2.4
) enforces complete annihilation of the logarithmic divergence
b_{0}(\log\lambda)|x-y|^{2}
, directly yielding
\bigl[Q_{4}^{1}v(R_{0}^{\pm}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0})\bigr]=\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1}
, with matching bounds. The mechanism for
Q_{41}^{1}
is identical.

Lastly, for
\alpha\in\{5,6\}
, the second-order cancellation
Q_{\alpha}(x_{i}x_{j}v)=0
permits

\bigl[Q_{\alpha}v(R_{0}^{\pm}-b_{0}G_{2}^{0})\bigr](x,y)=\lambda^{-2}[Q_{\alpha}vF_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda|\cdot-y|)(y)],

where
F_{2}^{\pm}(p)=F_{1}^{\pm}(p)-a_{1}^{\pm}p^{2}
.
The corresponding higher-order operators
\mathcal{L}_{m_{\alpha}+2}
entirely differentiate away the underlying logarithmic factor
\log(\lambda|\theta x-y|)
, driving
\Psi_{\alpha}
(the analogue
\Psi
) to absolute uniform boundedness:
|\partial_{\lambda}^{\ell}\Psi_{\alpha}|\lesssim\lambda^{-\ell}
. This superior decay cascades directly to
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
and identically to
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{1,2}
(via
Q_{42}^{1}
), definitively verifying (
2.25
). Concurrently,
\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}
(the analogue
\Phi
) obey the bound (
10.1
), which independently ensures that
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\pm}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4,\pm}^{1,2}
satisfy the estimate (
2.23
).
∎

10.2.
Oscillatory integral estimates

In this subsection we establish the estimates for the oscillatory integrals that arise in this paper. We begin by proving Lemma
2.9
.

Proof.

Assume
t>0
without loss of generality and abbreviate
\mathcal{E}(\lambda):=\mathcal{E}^{\pm}(\lambda,x,y)
and
h:=h(x,y)
.

Step 1: Reduction to a Model Oscillatory Integral.

Using Euler’s identities, both
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\pm}
can be expressed as linear combinations of integrals of the form

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi(\lambda)}\lambda\mathcal{A}(\lambda)\,d\lambda,

(10.4)

where the generalized phase
\Phi(\lambda):=\alpha t\lambda^{2}+\beta\lambda r
for
\alpha,\beta\in\{+1,-1\}
. For
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}
, the effective amplitude factor is
\mathcal{A}(\lambda)=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)
. For
\mathcal{N}^{\pm}
, the corresponding amplitude factor is
\mathcal{A}(\lambda)=t^{-1}\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{E}(\lambda)
.
We claim that estimating
\mathcal{N}^{\pm}
reduces entirely to the bounds for
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}
. To see this, we partition the frequency domain using
1=\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})+\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})
.

In the low-frequency regime (
t\lambda^{2}\leq 2\lambda_{0}\ll 1
), the inequality
|\sin(t\lambda^{2})|\leq t\lambda^{2}
ensures the integrand of
\mathcal{N}^{\pm}
is bounded by
\lambda|\mathcal{E}(\lambda)|
, precisely mirroring
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}
. In the high-frequency regime (
t\lambda^{2}\gtrsim 1
), the factor
t^{-1}\lambda^{-2}\lesssim 1
. Thus, the modified amplitude factor
t^{-1}\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{E}(\lambda)
satisfies the exact same decay assumptions (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) as
\mathcal{E}(\lambda)
.
Consequently, we restrict our subsequent analysis solely to the model integral (
10.4
) with
\mathcal{A}(\lambda)=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)
.

The phase derivatives are
\Phi^{\prime}(\lambda)=2\alpha t\lambda+\beta r
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)=2\alpha t
. If
\alpha\beta=-1
, a stationary point exists at
\rho=r/(2t)
. We decompose the high-frequency regime (
\lambda\geq t^{-1/2}\lambda_{0}^{1/2}
) into a stationary region
\mathcal{I}_{stat}=\{\lambda:|\lambda-\rho|\leq\lambda/2\}
and a non-stationary region
\mathcal{I}_{non}=\{\lambda:|\lambda-\rho|>\lambda/2\text{ or }\alpha\beta=1\}
. Note that
\frac{2}{3}\rho\leq\lambda\leq 2\rho
(i.e.,
\lambda\sim\rho
) in
\mathcal{I}_{stat}
, whereas
|\Phi^{\prime}(\lambda)|\gtrsim t\lambda
in
\mathcal{I}_{non}
.

Step 2: Proof of Part (i).

For the low-frequency part, assumption (
2.26
) yields directly

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\Big|\lesssim\int_{0}^{t^{-1/2}}\lambda|\mathcal{E}(\lambda)|\,d\lambda\lesssim h\int_{0}^{t^{-1/2}}\lambda\,d\lambda\lesssim ht^{-1}.

(10.5)

For the high-frequency part over
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{stat}}
, we apply the Van der Corput lemma (see, e.g.,
[
47
, P. 334]
). Since
|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)|\sim t
and
\lambda\sim\rho=r/(2t)
, setting
\psi(\lambda):=\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda),
it follows that

\begin{split}\left|\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{stat}}}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\right|&\lesssim t^{-1/2}\left(\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{stat}})}+\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{stat}}}|\partial_{\lambda}\psi(\lambda)|\,d\lambda\right)\\
&\lesssim t^{-1/2}\sup_{\lambda\sim\rho}\Big(\lambda|\mathcal{E}(\lambda)|+\rho|\mathcal{E}(\lambda)|+\rho\lambda|\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{E}(\lambda)|\Big)\\
&\lesssim t^{-1/2}(\rho h\langle\rho r\rangle^{-1/2})\lesssim ht^{-1/2}\frac{r}{2t}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{2t}\right)^{-1/2}\lesssim ht^{-1}.\end{split}

(10.6)

In
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}
, performing two successive integrations by parts yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=\left[e^{i\Phi}\frac{\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\right]_{\partial\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}-\left[e^{i\Phi}\frac{1}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\partial_{\lambda}\left(\frac{\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\right)\right]_{\partial\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}e^{i\Phi}\partial_{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\partial_{\lambda}\left(\frac{\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\right)\right)d\lambda.

(10.7)

Using assumption (
2.26
) together with the bounds
|\Phi^{\prime}|\gtrsim t\lambda
and
|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|\sim t
, both boundary terms are bounded by
O(ht^{-1})
, while the integrand is bounded by
O(ht^{-2}\lambda^{-3})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\right|\lesssim ht^{-1}+ht^{-2}\int_{\lambda_{0}^{1/2}t^{-1/2}}^{\infty}\lambda^{-3}\,d\lambda\lesssim ht^{-1}.

(10.8)

Combining (
10.5
), (
10.6
), and (
10.8
) establishes Part (i).

Step 3: Proof of Part (ii).
For
t\leq 2
, the oscillatory nature is not required. The support of
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}
and assumption (
2.27
) guarantee that the integral is bounded by
h\int_{0}^{1/4}\lambda^{1-\sigma}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}d\lambda
. This converges to
O(h)
under the conditions
\sigma<2,\nu\in\mathbb{R}
or
\sigma=2,\nu>1
.

For
t>2
, the low-frequency component extracts the precise logarithmic decay via the upper incomplete Gamma function
\Gamma(a,x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}u^{a-1}e^{-u}du
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t^{-1/2}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\sigma}|\log\lambda|^{\nu}}d\lambda=\int_{\frac{1}{2}\log t}^{\infty}\frac{u^{-\nu}}{e^{u(2-\sigma)}}du=\begin{cases}\frac{2^{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)(\log t)^{\nu-1}},&\sigma=2,\nu>1,\\[4.0pt]
(2-\sigma)^{\nu-1}\Gamma(1-\nu,\frac{2-\sigma}{2}\log t),&\sigma<2,\nu\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

Using the standard asymptotic expansion
\Gamma(a,x)\sim x^{a-1}e^{-x}
as
x\to\infty
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)d\lambda\Big|\lesssim\begin{cases}h(\log t)^{-\nu+1},&\sigma=2,\nu>1,\\[4.0pt]
ht^{-1+\sigma/2}(\log t)^{-\nu},&\sigma<2,\nu\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

(10.9)

For the high-frequency part, note that under the given parameter conditions (
0<\sigma<2
,
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
; or
\sigma=0
,
\nu\leq 0
; or
\sigma=2
,
\nu>1
),
the map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{-\sigma}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}
is decreasing for
\lambda\ll 1
. Thus,

\displaystyle\lambda^{-\sigma}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}\lesssim t^{\sigma/2}(\log t)^{-\nu},\quad\text{on the support interval}\ \lambda\in[\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}},2(2\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{4}}].

(10.10)

Applying the Van der Corput lemma in
\mathcal{I}_{stat}
exactly as in (
10.6
), by (
10.10
), the bound becomes:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{I}_{stat}}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)d\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim ht^{-1/2}\langle\rho r\rangle^{-1/2}\rho\cdot t^{\sigma/2}(\log t)^{-\nu}\lesssim ht^{-1+\sigma/2}(\log t)^{-\nu}.

Similarly, integrating by parts twice over
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}
yields the representation (
10.2
). Using assumption (
2.27
) together with the bounds
|\Phi^{\prime}|\gtrsim t\lambda
,
|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|\sim t
, and (
10.10
), we deduce that the boundary terms are bounded by
O(t^{-1+\sigma/2}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}h)
, and the integrand of the remainder integral is bounded by
O(t^{-2}\lambda^{-\sigma-3}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}h)
. Consequently,

\displaystyle h\int_{\lambda_{0}^{1/2}t^{-1/2}}^{2(2\lambda_{0})^{1/4}}t^{-2}\lambda^{-\sigma-3}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}\,d\lambda\lesssim ht^{\frac{\sigma}{2}-2}(\log t)^{-\nu}\int_{t^{-1/2}}^{\infty}\lambda^{-3}\,d\lambda\lesssim\frac{h}{t^{1-\sigma/2}(\log t)^{\nu}}.

Hence we obtain the desired estimates over
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}
. Combining these with the estimates over
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{stat}}
, we deduce the following bound in the high-frequency regime:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\Big|\lesssim\frac{h}{t^{1-\sigma/2}(\log t)^{\nu}}.

(10.11)

Summing the low- and high-frequency contributions yields the desired bound.

Finally, we emphasize that in the case
0<\sigma<2
,
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, or
\sigma=2
,
\nu>1
, a single integration by parts (requiring only derivative indices
\ell=0,1
) is strictly sufficient for the estimate over
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}
. Indeed, after one integration by parts, the remainder integral directly satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{non}}}e^{i\Phi}\partial_{\lambda}\left(\frac{\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}{i\Phi^{\prime}}\right)d\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim h\int_{\lambda_{0}^{1/2}t^{-1/2}}^{2(2\lambda_{0})^{1/4}}t^{-1}\lambda^{-\sigma-1}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle\lesssim ht^{-1}t^{\sigma/4}(\log t)^{-\nu}\int_{t^{-1/2}}^{\infty}\lambda^{-1-\sigma/2}\,d\lambda\lesssim\frac{h}{t^{1-\sigma/2}(\log t)^{\nu}},

where we used the fact that the map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{-\sigma/2}|\log\lambda|^{-\nu}
is decreasing for
\lambda\ll 1
. ∎

Remark 10.2
.

Based on the proof of Lemma
2.9
, we make the following two observations.

(i)

In the proof of Lemma
2.9
, we essentially establish that under assumption (
2.26
),

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi(\lambda)}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda\lesssim h(x,y)|t|^{-1},

while under assumption (
2.27
), we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi(\lambda)}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,y)\,d\lambda\lesssim\begin{cases}\dfrac{h(x,y)}{\langle t\rangle^{1-\frac{\sigma}{2}}\bigl(\log(2+|t|)\bigr)^{\nu}},&0<\sigma<2,\ \nu\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{or}\ \sigma=0,\ \nu\leq 0,\\[8.0pt]
h(x,y)\bigl(\log(2+|t|)\bigr)^{-\nu+1},&\sigma=2,\ \nu>1,\end{cases}

where
\Phi(\lambda)=\alpha t\lambda^{2}+\beta\lambda r
with
\alpha,\beta\in\{+1,-1\}.

(ii)

By estimates (
10.9
) and (
10.11
), in the case where
\sigma=2
and
\nu>1
, we obtain

\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{1}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\Big|\lesssim\frac{h(x,y)}{(\log t)^{\nu-1}},\qquad\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\Phi}\chi_{2}(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\,d\lambda\Big|\lesssim\frac{h(x,y)}{(\log t)^{\nu}}.

The high-frequency decay
O(h(x,y)(\log t)^{-\nu})
is strictly faster (by one full logarithmic power) than the low-frequency integral
O(h(x,y)(\log t)^{-(\nu-1)})
.

Next, we evaluate the following oscillatory integrals, which are important in the resonance analysis, especially in deriving sharp bounds when zero is a resonance of the second kind of
H
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For
|t|\geq 2
, the following asymptotic expansions hold:

\displaystyle I(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t\lambda^{2})\lambda\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(\log\lambda)^{2}\,d\lambda=\frac{\pi}{8}\frac{\log|t|}{|t|}+O\big(|t|^{-1}\big),

(10.12)

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle:=t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\lambda)(\log\lambda)^{2}\,d\lambda=\frac{\pi}{16}\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{|t|}+O\Big(\frac{\log|t|}{|t|}\Big).

(10.13)

Proof.

We first consider
I(t)
. The change of variables
u=|t|\lambda^{2}
yields

I(t)=\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{8|t|}I_{0}(t)-\frac{\log|t|}{4|t|}I_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{8|t|}I_{2}(t),

(10.14)

where
I_{j}(t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos u\,\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})(\log u)^{j}\,du
. Integration by parts via
\cos u=\frac{d}{du}\sin u
gives

I_{j}(t)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin u\,\frac{d}{du}\Bigl[\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})(\log u)^{j}\Bigr]du.

By the Leibniz rule, the contribution where the derivative acts on the cutoff function
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}
is supported entirely in the regime
u\sim|t|
. Since
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}
is smooth, repeated integration by parts shows that this boundary contribution decays rapidly, bounded by
O(|t|^{-N})
for any integer
N\geq 1
.
Consequently, for
j=0
, we immediately obtain
I_{0}(t)=O(|t|^{-N})
.

For
j=1
, the derivative acting on
\log u
yields the principal integral:

I_{1}(t)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})\,du+O(|t|^{-N})=-\frac{\pi}{2}+R_{1}(t)+O(|t|^{-N}),

where the remainder is defined as
R_{1}(t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\bigl(1-\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})\bigr)du
. A further integration by parts on
R_{1}(t)
yields:

R_{1}(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos u\,\frac{d}{du}\Big[\frac{1-\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})}{u}\Big]du.

The derivative inside the integral is bounded by
O(u^{-2})
, and the integrand is supported on
u\gtrsim|t|
. This directly evaluates to
R_{1}(t)=O(|t|^{-1})
. Hence,
I_{1}(t)=-\frac{\pi}{2}+O(|t|^{-1})
.

For
j=2
, observing that the untruncated integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\log u\,du
converges to a constant, an analogous integration by parts for the remainder confirms that
I_{2}(t)=O(1)
.

Substituting the respective bounds
I_{0}(t)=O(|t|^{-N})
,
I_{1}(t)=-\frac{\pi}{2}+O(|t|^{-1})
, and
I_{2}(t)=O(1)
into (
10.14
) establishes (
10.12
).

For
J(t)
, noting that
t^{-1}\sin(t\lambda^{2})\equiv|t|^{-1}\sin(|t|\lambda^{2})
, the identical change of variables leads to:

J(t)=\frac{(\log|t|)^{2}}{8|t|}J_{0}(t)-\frac{\log|t|}{4|t|}J_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{8|t|}J_{2}(t),

(10.15)

where
J_{j}(t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(\sqrt{u/|t|})(\log u)^{j}\,du
.
By analogous reasoning,
J_{0}(t)
is the sole term providing the leading-order behavior, yielding
J_{0}(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin u}{u}\,du-R_{1}(t)=\frac{\pi}{2}+O(|t|^{-1})
. For
j=1,2
, the untruncated integrals evaluate to convergent constants, ensuring
J_{j}(t)=O(1)
. Collecting these results into (
10.15
) immediately yields (
10.13
), which completes the proof.
∎
